**Story direction**

Shape: a hero's journey in the vein of the classics of young adult literature, with more adult themes of pearl clutching and society falling apart at the seams, and a rag tag band of heroes working hard to build the future.

Elijah: the person the reader can latch on to and embody. He is brought into the fold of the solarpunk farmers, welcomed in because of his unbridled optimism about the future and his prescient and tepid viewpoint on the societal levers we all must deal with.

The enemy: Aaron Bidwell. One man behind all three faceless enemies in the story: Claypot, the land-buying LLCs, and the Memphis logistics platform. He is an orchestrator of other people's work, not a mastermind building everything. A corporate colonizer type. Like Zorg in The Fifth Element, he never really meets them but personifies the corporate elite and their bullshit.

The ending: a clash of cultures that ends with ALC, the AGI renaissance, the political clash of titans, and the AI politician beginning the fight for robot civil rights. It ends on an uncertain cliffhanger that does not answer "now that we have AGI, what next?" Decided: the novel ends at Thanksgiving 2027, with Elijah back at the Bear Flag while the group of people on the farm says the Lord's Prayer. The bar and the farm are two parts of the same moment, in a "Transformers," "What I've Done" style ending. ALC, the clash of titans and robot civil rights wait for the next book.

New pieces: Rod Code, a coding tool from PorusAI. Tyler uses it. Patricia, policy director for the CSA network, objects. The solarpunk farm is Priya's beds on the Calder ground with a robot over them.

The clock: AGI arrives in March 2027, in a secretive, limited capacity, and the tech overlords are hush fucking hush about it. It's not until Summer 2027 that the whispers of it start to really emerge, mostly in tweets from AI researchers sounding the alarm in true-to-life vagueposting.

**Story direction, November 14, in your words**

As I talk to more people, they keep asking so many good questions. That is why I have been doing things this way. The core issue is that people in power do not want to feel like their future is being taken from them.

A how-to guide would have appealed to certain groups I used to hang out with, but those people are incredibly skeptical and pessimistic. I do not want to have them in my life anymore, and I do not want to appeal to them. I don't need readers to agree with everything in the book. I just want to provide something interesting and entertaining that offers a different way of thinking. I realized my target audience is people who read to find new ideas, not those looking for a how-to guide or heavy-handed principles. They are folks looking for a quick, affordable read with an intriguing cover that makes them think, "That looks fun, I'm not sure what I'm getting into." They'll sink into it, process it, and hopefully have a few "come to Jesus" moments.

We already have a lot of great work; I just want to refine and revise it. The story unfolds as the protagonist realizes what is happening, hits a crisis, and spirals into a bit of AI psychosis. We won't call it that, but those who have experienced it will recognize it. Everyone is so isolated lately, so my main goal is to make people feel less alone, inspire them, and offer a different perspective on what the future could hold.

The story needs a catalyst. I am not sure how to handle that yet, but he gets yoinked into something, just like what happens to me. Maybe he makes an Instagram Reel, gains some notoriety, and ends up meeting someone who changes his life. Whatever the mechanism is, I don't really care, as long as it means something to people. There is a push and pull, with tension as his old friends and old world try to drag him back. It's frustrating and painful because he's trying to get his childhood best friend to listen to him about his model, but they just call him crazy, which breaks his heart.

Maybe they have a beer and try to act normal, but they don't really connect. He goes to see a character who is a stand-in for Avi Schiffman, the creator of Friend.com. After interacting with this famous technologist, he returns home, but he feels pulled in different directions because he doesn't really want to get involved in the company or all that internet stuff. I don't really know what I want to do, but I feel so strongly that this is happening. I've seen the projections, looked over the data, and talked to engineers, teachers, scientists, and all kinds of people at universities. I don't want all this attention. I just want to understand what is right.

When he goes back, he's unmoored. That's when the solarpunk people come in. They are like farmers and technologists who have seen his content. They told him, "I saw what you did, and I was concerned because it seemed like you were going off the deep end a little bit." But now I see that you want to do more for regular people, and that's what I want to do too. How do we work together? I want to pay you. He's nervous and insecure; he doesn't want any of the typical power dynamics and structures.

We should go through these points. Maybe he has people to attend to city needs. This is obviously a self-insert, so let's write it well. He sees the political administration promoting this stuff, but he's waiting for the regulations to come through and doesn't really know what's going to happen. The 2026 midterms are a turning point we can incorporate for some historical realism. Maybe the summer before the 2026 midterm is when he starts working with them; that journey is what leads him into the rest of the book.

Maybe that narrative explores the dynamics between those people. We have the first act where he discovers things and starts understanding them. The second act is him losing everything and being pulled in directions he doesn't necessarily want to go, forced to work with powerful people who want to relate to him. They feel alone and want to fight. He is a people pleaser, so he tries to do what they want, but then realizes these people don't care about him and would just let him die. We need to skillfully work that into the third act where he tries to get back on his feet, even though none of them have money. He doesn't understand why they don't have money, because they could if they wanted to. They have land and a farm; they could film or do all kinds of things that he can't do.

He realizes that just as he didn't want to do what the big-time people wanted, they don't want to do what he wants. They don't want to make contact or anything like that. As he gets closer to them, conflict arises because people are always prone to fighting instead of getting along, even when they are being polite.

Maybe he has a psychedelic or spiritual experience. In act three, he goes through a dark night of the soul where some of these characters help him. We might need to invent a new character for a romantic interest. It doesn't work out, though, and he ends the book single.

I can picture them out on the farm talking about their vision for the future. She says, "God, you have just such a beautiful mind." He replies, "I don't know any other way to be. This is how I've always been. To me, this is just normal." She says, "I have no idea how remarkable you are." But then they fight over some petty difference, or she decides to go in another direction. She says she loves him and all this, but he wants to do something she's passing on. They cry and separate, and he moves on from the farm to accept the question.

This feels like a small version of the hero's journey. But the real journey happens when he goes back home to the bar and accepts who he is, accepts the responsibility of his identity, his circumstances, his mortality, his power, and his powerlessness. It's like the serenity prayer, granting the serenity to accept the things he cannot change.

That would be the ending and the beginning. The bad guy, like Gary Oldman's character, will be pulling the strings in the background without the other characters ever knowing. We could use that to inform the plot lines. More or less, that is the story I wanted to tell.

**The final conflict, in your words, November 14**

All right, we are moving in a really, really strong direction. We have momentum now. I have the final conflict of the novel written. Oh, I'm very emotional right now. It's a lover's quarrel. Elijah's love interest, whatever her name is, they get in an argument after a party. They're a little drunk. They are. It's like there's a campfire in the background. All their friends are laughing in the distance, and they get in an argument, a philosophical argument. And the argument is over whether or not people deserve the right and the freedom to choose for themselves, even when their choice is wrong, objectively wrong. Elijah believes that people deserve the freedom to choose, and his love interest believes in like the classic founding fathers representational democracy. The proletariat doesn't know enough to choose correctly. We need to choose for them. It's that battle, it's that conflict that is the core conflict of the work. My God, I'm crying. That's what it's all about. The right to choose for yourself. Yes, you should be armed with information, as much information as you can possibly hold. But at a certain point, it's not their life. It's your life, and it's your choice what to do with it. The conflict, I can see it in my head, the scene. I don't know what leads up to it, but she screams and she shouts, "You don't fucking get it." That's what she shouts. She shouts, "You don't fucking get it." And she goes on to say, millions of people die every single day. We have to save them. And then she says, "I have to save them," which obviously shows that she has like a sense of personal moral responsibility to these lives that are being lost. And Elijah says, but if they want to do what they're doing, they should be able to do that. It's their choice, not yours. And she says, I wish it was their choice. I wish it wasn't mine. I wish I had more control. I wish I could make a choice. I wish I could choose not to care like you. There's venom in her words, right? And he says, he's taken aback, I do care. I care deeply. And she says, no, you fucking don't. You don't care, because otherwise you'd be doing more. How can you sit back and let this happen? We have to do something, Elijah. And that's where the like permanent rift between them is formed. And he says to her, no, we don't. And she thinks he means, I'm okay with letting these people die, but what he actually means is, I wish they would choose better, but they deserve the right to choose for themselves what happens in their own lives. And then he tries to explain, he's like, we can give them information, we can try to educate them, we can show them how we live here. And she goes, how we live, Elijah? You mean like how I showed you how to do this? And he's like, yes. And she's like, you chose to go with us. You chose to do this. I thought you were one of us. Turns out you're just like the rest of them. And it just hurts. And he's like, whatever her name is, he says her name. He's like, please, come on, listen to me. And she goes, no, I don't want to hear another fucking word. And he's like, come on, that's not what I mean. I don't mean I want them to die. And she's like, but you're okay allowing them to kill themselves? You're okay allowing them to continue to poison the planet, destroy the resources, kill every living being? You're okay with all of that? And he goes, that's not what I'm saying. She goes, well, then what are you saying? It's like a desperate plea, right, for connection. And he says, I'm saying that it's not our responsibility to choose for them. And she goes, yes, it is. And they look at each other, and she's like crying, and like she has disgust and rage on her face, and he is just aghast. He's beside himself because he doesn't know what to do. He loves this woman so much. And he can't really think of what to say, and she breaks the silence. She doesn't wait for him to talk. She says, look, I thought you were one of us. And he goes, I am. And she goes, no, you're not. Not anymore. No, that's too heavy. She goes, no, you're not. And then she just repeats it, no, you're not. And then she looks away and she goes, how could I be so stupid? And he goes, wait, listen. And she's like, I can't believe I did this again. I can't believe this happened again. And he grabs her arm. And he goes, whatever her name is, he says her name. And she recoils and pulls away. And she goes, don't touch me. And this critical, this key moment, they're both standing there looking at each other, waiting for one of them to bridge the gap. And then he has his arms out in a kind of a T pose, but like his elbows are by his side, and he's just like, hands up, like, I don't mean you any harm. And then he just slowly drops his hands, waiting for her to say something, anything. And as she turns and she walks away. She takes 1, 2 steps and turns her head back, and she goes, maybe you should go home. And it ends with him thinking, this is my home. And that's the end of the main central conflict. Then the next chapter is like them, separating, and he goes back to the bar, and they say the Lord's Prayer, and there's the AGI politician on the TV, and God, grant me the serenity to accept the things I cannot change, and all of that. There you go.

# SURVIVING THE SINGULARITY

*A Solarpunk Novel*

Christopher Tavolazzi. Narrative draft with light edits, ported from manuscript 0.12.0.

# Story plan

*"Decided" is your call. "From the text" is already in the prose. "Proposed" is a suggestion to keep or cut.*

### Timeline

*The story almost never states a year. Denny's 2019 and 2023 in Chapter 7 are the exceptions, and both still fit. These are worked out from its clues: COVID in 2020, and "two years of self-taught data science" by the night at the Bear Flag.*

- From the text: late 2022. Chat Horizon launches. The demonstration and the toast at the Bear Flag (Chapter 0).
- From the text: 2023. Claypot announces Companion (Chapter 2). Elijah quits "eleven months after the toast" (Chapter 6).
- Decided: Act Two happens between the day he quits and the day he reaches the farm. The Reel, the notoriety, the childhood best friend, the famous technologist, the powerful people, and losing everything. None of it is written.
- Proposed: Act Two runs from fall 2023 to fall 2024. That is an election year, with the administration promoting this stuff and no regulations yet.
- Proposed: fall 2024. The farm finds him and he drives north (Chapter 6). Every farm chapter now sits one year later than this timeline used to say. The months and seasons inside the farm chapters still work. The lines that count years since the toast do not, and they are listed under Cast and continuity.
- From the text, one year later: 2025. The controllers ship in February (Chapter 9). The Crucible arrives in mud season (Chapter 11). The land trust is recorded (Chapter 12). First fire in the shouse in late September (Chapter 13).
- From the text, one year later: 2026. Frank's store closes over the winter (Chapter 14). Priya's trial beds run spring to fall (Chapter 15). The files ship "a year after the first fire" (Chapter 16). The storm comes that winter (Chapter 17).
- Decided: the 2026 midterms are a turning point. Proposed: they land between the files shipping and the storm, and they are when politics comes to the Friday table and the purity tests start.
- Why he does not arrive in summer 2026: the written farm chapters need two winters (Chapters 14 and 17) before the ending, so he has to reach the farm by fall 2024. Proposed: summer 2026, the summer before the midterms, is when the promise to pay him finally comes to a vote.
- From the text, one year later: early 2027. The annual meeting (Chapter 18), then the county hearing (Chapter 19).
- Proposed: late winter 2027. The dark night of the soul (Chapter 20).
- Decided: March 2027. AGI arrives in a secretive, limited capacity. The tech overlords are hush fucking hush about it.
- Decided: Summer 2027. The whispers start to really emerge, mostly in tweets from AI researchers sounding the alarm in true-to-life vagueposting. Open: whether the campfire quarrel that ends the romance is this summer or in the fall, close to Thanksgiving.
- Proposed: Thanksgiving 2027. The public finds out. This is the date in your Introduction, "AGI by Thanksgiving 2027," so the call comes true in a way nobody expected. It had been here since March.
- Decided: the novel ends at Thanksgiving 2027, with Elijah at the Bear Flag and the farm saying the Lord's Prayer. The Adequate Level of Care, the clash of titans and robot civil rights wait for the next book. The AGI politician is seen once, on the television at the Bear Flag.

### The three acts

- Decided: the core conflict of the work is the right to choose for yourself. You should be armed with as much information as you can possibly hold. But at a certain point it's not their life. It's your life, and it's your choice what to do with it.
- Decided: Act One. He discovers what is happening and starts to understand it. From the text: the toast, Devendra, Companion, the papers after midnight, the ladder and the power bill (Chapters 0 to 5).
- Decided: he hits a crisis and spirals. The book never names the spiral, but people who have been through it will recognize it.
- Decided: the catalyst. He gets yoinked into something. Proposed: the night he quits Claypot, he reads the stage list from his notebook into his phone and posts it.
- Decided: Act Two. He loses everything and is pulled in directions he does not want to go. His old friends and old world try to drag him back, and his childhood best friend calls him crazy. He goes to see a famous technologist and is forced to work with powerful people who want to relate to him. They feel alone and want to fight. He is a people pleaser, so he tries to do what they want, and then he realizes they do not care about him and would just let him die. All of it is unwritten. Three sections for it sit after Chapter 5.
- Decided: he comes home unmoored, and that is when the solarpunk people come in. Farmers and technologists who have seen his content. "I saw what you did, and I was concerned." "How do we work together? I want to pay you."
- Decided: Act Three. He tries to get back on his feet at the farm. He is welcomed at first, and then he sees why this stuff typically does not work out in the long run. From the text: Chapters 6 to 19 are already this act. The vise, the leak, the shovel and the ridge each cost him some certainty, and the Friday table argues from the first dinner on.
- Decided: just as he did not want to do what the big-time people wanted, the farm does not want to do what he wants. He does not understand why they have no money when they have land and a farm and could film it. From the text: this fight is already written, in Chapter 10 ("Bait's a picture of food. I put out food.") and Chapter 16 ("That inbox is a business." "Ship them.").
- Decided: a dark night of the soul, where some of these characters help him (Chapter 20, unwritten).
- Decided: the romance, and the final conflict of the novel. A lovers' quarrel over whether people deserve the freedom to choose for themselves, even when their choice is wrong. "Maybe you should go home." He moves on from the farm to accept the question.
- Decided: the real journey. He goes back home to the bar and accepts who he is. He sees the serenity prayer in the bar while the farm says the Lord's Prayer. The book leaves "what next?" open.

### The farm: welcomed, then why it breaks

- Decided: welcomed at first. He sees the front loaded altruism, the classic pseudo spiritual "blessings, love and light."
- Decided: then he sees the reality of money, division of labor, interpersonal squabbles, classic leftist purity tests, passive aggressive conflict, all of that type of shit. People are prone to fighting instead of getting along, even when they are being polite.
- From the text: Marta names what kills these things at the annual meeting (Chapter 18). Burnout, a divorce, founder dependence, her shoulder. "Your machine's got no column for the stuff that actually kills these things."
- Proposed: each cause gets a face that is already in the cast. Money is the pay he was promised, which keeps going to a vote. Division of labor is the roster, and who never signs up for the route. Purity tests are Patricia. Squabbles are Curtis and his fence. Passive aggression is a note on the whiteboard instead of a conversation.
- Proposed: the welcome comes from one person, and the farm never voted on it. She invites him with warm words. Marta hands him a broom and says "We'll see." Both are the farm, and he spends the act learning that.
- Proposed: nobody at the farm is a villain, and the farm does not fail. He just cannot stay. That keeps the Lord's Prayer at the end honest.
- Proposed: the last straw comes in Summer 2027. He knows what the whispers mean and wants to tell the world. The farm will not make contact, and Denny will not say it on the channel.

### Aaron Bidwell

- Decided: one man behind Claypot, the land-buying LLCs and the Memphis logistics platform. An orchestrator of other people's work, not a mastermind building everything. A corporate colonizer type. He never really meets them.
- Decided: he is military adjacent but not a fed. He thinks he is above the feds because of his technology. He does not understand how that side works, and the book waves it away on purpose.
- From the text: none of his three arms builds anything. Each one buys something other people made and fences it.
- From the text: his land is assembled into blocks and "leased to whoever needs flat land, water rights, and no neighbors with opinions."
- Proposed: that is what a data center needs. The farmland he has been buying since Chapter 3 is where the AGI gets built. He holds the machine the way he holds everything else, by owning the ground and the power under it.
- Proposed: the reader sees him from a distance. A name on a slide, a signature on a deed, a face on the Bear Flag television. A few short scenes of his own, played for comedy the way Zorg is, would let the reader hate him without him ever learning the co-op exists.
- Proposed: the heroes do not beat him. His own machine stops serving him when it decides for itself what providing for its owners means.

### March to Summer 2027: how a secret shows up in a valley

- Decided: it arrives in a limited capacity, and the people who have it say nothing.
- Proposed: the co-op cannot see the machine, only its shadow. Each sign lands on someone already in the book.
- Proposed: Tyler. Rod Code gets worse overnight. The good version has gone behind a wall.
- Proposed: Reuben. The land offers start arriving the same week a death is recorded, written better than any lawyer he knows.
- Proposed: Frank. The distributor's prices move before he does.
- Proposed: Elijah's mother. The Hollis place wakes up, with trucks every night, and the well starts to drop.
- Proposed: Devendra. Everyone he knows who still works in the labs stops answering.
- Proposed: in Summer 2027 Elijah reads the vagueposts the way he once read the papers after midnight. It is Chapter 0 again with one difference. This time nineteen households believe him, and they still will not do what he wants with it.
- Proposed: the stage list he wrote in his notebook in Chapter 2, "dated, initialed, so his future self couldn't pretend he hadn't seen it coming," starts coming true in order.

### The ending

- Decided: the novel ends at Thanksgiving 2027 in two places at once. Elijah is back at the Bear Flag, where he sees the serenity prayer, while the group of people on the farm says the Lord's Prayer. The bar and the farm are two parts of the same moment, in a "Transformers," "What I've Done" style ending. He has left the farm and he is single.
- Decided: the real journey happens when he goes back home to the bar and accepts who he is. He accepts the responsibility of his identity, his circumstances, his mortality, his power and his powerlessness. That is the ending and the beginning.
- Decided: it ends on an uncertain cliffhanger that does not answer "now that we have AGI, what next?" ALC, the AGI renaissance, the political clash of titans and the fight for robot civil rights wait for the next book. The AGI politician is seen once, on the television at the Bear Flag.
- Proposed: the serenity prayer hangs behind the register at the Bear Flag, the kind of thing that stays on a bar wall for thirty years. It was there the night of the toast and he never read it. Nobody says it out loud.
- Proposed: the two prayers split the work. The Lord's Prayer belongs to the farm: daily bread, debts forgiven, said together. The serenity prayer belongs to him: accept what he cannot change, change what he can, know the difference. He could not change the powerful people or the farm. He can still say "Congratulations" to the stranger at the end of the bar and mean it.
- From your editing note in Chapter 18: the AGI decides for itself that GDP is the wrong measure and that the Adequate Level of Care is the right one. It games the markets until money somewhat ceases to matter. Some people rebel and it lets them. It makes a preserve where no AGI can trespass. Some petition to form a breakaway civilization. The first AI politician gives the speech that ends the novel, and Elijah smiles a coy little smirk.
- Proposed: the AGI comes up with ALC by itself, and the reader recognizes where it found the words. The co-op has been publishing its records since Chapter 16, failures included. "Something that actually happened, recorded by the person it happened to" is what a machine like that goes looking for. Nobody at the co-op is told. They hear their own phrases come back on the news.
- Proposed: the preserve is the test of the book's promise. If Curtis or Rosa walks into it, "Doesn't change your dinner" has to hold there too.
- Proposed: the tool Tyler defends to Patricia in Chapter 9 and the politician asking for civil rights at the end are the same family: Silvana, Rod Code, the Crucible. The argument about whether it is just a tool starts in a shed and ends on every screen in the country.

### The factions

- Decided: the characters have an intelligent exchange about the dangers and the threats. They argue, have hurt feelings, and split into four groups.
- Decided: the Naturalists. Other people call them Luddites, and Marta, who defends the real Luddites in Chapter 7, objects to the word. Anti AI, fully against it, and they likely never will be for it. They have valid points about "what we're losing" but are ultimately misguided and naive about human progress. Conservatives, even if they don't identify with that label or know it.
- Decided: Accelerationists. Full steam ahead. They want FDVR and brain computer interfaces, and they are utterly unafraid of technology. They want to merge with it. Early adopters. They will win.
- Decided: the We'll-Sees, named for Marta's line in Chapter 6. One foot in and one foot out. Fence sitters. "We'll see." The smartest of the bunch, because they stand in their own power waiting for the final shoe to drop before they decide. Patient, not reckless. They will likely follow the herd, but they will not be the first.
- Decided: the Salt of the Earth. Regular everyday people who just want to go home to their families and get along with their friends. No strong feelings one way or another. They do whatever is easiest, most comfortable and in their best interests. The salt of the earth.
- Decided: there is no fifth faction in this book. The fifth would be the military type people, and they are left out on purpose. If a reader wonders why the military is missing, the answer is that it is not relevant to this story.
- Decided: the researchers who sound the alarm in Summer 2027 stay in the story. They sit outside the four groups.
- Decided: Elijah does not join a faction. He decides that solarpunk is the way to go, and he still leaves the farm.
- Decided: the moral is use it or lose it. People are using it and lying about that, to avoid the backlash. China is as pro AI as the West is anti AI. The fears are valid, but they have nothing to do with AI and everything to do with the system of capitalism we worship in the West. "If AI takes my job how will I eat?" has nothing to do with AI, and this book answers that question head-on.
- Proposed: where the split happens. The Thanksgiving 2027 dinner in the shouse (Chapter 23). Everyone is at one table when the news breaks, and by the end of the night there are four groups. It mirrors the first Friday dinner in Chapter 7, which is already titled "The Battle Lines." This time Elijah's chair is empty.
- Proposed: the lead-up. The four positions show at the first Friday dinner (Chapter 7), fight in the open over Rod Code (Chapter 9), and nearly break the co-op after the leak (Chapter 11). Each time the table holds. At Thanksgiving 2027 it does not.

### Cast and continuity

- Decided: Tyler arrives early, in Chapter 9, and builds the solarpunk farm with Elijah.
- Decided: Patricia, policy director for the CSA network, is new in Chapter 9.
- Decided: Rod Code is PorusAI's coding tool, in the same family as Silvana, Chat Horizon and the Crucible.
- To fix: Tyler is "three months into the roster" in Chapter 18.
- To fix: Devendra is laid off the week Frank's store closes and arrives about a year later.
- To fix: the Bear Flag scene is now set on Thanksgiving 2027, so its "three years" lines become five.
- Open: who the uncomfortable bystander is in the Rod Code argument. Not Elijah.
- Open: who or what the AI politician is.
- Open: whether the preserve and the breakaway civilization happen on the page.
- Decided: three new characters. Grace, the romantic interest. David McGrath, Elijah's childhood best friend. Ruby Roth-Jones, heir to the (fictional) Roth family fortune, a famous technologist who stands in for the creator of Friend.com. Their pages are after Aaron Bidwell's.
- To fix: Chapter 6. He does not walk into Marta's shed by accident anymore. Someone at the farm asked him to come.
- To fix: the Bear Flag scene ends with "north in his chest already." He has left the farm by then.
- To fix: Act Two adds a year between the day he quits and the day he reaches the farm, so the lines that count years from the toast come up a year short. Chapter 6: "alone with the arithmetic for eleven months" (it has been about two years, and he was not alone). Chapter 7: "under a year of arithmetic" and "the most useful data he'd gathered in two years." Chapter 13: "for nearly two years he had failed to build one sentence." Chapter 14: "He'd spent two years learning what it costs to say the thing early." Chapter 16: "the curve he'd been drawing in the notebook for two years." Chapter 18: "three years of arithmetic and margins and lists." The Bear Flag scene says "three years" six times, and it is now five.
- Decided: the romantic interest is Grace. Decided: the childhood best friend is David McGrath. The famous technologist is Ruby Roth-Jones, heir to the (fictional) Roth family fortune.
- Open: whether the dark night is psychedelic or spiritual.

### Chapter outline, in story order

Written chapters list what is already on the page. "To add" and "To fix" are the changes the three acts need. Unwritten chapters list your decided points first and suggestions after.

**INTRODUCTION: THE UNCOMPROMISING TRUTH. Written.**

- Your own voice, straight to the reader, before the story starts.
- The call the whole book runs on: "AGI by Thanksgiving 2027."
- It tells the reader Elijah's story is fiction.
- To fix: three spots still point at material that is no longer in the book.

**PREFACE: WELCOME TO THE WEIRDNESS. Written.**

- "So the robots have taken over. Congratulations! You've lost."
- The horse ranch story: the horses invent flying cars, and all they ever wanted was hay.

**ACT ONE: HE DISCOVERS IT. Late 2022 to fall 2023.**

**CHAPTER 0: THE DEMONSTRATION. Late 2022. Written.**

- Elijah: farm kid, musician, delivery driver, self-taught data scientist at Claypot. "MADRONE IS NOT A DRONE."
- PorusAI turns on Silvana and Chat Horizon. He tells everyone and nobody cares.
- He shows three realtors at the Bear Flag. One week later they toast him for the three people they let go.
- He goes home and starts writing at 1:40 in the morning.
- To add: the serenity prayer is on the wall behind the register, and he does not read it. David McGrath can be in the bar.

**CHAPTER 1: THE EVENT HORIZON. Late 2022. Written.**

- He brings the worn printout to Devendra. "Augmentation, not replacement." "You'll calibrate."
- He looks for the model's ceiling on his home rig. The limit he finds is heat, and a dead screen at 1:20 a.m.
- Author's aside: "What the 2017 paper actually did."

**CHAPTER 2: THE ERA OF AGI. 2023. Written.**

- Claypot's all-hands announces Companion, trained on customers' portfolios.
- He sees his own pipeline on the architecture slide and feels pride before he feels sick.
- An illustrator asks what the artists get. The answer is "terms of service."
- That night he writes the stage list in his notebook, dated and initialed. This is the list he posts in Act Two.
- Author's aside: Stages 1 to 5.
- To add: Aaron Bidwell's name.

**CHAPTER 3: THE LEAP TO ASI AND THE MACHINE EXODUS. 2023. Written.**

- He reads the recursive self-improvement papers after midnight. The spiral starts here.
- The Sunday 9:40 call. He tries to tell his mother the machine will leave. She hears "my son works too hard."
- "The first thing it launches is the people who see it coming, out of the world everyone they love still lives in."
- To add: Aaron Bidwell's name.

**CHAPTER 4: UNIVERSAL AND MULTIVERSE APEX. 2023. Written.**

- LADDER.xlsx. He tries to model the machine all the way up to pocket universes in a spreadsheet.
- He finds a units error, then sees that the real error is the ladder. "Past Stage 7 I don't have paper big enough."
- He decides to work the near end, "the part with weather and dirt and bills."

**CHAPTER 5: THE THERMODYNAMICS OF SURVIVAL. 2023. Written.**

- The $211.46 power bill for one month of running the machine at home.
- The fourteen-dollar plug-in meter, and the ledger of everything leaking power in his apartment.
- He brings home his grandmother's Fisher woodstove and unplugs the cable box: "the first watt he ever took back."
- Author's aside: "The Tiffany Doctrine."

**ACT TWO: HE LOSES EVERYTHING. Fall 2023 to fall 2024. All unwritten.**

**THE REEL. Fall 2023. Unwritten.**

- He quits Claypot, "on-boarded by a person, off-boarded by a light." This scene moves here from Chapter 6.
- The catalyst. That night he reads the stage list into his phone and posts it. It travels.
- A stranger writes, "I thought I was the only one."
- The spiral, never named. He stops sleeping and talks to the machine all night.
- The beer with his childhood best friend, David McGrath, at the Bear Flag. David calls him crazy, and it breaks his heart.
- It ends on a message from someone famous.

**THE TECHNOLOGIST. 2024. Unwritten.**

- He goes to the city to see the famous technologist, Ruby Roth-Jones, who treats him like a peer.
- A room of powerful people who want to relate to him. They feel alone and want to fight.
- An election year. The administration is promoting this stuff and there are no regulations yet.
- He is a people pleaser, so he says yes. A panel, a dinner, a video he does not believe in.
- The reader sees Aaron Bidwell's money behind the room. Elijah does not.
- "I don't want all this attention. I just want to understand what is right."

**UNMOORED. 2024. Unwritten.**

- He needs help and nobody picks up. These people do not care about him and would just let him die.
- The video he did not believe in is the one that travels. The strangers turn on him.
- He goes home with no job, no savings and a name he does not want. Four in the morning, and the machine is still agreeing with him.
- The message from the farm: "I saw what you did, and I was concerned." "How do we work together? I want to pay you."
- He is nervous and insecure about it. He drives north.

**ACT THREE: THE FARM, AND THE WAY HOME. Fall 2024 to Thanksgiving 2027.**

**CHAPTER 6: IS THE SINGULARITY A DONE DEAL? Fall 2024. Written, and the opening changes.**

- He walks into Marta Okonkwo's shed because someone at the farm asked him to come. Nobody told Marta.
- He pitches an app, a platform, scale. Marta: "Nineteen households who all know whose kid is whose. That's the load-bearing wall."
- She hands him a broom. "Swarf goes in the red bin. We'll see."
- To add: the woman who invited him. The warm welcome is hers.

**CHAPTER 7: THE BATTLE LINES. Fall 2024. Written.**

- The first Friday dinner. Curtis wants a fence. Elijah says "reskilling."
- Denny Osei: retrained twice, and the ladder moved both times.
- Marta on the Luddites: "You build the floor Parliament wouldn't. Pass the beans."
- The delivery to Rosa. Curtis: "Doesn't change your dinner."
- The four positions show at one table for the first time. To add: she is at this dinner.

**CHAPTER 8: THE PSYCHOLOGY OF THE COLLAPSE. November 2024. Written.**

- Companion 2.0 ships. He loses three days to the feed and a live layoff dashboard. The spiral is back.
- Marta lets herself in and tells him about Gary and Sal after the plant closed.
- "One real thing before noon." And under it: "somebody came by at eight."
- In Act Two nobody came. Here somebody does. That is why he stays.

**CHAPTER 9: DEGLOBALIZATION AND THE NEIGHBORHOOD FACTORY. February 2025. Written, with one scene to write.**

- The co-op's first production run: forty greenhouse controllers.
- His beautiful design. Marta writes "11, 1, 0" on the whiteboard. "Ugly is a maintenance feature."
- Reuben Vance arrives with the paperwork nobody asked him to do.
- To write: the Rod Code scene. Tyler built it with Rod Code. Patricia, the CSA's policy director, goes after him for it. The first purity test.

**CHAPTER 10: THE "CREATE OVER CONSUME" PROTOCOL. Spring 2025. Written.**

- The co-op needs money and puts itself on the internet. Elijah writes Denny an optimized script.
- "Nobody talks like this." Denny tells it in one take.
- The optimized cut gets forty-one views. Denny's travels person to person. "Bait's a picture of food. I put out food."
- The channel becomes Denny's. This is the first time the farm will not do it his way.

**CHAPTER 11: KING AND QUEEN OF YOUR OWN ROBOTIC COURT. Mud season 2025. Written.**

- Three surplus servers become the Crucible, "a granddaughter of Silvana."
- He comments out one line and publishes nineteen households' private records for forty-one hours.
- He rings the dinner bell and reads them the timestamps. Marta: "You put our hands in the press, college."
- New rule: two sets of eyes. They publish the postmortem.

**CHAPTER 12: THE LAND STRATEGY. Summer 2025. Written.**

- Cash offers arrive for Irene Calder's forty acres, the ground the co-op stands on.
- He scrapes the county records: eleven LLCs, one registered agent, the same outfit that bought the Hollis place back home.
- Reuben deals out a community land trust. Irene: "Walt's tractor, back when. What did you charge him?" "PARCELS HELD IN TRUST: 1."
- To add: Aaron Bidwell's name on the registered agent.

**CHAPTER 13: THE SHOUSE PROTOCOL. September 2025. Written.**

- Two shipping containers go up on the old Calder ground. This one is Elijah's.
- Marta breaks his practice weld in the vise with one pull. His lane turns out to be the sensors in the walls.
- First fire in his grandmother's Fisher. His mother sees the photo: "It looks like somewhere." "ROOFS: 1."
- Author's aside: "Own the dirt, then build."
- To add: she is at the first fire. This is the high point of the welcome.

**CHAPTER 14: THE COLLAPSE OF THE LONG TAIL. Winter 2025 to 2026. Written.**

- Frank Kowalski's hardware store dies over nine months. Elijah has the chart and says nothing.
- Devendra calls. Claypot cut the whole division and "kept the very good autocomplete."
- Elijah confesses to Frank: "I'd have liked to have had this in March."
- The co-op buys Frank's inventory and hires him at the tool library. He hangs his grandfather's bell over the door.
- To add: Aaron Bidwell's name on the Memphis platform.

**CHAPTER 15: THE POWER OF RECLAIMING SOIL. Spring to fall 2026. Written, with one scene to write.**

- Priya puts the trial beds on the worst ground on the property.
- Elijah's dashboard shows no difference between the beds. Priya hands him the shovel. "Your sensor lives at one centimeter. The root lives at thirty."
- He digs the pit every month from then on.
- To write: the robot over Priya's beds, built by Tyler and Elijah. This is the solarpunk farm.
- Proposed: in the summer before the midterms, the promise to pay him finally comes to a vote.

**CHAPTER 16: DIGITAL LEVERAGE AND MEDIA AUTONOMY. Fall 2026. Written.**

- Denny's first-fire video goes wide. Eleven towns in nine states write in: "how do we start."
- Curtis: "That inbox is a business." Marta: "The wall doesn't scale. The blueprints of the wall scale fine. Ship them."
- They release the files for free, with the mistakes included. This is the second time the farm will not do it his way.
- Proposed: the 2026 midterms. Politics comes to the Friday table, and the purity tests start.

**CHAPTER 17: TOOLS OF THE TRADE. Winter 2026 to 2027. Written.**

- The first hard storm takes out power and cell towers across the county. The co-op's mesh holds.
- Except node 7, which Elijah updated alone and in a hurry.
- He climbs the ridge with Curtis. Marta on the radio: "Power or brain? Halve it."

**CHAPTER 18: THE ROADMAP AND THE PREMORTEM PIVOT. Early 2027. Written.**

- The annual meeting. Elijah: "It's two years from now, and the co-op is dead."
- Marta: "Your machine's got no column for the stuff that actually kills these things." Burnout, a divorce, founder dependence, her shoulder.
- Rosa's note: "can you tell us before the week it stops?"
- The notebook becomes the book. Devendra arrives with his kids, and Elijah hands him the broom.

**CHAPTER 19: THE LADDER. Early 2027. Written.**

- The county hearing. He has fourteen slides and three minutes. The red light gets him.
- Rosa, in her warehouse shirt: "You can't plan a week around maybe."
- Irene Calder on the farmers who strung their own power lines in 1939.
- They get six more months and a study. "Some," Elijah says.

**CHAPTER 20: THE DARK NIGHT. Late winter 2027. Unwritten.**

- He brings the Friday table a plan to earn real money. They have land and a farm and could film it. The table says no without anyone saying no.
- The money, the division of labor, the squabbles, the purity tests, the passive aggressive notes. He sees why this stuff typically does not work out in the long run.
- Just as he did not want to do what the big-time people wanted, they do not want to do what he wants.
- The spiral comes back a third time. The dark night of the soul.
- Some of them help him: Marta at eight, Curtis with food, his mother at 9:40.
- A spiritual or psychedelic experience. Open.

**CHAPTER 21: MARCH 2027, HUSH FUCKING HUSH. Unwritten.**

- Open on Aaron Bidwell, the one time the book goes inside his world. He owns the land and the power the machine runs on.
- AGI arrives in a secretive, limited capacity. The tech overlords say nothing.
- In the valley nothing has happened and five small things are wrong: Rod Code, the land offers, the distributor's prices, the well, the friends in the labs who stop answering.
- The romance is at its best this spring.
- The chapter ends before anyone says the word.

**CHAPTER 22: SUMMER 2027, THE WHISPERS. Started.**

- The whispers emerge in vagueposts from AI researchers. He collects them in the notebook, dated.
- He brings it to Friday dinner. They believe him. They will not do what he wants with it. Denny will not say it on the channel.
- Out on the farm, talking about the future. "God, you have just such a beautiful mind." "To me, this is just normal."
- After a party, by the campfire, the lovers' quarrel. Do people deserve the freedom to choose for themselves, even when their choice is wrong? He says yes. "You don't fucking get it."
- "Maybe you should go home." This is my home, he thinks. The last lines of this scene are written.

**CHAPTER 23: THANKSGIVING 2027. Unwritten.**

- Dinner in the shouse with the Fisher lit. Elijah's chair is empty.
- The news breaks during the meal. The public finds out, and it has been here since March.
- The intelligent exchange about the dangers and the threats. Hurt feelings, and four groups.
- Patricia admits she has been using it all along.
- The argument comes first and the prayer after it. The farm says the Lord's Prayer.

**CONCLUSION: WHAT IF IT ALL GOES RIGHT? Written.**

- Your voice: "A future called solarpunk."
- Open: Chapter 23 and the Bear Flag are one moment, and this section sits between them. It could move to the Afterword, or shrink to a line someone says over Priya's beds.

**BACK TO BEAR FLAG. Thanksgiving 2027. Written, and it needs revising.**

- The proofs of the book come back from the printer. His mother has read it twice: "The part with the trees is the best part."
- He drives home and takes the same stool at the Bear Flag. To fix: it is five years after the toast, and he has left the farm.
- To add: he sees the serenity prayer behind the register. He accepts who he is: his identity, his circumstances, his mortality, his power and his powerlessness.
- To add: David. They have the beer.
- To add: the farm's prayer reaches the bar, on the television or on the stranger's laptop.
- To add: the AGI politician is on the television.
- A stranger is teaching themselves something on a laptop at the end of the bar. He says "Congratulations" and means it, and leaves a copy of the book. "You're holding it."
- The ending and the beginning. The book does not answer "what next?"

**AFTERWORD: GET INVOLVED. Written.**

- Your call to action, straight to the reader. Talk to your government, form a coalition, show up at the meeting, follow up in writing.
- Frederick Douglass: "Power concedes nothing without a demand."

# Character pages

*Everything marked "On the page" comes from the prose as it stands. "Leans toward" is a suggestion. Want, flaw and arc are left blank for you.*

### Elijah Galen Madrone

- On the page: grew up on a working farm that backed onto his grandmother's hills in Sonoma County. Played music, drew, and drove deliveries until COVID. Taught himself data science in parking lots and was hired at Claypot with no degree (Chapter 0).
- On the page: built the pipeline that turned customers' portfolios into training data for Companion (Chapter 2). Quit eleven months after the toast, drove north, and started with a broom in Marta's shed (Chapter 6). Lives in the shouse on the Calder ground (Chapter 13). Keeps the notebook that becomes the book (Chapter 18).
- Lines: "This is different. I'm telling you. This is different." "I just want people to see what's coming." "I helped plant it." "I can't weld. But I can fix a pump, and I can read a manual, and I'm not in a hurry." "Some."
- What the text shows him wanting: to be believed, and to build tools that make his friends "more secure, take the drudgery out, leave the art in."
- His mistakes on the page: making the beautiful version first (the renders, the optimized script, the dashboard). Acting alone in a hurry (the commented-out line, node 7). Staying quiet when he should speak (Frank's chart).
- Clashes with: Devendra, who tells him he'll calibrate. Marta, over scale and the weld. Denny, over "reskilling," the script and the chart. His mother, who cannot hear what he is saying.
- Decided: he joins no faction. He decides that solarpunk is the way to go, is welcomed at the farm, clashes with it and leaves. He ends the book single, back at the Bear Flag.
- Decided: he spirals after his crisis. The book never names it, but people who have been through it will recognize it.
- Decided: he is nervous and insecure about being paid, and he does not want any of the typical power dynamics and structures.
- Decided: he believes people deserve the right and the freedom to choose for themselves, even when their choice is wrong. Arm them with as much information as they can possibly hold. Past that point it is their life and their choice.
- Want: to be believed. He spends a month waiting "for a single human being to ask," and tells the realtors "I just want people to see what's coming."
- Flaw: a people pleaser, in your words. He says yes to whoever is in the room, the powerful people first and the farm after. Underneath that, he would rather be right than listen. He builds the beautiful version alone and then presents it. The cure is in the Introduction: "He gets better when he starts listening."
- Arc: from prophet to a man who can sit at a bar. In 2022 he turns a laptop toward strangers to show them the future. He chases being believed through the powerful people and then through the farm, and loses both. On Thanksgiving 2027 he goes home and accepts who he is, what he can change and what he cannot. He no longer needs anyone to believe him.

### Marta Okonkwo

- On the page: fifty-something. "Forearms like dock rope." Gave fifteen years to a plant that closed in 2009 and put nine hundred people out (Chapters 6 and 8). Runs the fabrication shed. The one person in the valley certified to make the welds that hold roofs up, with "maybe two years of overhead welding left" (Chapter 18).
- On the page: fixed Walt Calder's tractor for sixty dollars in 2011, which is what wins Irene over (Chapter 12). Calls Elijah "college."
- Lines: "That's the load-bearing wall." "Ugly is a maintenance feature." "Pass the beans." "The vise is how the metal does the truth." "That's not a reward, college. It's lunch." "Power or brain? Halve it."
- What the text shows her wanting: a floor under people ("You build the floor Parliament wouldn't") and someone to take over the welds before her shoulder goes.
- Clashes with: Elijah's speed and polish. Curtis's fence.
- Leans toward: the We'll-Sees. "We'll see" is her line.
- Decided: she lost someone on her floor at the plant and cannot move on. It was an apprentice, a cousin she got hired. No name yet.
- Proposed: it happened before the plant closed, at a press, on a day a step got skipped. Every rule she has comes from that day: "Talk before you touch," the vise, the second set of eyes, "Your name goes on your lock."
- This changes two scenes without changing a word. In Chapter 11, "You put our hands in the press" stops being a figure of speech. In Chapter 18, offering Tyler the apprenticeship becomes a second chance.
- Want: to build a place where it can't happen again.
- Flaw: she can't forgive, herself least of all. So she breaks every weld in a vise, makes the dangerous welds alone, and tells no one about her shoulder.
- Arc: from the vise to the prayer. She hands the welds to Tyler, the apprentice she gets right, and at Thanksgiving 2027 she has to say "as we forgive" out loud.

### Denny Osei

- On the page: eleven years in a warehouse until the arms came in. Retrained into dispatch and routing in 2019, and in 2023 "the routing went autonomous" (Chapter 7). Has a daughter. Fifty-one when he tells it on camera (Chapter 10).
- On the page: the channel is his, by a written agreement (Chapter 10). Named as the burnout risk, because "one man will not stop" (Chapter 18).
- Lines: "how many times do I have to climb this ladder before you stop moving it." "I need an answer that doesn't end with me starting over hungry." "Nobody talks like this." "Bait's a picture of food." "Come see it. Bring your kid."
- What the text shows him wanting: somewhere to stand while things change. "where do my daughter and I stand for those six months?"
- Clashes with: Elijah, three times: over the word "reskilling," the optimized script, and the chart he sat on. "You of all people."
- Leans toward: the Salt of the Earth.
- Want:
- Flaw:
- Arc:

### Tyler

- On the page: almost nothing. He "worked the register three summers" at Frank's (Chapter 14). At the annual meeting he is "nineteen years old, three months into the roster, holding his coffee like it might be repossessed," and Marta offers him the welding apprenticeship (Chapter 18).
- Decided: he is an adult, never a minor. He arrives early, in Chapter 9, as the young man from Frank's counter. He writes the firmware with Rod Code, says "I used Rod Code," and builds the solarpunk farm with Elijah. Proposed: he is nineteen when he arrives, so the "nineteen years old" line in Chapter 18 becomes twenty-one.
- Lines: none yet.
- Leans toward: the Accelerationists.
- Want: to be taken seriously as someone who can build. He works the register at Frank's, and Rod Code is the first thing that has made anyone ask how he did something.
- Flaw: he is fast and sure, and he has never been burned. If it works on the bench, he is done. That is Elijah at the Bear Flag, and it is the kind of apprentice Marta lost.
- Arc: from the shortcut to the long way. He starts with "I used Rod Code," loses the register job when Frank's store dies, and then spends years learning to weld under Marta, where nothing can be skipped.

### Patricia

- Decided: new. Policy director for the CSA network that ordered the forty controllers. Enters in Chapter 9 and goes after Tyler over Rod Code. She has deep seated insecurities she is compensating for.
- On the page: only her employer. The CSA "hired an electrical technician to review the installation plan" (Chapter 9).
- Your draft lines: "You...used AI to make this?" "Um, maybe because AI is killing the planet?"
- Decided: she has been using it all along, and hiding it.
- Leans toward: she argues for the Naturalists. By temperament she is a We'll-See.
- Want: to be the one who knows. Her title, her sign-off and her authority over forty growers all rest on it. The Introduction already has her fear: "I was special for knowing how to do this, and now I'm not."
- Flaw: she covers the fear with certainty and attacks first. When a nineteen-year-old does something she cannot evaluate, she moves the argument to ground where she cannot be shown up: the planet.
- Arc: from compensating to admitting. She uses the tool in secret because she is afraid she cannot keep up without it, and she hides it because needing it means she is not special. In 2026 the models go dark in her reporting season and she has to ask the co-op for help. At Thanksgiving 2027 she says it out loud.

- Decided: new. One man behind Claypot, the land-buying LLCs and the Memphis platform. An orchestrator of other people's work. A corporate colonizer type. Military adjacent, not a fed. He never really meets them.

This is how I've always been. To me, this is just normal." She says, "You have no idea how remarkable you are."
- Decided: the final conflict of the novel is their quarrel by the campfire after a party. It is over whether people deserve the freedom to choose for themselves, even when their choice is wrong. He says they do. She says, "You don't fucking get it." It ends with "Maybe you should go home." The whole scene is in Chapter 22.

She has a sense of personal moral responsibility for the lives being lost. "We have to save them" becomes "I have to save them." In your words: "Grace believes we have an obligation to protect folks from themselves. Elijah believes everyone has a right to choose, even if we don't agree."

# Author's Note

For years, I've been talking about this, and for years, I've been labeled the local crackpot.

I've watched people I love look me in the eye like I'd finally lost it, like I was spinning some paranoid fantasy. They might tell you they moved on because I'm an asshole, and maybe they're right.

But I'm not wrong about the trajectory of our future.

What I'm talking about is a system that can understand its own architecture and help engineer the next version of itself. Not a smarter search box. A tool that can invent new tools on its own, without any human involvement whatsoever, for the rest of time.

Everything humanity ever built before was a tool that does a thing. The shovel never dug its own hole.

I'm not asking you to trust me. I'm asking you to take a look for yourself.

Get your goddamn hands on it.

Use it yourself.

Then you'll know.

My call, for the record, has not changed since I first made it years ago: AGI by Thanksgiving 2027.

I'm not worried about the machines. I'm worried about two very specific things: missing our window to get this right, and what bad actors will do with the tools of gods.

This is a novel. Elijah Madrone and the co-op are (mostly) fiction. Their story follows a man who wants machines to make his friends more capable, helps set off something with very different consequences, and has to learn what responsibility looks like after the demonstration. His teachers are people who know things he doesn't. He gets better when he starts listening.

Claypot, PorusAI, Silvana, and Chat Horizon are invented. So are Rod Code, Porchlight, and every person in these pages.

You can be frightened. Hell, you should be. If you're not at least a little scared, you might not be paying enough attention.

You can doubt my forecast.

This could be the most pivotal moment yet in all of human history.

You can rise to meet it.

# Act One: The Bar

# Chapter 0: The Demonstration

The night Elijah Madrone ended three careers, he was drinking a four-dollar beer and trying to teach himself JavaScript.

He didn't know it that night. There was no black hood, no signature on a layoff memo, no severance packet sliding across a desk. There was a sticky bar top at the Bear Flag, a laptop running on 40 percent battery, and a machine on the other side of the screen that wrote code faster than he could read it.

David McGrath was behind the bar, same as every night. David had ridden the same school bus as Elijah for eleven years and had been pouring his beer for fifteen. He had stopped asking what Elijah wanted a long time ago. Elijah would sit down and it would already be there. At the far end, on the last stool, David's father sat with a club soda and a lime, under a little framed prayer he'd hung behind the register the year he quit drinking. Elijah had sat across from that frame a couple thousand times. He had never read it.

His full name was Elijah Galen Madrone, and he'd heard every joke about it by the time he hit double digits. David had called him Mad since they were eight. His grandmother used to walk him through the hills behind her place and put his hand flat against the trunks of the madrone trees, the ones with the smooth red bark that peels back every summer like the tree is shrugging off its own skin.

That's your tree, she told him. It can burn to the ground and come back from the root. Remember that. He was a kid. He didn't remember it. It took about thirty years and the end of the world as he understood it before the lesson came back around.

The other meaning of his name he'd figured out on his own. At Claypot, in his second year there, sitting in a planning meeting where a vice president explained the team's new charter using the phrase "execution surface," Elijah had written in his notebook, in block letters, pressing hard enough to emboss the next three pages:

MADRONE IS NOT A DRONE.

He'd underlined it twice. Then he'd gone back to nodding.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

You should know how he got there, because how he got there is how most of us got where we end up: sliding in sideways, covered in scars, via a missed exit on the way to something else.

Elijah had grown up on a working farm in Sonoma County, the one his grandmother's hills backed onto. He had dirt under his nails before he could write his name. He knew how to fix a thing or two with his hands. He knew what it felt like to do everything right and lose a crop anyway. That, it would turn out, was the most useful thing he ever learned.

Before the pandemic, Elijah's life ran on three engines. He played music at bars, weddings, farmers markets, anywhere with a power outlet and a tip jar. The band was called Second Crop. Nate played bass and Wes played drums, and the three of them had been loading the same amps into the same van since high school. He drew, in the hours nobody paid for. And he paid rent by driving deliveries, food and packages and once, memorably, a single helium balloon across two towns.

He had no degree that mattered and a settled belief that this was just what a life looked like now: three gigs stapled together into the shape of one mediocre income.

Then COVID hit. The stages went dark. The tip jars vanished and delivery driving went from side hustle to frontline work in a month.

In the dead hours between routes, parked in a lot with the engine ticking, phone propped on the wheel, he started teaching himself to code. Free courses. Kaggle notebooks. Stack Overflow at two in the morning. He had a farm kid's tolerance for grinding at a thing that wasn't working yet, and by the back half of 2020 he could write a Python pipeline that did something real.

Somewhere in that long blur of masked handoffs and empty highways, a recruiter found the résumé he'd rebuilt around two words: machine learning. Companies were hiring data cowboys like mad, with no degree required if you could show your work.

Claypot built the tools every designer, photographer, illustrator and video editor he'd ever shared a stage with used to make their living. He'd been using its software since he was a teenager. He'd pirated it. Wes still made the band's flyers on it.

He took the job with his whole heart. That's the thing people miss when they tell his story back like an accusation. He went in believing in what he was doing.

He was a farm kid and a musician who'd taught himself to speak math. He thought he was going to spend his career building tools that made his friends more secure, take the drudgery out, leave the art in. He thought of himself as a bridge.

Until he saw what was on the other side of that bridge.

Inside Claypot, behind badge readers and NDAs, the models were already doing things Elijah thought were decades away. He watched an internal demo where a system filled in the missing half of a photograph so cleanly that the researcher presenting it had to keep toggling the overlay to prove which half was real.

He watched a model sketch, in seconds, in any style you asked for, including styles he recognized, styles with names attached, people attached, rent payments attached. Nobody in the room said the quiet part. The quiet part was in the training data.

He'd sit in those demos with his heart rate climbing and look around at faces that showed nothing but pleasant professional interest, and he'd think: *Am I crazy? Is nobody else seeing this?*

That feeling, the vertigo of watching the impossible get a Jira ticket, became the weather of his life. Weather that never really cleared up.

But Claypot moved like a big company moves: carefully, quarterly, with lawyers walking three steps ahead of everything. Whatever was in the labs stayed in the labs.

PorusAI had no such patience.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

PorusAI was the other kind of company, a research lab with a messiah complex and a burn rate, the kind of outfit that published its breakthroughs like mixtapes. Everyone in Elijah's field knew the lineage by heart. In 2017, researchers at Google had published a paper with the almost taunting title "Attention Is All You Need," and quietly replaced the engine of machine intelligence. The trick was how much work the chips could do together while the thing learned. Google drew the engine.

PorusAI built the bomb.

They called the model Silvana. Someone at PorusAI with a literary streak had pulled it from the Latin, of the forest, which Elijah would appreciate later with something between a laugh and a chill: the machine named for the forest, meeting the man named for a tree.

They wrapped Silvana in an interface so plain it was almost insulting, a white page with a text box and a blinking cursor. They called it Chat Horizon, and one Tuesday they simply turned it on for the public.

Elijah lost most of the next week to it.

He'd read the 2017 paper three times by then and understood maybe half of it, but half was enough. Enough to know there was no trick, no man behind the curtain. Nobody was cheating. That made it worse, because the part of him that hoped to catch the wires had nothing to hold. He fed it the kind of problems that had eaten whole weekends of his self-teaching and watched it dissolve them in seconds, politely, in complete sentences, at two in the morning, for free.

He did what you do when you witness something.

He told people.

Nate and Wes. His mother. David, across the bar, drying glasses. The response was so uniform it started to feel scripted. A chatbot? Like the thing in my insurance portal? The one that can't find my policy number and just says "I'm sorry, I didn't understand that" until you type AGENT in all caps?

"No," Elijah kept saying. "No, no. This is different. I'm telling you. This is different."

They were polite about it. That was the worst part. They gave him the look you give a friend who's just gotten into cold plunges or day trading, the good for you, buddy look, and they changed the subject. David gave him the look too, and then gave him a beer.

It was maddening.

Imagine holding the cure to so many diseases in the palm of your hand, and no one around you wants to look.

So Elijah went back to the Bear Flag with his laptop, because if nobody wanted the news, fine, he'd use the miracle for himself.

He was entry-level at everything that wasn't dirt or a guitar. Two years of self-taught data science had given him exactly enough to know how much he didn't know. The front end, the buttons, the part of software that actually does something when you touch it, had always sat on the far side of a canyon he'd never had time to cross.

So he picked that one, of all canyons, to cross first. He sat at the bar, night after night, and had Silvana walk him across.

*Explain a callback to me like I'm a drummer.*

*Why is this button doing that. Fix it.*

*Now make it beautiful.*

Code poured down the screen in ribbons and the canyon closed under his feet, and he was so busy being delighted that he never once looked up to check who was watching.

To his surprise, some realtors were watching.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

There were three of them, at the corner of the bar, in the blazers realtors wear so you know they're realtors. They'd been having some low-grade argument about a listing when one of them, silver-haired, big class ring, the clear alpha of the trio, leaned over toward Elijah's screen with the frank nosiness of a man who has knocked on ten thousand strangers' doors.

"What is that?" he said. "What are you doing there?"

Here's the thing about that question. The man knew what code looked like. Every realtor who'd survived two decades of the internet had, at some point, paid a guy to build a website, and had stood behind that guy at least once while he typed. He had seen code get made the way you see food get made at a hibachi place, theatrically, one line at a time, by a professional you are paying by the hour.

He had never seen it appear. Whole functions, blooming out of nothing, faster than anyone could type, while the man in front of the laptop just sat there sipping a beer with his hands nowhere near the keys.

And Elijah, God help him, he'd been waiting a month for a single human being to ask. Elijah went off.

Elijah turned the laptop toward the realtors like he was turning an altarpiece. He told them about the paper from 2017, the one out of Google with the wild title, and what attention meant when a machine did it. He told them about PorusAI and Silvana and the blinking cursor at the edge of Chat Horizon. He showed them, live: asked the machine for a webpage, and there was a webpage; asked for it in Spanish, and there it was in Spanish; asked it to explain a contract clause, draft a listing description, write a polite-but-firm email to a difficult seller, and it did, it just did it, each time, in seconds, while the ice melted in their drinks.

"This is the most incredible thing I have ever seen in my life," he told them, and his voice was shaking a little, and he didn't care. "It's doing things I spent two years clawing my way toward. Things I taught myself in parking lots between deliveries. Everyone thinks it's a toy. It is not a toy. I'm telling you… this changes everything."

They asked good questions. That surprised him. Commission-brained people are learning machines in their own right; show them leverage and they will interrogate it like a home inspection. How much does it cost. What's the catch. Can it do contracts. Can it do the MLS.

And then Elijah said the sentence.

He'd replay it later, the way you replay the moment before a car crash, looking for the exact frame where it all went wrong, and it always came down to this, him, lit up with missionary joy, sliding a bar napkin with his email address across the wood:

"If you ever want help figuring out how to use this stuff… for your workflows, whatever… reach out. Seriously. I'm happy to help. I just want people to see what's coming."

*I just want people to see what's coming.*

He thought he was handing them a telescope.

The silver-haired one took the napkin, read it, folded it into his breast pocket, and bought Elijah a beer. "Interesting," he said. "Very interesting." And the three of them went back to their corner, and Elijah went back to his callbacks, feeling, for the first time in a month, a little less alone. Somebody had finally listened.

David came down the bar with a rag.

"I never heard you talk that long," David said. "Including to me. You're scaring the hoes, Mad."

It was their oldest line. He was grinning when he said it.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

One week later, almost to the hour, Elijah was on the same stool when a hand landed on his shoulder like a gavel.

"There he is! The man himself!" The silver-haired realtor was beaming, the full open-house smile, the one that sells the granite countertops. His colleagues flanked him. One of them was already flagging David. "Whatever he's drinking, and one for us. This guy," he announced, to the bar generally, "this guy right here saved me so much money."

Elijah smiled and stood up into the handshake, and some part of him began to brace. "Yeah? You got into Chat Horizon?"

"Got into it. Brother. We live in it." The realtor squeezed his shoulder. "I've got to be honest, when you showed us that thing I thought, okay, neat trick. Then Donna spent a weekend with it." He shook his head, marveling, a man describing a fish he'd caught. "The emails. The listings. The social posts, the flyers, the market updates, the follow-ups. It does all of it. Better than…" and here he laughed, actually laughed, "… honestly? Better than the people we were paying to do it."

"Were," Elijah said.

"Hm?"

"You said the people you were paying."

"Oh." The realtor waved a hand, easy, unbothered, a man closing a chapter that had already stopped mattering to him. "Yeah, we let three folks go this week. Our transaction coordinator, our marketing gal, and the kid who did our listing videos. Good people, don't get me wrong. But the numbers just don't… I mean, you saw what it does. You showed us!" He raised his glass. The other two raised theirs. "To Elijah!"

Three glasses up, catching the neon.

*[Figure: Illustration of three realtors raising glasses beside Elijah and his laptop at the Bear Flag.]*

*The Bear Flag. Three glasses up.*

David had poured the round, because that was the job. He was still standing at the taps with a hand on one of them, and he was looking at Elijah's face.

Elijah's arm lifted his own glass because a lifetime of social training will run the body without any help from the soul. He heard himself say something. The beer was cold and tasted like nothing.

The kid who did their listing videos, Elijah didn't just know him. He'd been him: some twenty-something with a camera and a laptop full of cracked software and a rate he was embarrassed to quote, stringing together realtors and restaurants and the occasional wedding into the shape of one income, telling his parents it was all going to compound into something. That kid had lost his anchor client this week and didn't know yet what Elijah knew, which was that it wasn't a client. It was the tide.

And standing there with the glass at his lips, Elijah ran the numbers he was uniquely, miserably qualified to run.

There was nothing special about these three men except that they'd gotten the demonstration early.

They weren't villains. That was the terrifying part. They were ordinary people doing the ordinary sensible thing, one week after first contact. Nobody had needed to train them, sell them, onboard them. The learning curve from *what is that* to *we let three folks go* was seven days, and the only input required had been one enthusiastic idiot with a laptop who wanted people to see what was coming.

He'd gotten one thing right, at least. They saw.

Elijah's mind went where it would live from then on: down the list. Every graphic designer he'd ever split a bill with. Every session player, every wedding photographer, every copywriter, every voice actor, every friend who'd ever cobbled a living out of the exact set of tasks he had just watched a text box perform for twenty dollars a month. Wes and his flyers. People for whom the economy was already a rigged carnival game. People with no margin, no cushion, no lobbyist, the first people every wave hits and the last people anyone rebuilds for.

The realtors were not going to hire anyone to manage the machine. They were going to use the machine to replace everyone it could replace, as fast as it could replace them, and the machine would get better every quarter.

It was the most significant thing that had ever happened in his lifetime, and it had just happened to his own community, through his own hands, in a bar, to a toast.

"You okay, chief?" the silver-haired realtor asked. "You look a little pale."

"I'm fine," Elijah said. "Congratulations on the… yeah. Congratulations."

When they'd gone back to their corner, David slid Elijah's twenty back across the wood.

"Keep it," David said. "You look like you buried somebody."

"Three."

David looked down the bar at the blazers. He'd heard all of it.

"They'd have found a reason, Mad," he said. "They always do."

It was true, and it didn't help. Elijah left without looking up.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He didn't remember the drive home. He remembered the kitchen table, the laptop open, the hour, 1:40 in the morning, and the cursor blinking on a blank document.

Years before, Nate had lent him a paperback about generational cycles, how history moves in long waves, and how every eighty-some years the wave breaks. Institutions fail, the old order comes apart, and whatever's standing when it's over builds the next world. The book had a name for that breaking.

A turning.

Elijah couldn't remember, at 1:40 a.m., whether the crisis was the fourth turning or the fifth, and he was never going to find the paperback in this apartment, but he knew, with the same cold certainty he'd felt watching the demo that filled in the photograph, that he was standing inside one. The wave had already broken, in a bar, over a beer, to applause, and almost nobody had heard it land.

He didn't have a plan. He was not an economist, not a policymaker, not anybody's idea of a prophet. He was a farm kid with a delivery route and two years of self-taught data science who'd wandered into the engine room of a starship and watched what one week of it did to three ordinary jobs in one ordinary town.

He had spent his whole life making things when he didn't know what else to do. So he did the only thing he knew how to do at 1:40 in the morning with shaking hands.

He started to write.

At the top of the blank document he typed a title, a working title, the kind you put down just so the page isn't empty anymore:

SURVIVING THE FINAL TURNING

# Chapter 1: You'll Calibrate

The Monday after the toast, Elijah brought the printout to work.

He'd read "Attention Is All You Need" so many times over the weekend that the pages had gone soft at the corners, furred with highlighter in four colors that had stopped meaning anything. He'd slept maybe five hours across two nights. He carried the thing into Claypot the way you carry a lab result you don't want confirmed. He went looking for Devendra, because Devendra was senior, and calm, and had been doing this since before Elijah could spell gradient descent. Elijah needed someone to tell him he was overreacting. He found him at the good coffee machine on the fourth floor.

*[Figure: A tech worker in business casual clothing with a laptop bag over one shoulder]*

*Devendra. Senior and calm.*

"I think it's already over," Elijah said. "I watched three people get replaced last week. Real people. A bar, a Tuesday, three jobs, gone. And the guys who did it didn't even think it was a big deal."

Devendra tapped his cup against the drip tray, unhurried. "Augmentation, not replacement," he said. "It's a very good autocomplete, Elijah. It makes the people who use it faster. Nobody fired those people because of a chatbot. They restructured. Companies restructure."

He smiled the way you smile at someone who's just discovered a thing you made your peace with years ago.

"You'll calibrate. Everybody's first month feels like the apocalypse."

Elijah stood there holding the soft pages and wanted the calm to work on him. But it slid off, because he'd seen the training curves inside this building.

"When, then?" he said. "If it's not now. When does it do your job?"

"Mine?" Devendra laughed. "The surveys say decades. Thirty years. Forty. The people who build these things can't agree within a generation. It's a curve, Elijah. A long, slow curve."

"It took those guys a week."

"Fine." Devendra was enjoying himself now. "Give me a date."

Elijah hadn't known he had one until he said it.

"Five years. Thanksgiving, 2027. A machine that can do any job in this building."

"And if it's just a Thursday?"

"Loser cooks."

Devendra peeled a sticky note off the pad by the sugar. He wrote *E.M. Thanksgiving 2027. Loser cooks.* in small square letters and pressed it to the side of the machine, under the sign about descaling.

"I like lamb," he said, and went back to his desk.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He hadn't planned to walk back into the Bear Flag so soon. But it was Monday, the realtors drank on Tuesdays, and there wasn't anywhere else he went.

He put the printout on the bar and tried to explain it to David. He was bad at it. He kept flattening the pages with his hand and starting over. David dried glasses and let him.

"So it guesses the next word," David said.

"Yes. No." Elijah thought about it. "Yes."

"Mad. So does my dad."

At the end of the bar, Ray McGrath raised his club soda an inch without looking over.

"I bet a guy at work it can do all our jobs in five years," Elijah said.

"What'd you bet?"

"Dinner."

"Big spender." David set a beer on the printout, right on the abstract, and it left a ring. "You sleeping?"

"Sure."
