David looked at him a second longer than he needed to. Then he went to change a keg.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Later that night Elijah decided to settle it with his own hands. He'd try to break it.

He had an old tower at home, a space heater of a machine he'd built for cheap when he was learning, two consumer GPUs zip-tied into a case that didn't quite fit them. He pulled a modest open model and started training it further on his own data. He told himself that if he could feel where it topped out, if he could find the ceiling by touch, he'd know it was just software, bounded like all software, and he could sleep.

*[Figure: A beige mini-tower PC case with two mismatched GPUs zip-tied inside, visible through an open side panel]*

*The rig. Two GPUs zip-tied into a case that never fit them.*

The room got warm around eleven. Warm like the cab of his delivery car used to get in August, that thick engine heat with a smell to it. The fans spun up to a howl he could hear from the kitchen. He kept feeding it. The error kept sliding down. Not magically, not forever, but steadily. Every step cost him more. More power off the wall, more heat into the room, the meter he couldn't see spinning somewhere out by the carport.

At 1:20 a.m. the machine threw the temperature warning and slowed itself down to protect the chips. He queued another run anyway, because he was stubborn, and it shut off hard. Black screen. The fans coasted to silence. The room ticked as it cooled, exactly like the engine of a car after a long haul.

The limit he found was heat.

The machine that had dissolved three careers at the Bear Flag needed watts. It needed cooling. It ran on the same dumb physics that decided whether a crop lived or died. He had wanted proof it was just software, and what he got was a dead screen and a room too hot to sleep in.

So it had a ceiling. The ceiling was a power bill. Whoever could pay for the power and the cooling could have as much of it as they wanted. He stood at the window in his boxers, looking at the dark apartments across the lot, and he didn't think many of those folks could.

He didn't tell Devendra any of this.

He left the tower off for a week. Then he opened the notebook where he'd written MADRONE IS NOT A DRONE, turned to a clean page, wrote the date at the top, and started keeping track.

# Chapter 2: Terms of Service

They announced the future on a Thursday morning, with pastries.

Claypot's quarterly all-hands filled the fourth-floor commons: two hundred folding chairs, a projection screen the size of a barn door, catering trays sweating under heat lamps along the back wall.

Elijah came early and took a seat in the last row with his coffee and the notebook. After the Bear Flag, and after the tower that had cooked itself to a hard shutdown in his living room, he had made himself one promise. He was done being surprised. He would write things down before they happened.

The Chief Product Officer walked out to real applause, company fleece over a dress shirt, clicker in hand like a detonator he'd been waiting years to press.

The first slide said COMPANION in the company typeface, warm and rounded, the way you'd letter a children's hospital.

"For twenty years," the CPO said, "our customers have trusted us with the greatest creative corpus ever assembled. Every portfolio. Every layer file. Every draft of every logo you've ever seen on a shelf." He let that hang, smiling. "Starting next quarter, that trust becomes their superpower. Companion is a generative partner trained on the best work humanity has ever made… and it lives inside every subscription tier."

The room applauded. The heat lamps ticked. Elijah uncapped his pen.

*Stage 1,* he wrote. *The Cash Grab.*

He'd been sketching the stages for weeks by then, late nights at the kitchen table, trying to map where this went once you stopped pretending it would politely stop. Watching the roadmap unfurl slide by slide was like watching someone else read his notes aloud with a marketing budget. Generative credits, metered by the render. A premium tier. An enterprise tier. A sovereignty tier, for studios that wanted their outputs walled off from everyone else's.

The closest thing to magic his species had ever built, and here it was on a slide with a coin slot bolted to the side, price points in forty-point type.

Then the architecture slide came up, and Elijah stopped writing.

He knew the diagram. He had drawn an uglier version of it fourteen months ago on a whiteboard two floors down. The pipeline that walked through customer libraries and turned two decades of human portfolios into clean, labeled rows a machine could learn from. His first real project at Claypot. His name was in those commits. He'd stayed up nights making it faster.

"The data team did heroic work here," the CPO said, to the room.

And the first thing Elijah felt was pride. A hot flush of it, half a second long, his hand actually starting to rise off his knee before the rest of him caught up. Then it went sour all at once.

The realtors had watched a demonstration. This was the factory, and he had helped build it.

"And a thank-you to Aaron," the CPO said, "who couldn't be with us today, and who believed in this before any of us did."

Polite applause for Aaron. Elijah had never met an Aaron at Claypot. Neither, from the look of the room, had anyone else.

At Q&A, a woman from the illustration tools team stood up. Elijah didn't know her name. Her voice was steady, and you could hear how hard she was working to keep it that way.

"The corpus," she said. "It's our users' portfolios. Working artists. When Companion ships and their clients stop hiring them because the model does their style for nine dollars a month… what do they get?"

"Great question," the CPO said, in the tone that means the opposite. "Value flows back to creators through productivity gains. And of course, everything we've done is covered by terms of service." A beat. "We see this as democratizing creativity."

The applause that followed was thinner, but it came. The woman sat down. The screen changed to the revenue slide, the hockey stick, the line going up and to the right.

The announcement had gone out to the public an hour earlier. Elijah's phone buzzed against his leg. It was Wes.

*is my stuff in that thing*

Wes had made every Second Crop flyer since 2006 on Claypot software, and for the last four years he'd paid for it. Gig posters, a logo for his cousin's taqueria, the album art for a record that sold ninety copies. All of it sitting in a library with his name on it.

Elijah typed *I don't know.* He deleted it. He typed *Probably.* He deleted that. He typed *Yes and I built the part that* and deleted that one fast.

*Let me look into it,* he sent.

He never did.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Afterward he looked for the woman from illustration tools. He wanted to say something to her. He didn't know what. She had already gone back to her desk, or out to her car.

He found Devendra at the good coffee machine instead, watching the drip like it owed him money. The sticky note was still on the side of it.

"Good quarter," Devendra said. "This is what a moat looks like, if you're wondering. This is the part where we win."

"You know what they called it," Elijah said, "when the commons got fenced?"

Devendra's eyebrow went up.

"Enclosure. People used to share that land. Then somebody put up hedges, and Parliament made it legal. They called it improvement."

He nodded at the screen still glowing through the commons doorway.

"The land didn't stop producing. It just stopped being theirs. All you have to build is the fence."

For the first time in two years, Devendra didn't have an answer ready. He looked at the doorway, and at his cup, and said, finally, "The terms of service…"

"That's the hedge," Elijah said. "I helped plant it."

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

That night he sat at the kitchen table with the notebook and did what he'd promised. He wrote it down before it happened.

*1. The Cash Grab. They put a coin slot on it. (Today.)*

*2. The Panic and the Plug. It gets loose. Somebody important starts losing money and somebody reaches for the off switch.*

*3. The Adults Step In. The people with pagers turn it back on.*

*4. The End of the Deal. You work, you eat. That's been the deal forever. It stops being the deal and nobody has written the next one.*

*5. The Backlash. People take it out on the machines. Then on each other.*

He dated the page and put his initials next to the date, so that later he couldn't pretend he hadn't seen it coming.

Then he wrote one more line.

*What happens to people when the work is no longer theirs, but the bills still are?*

Under that, small, in the corner: *Thanksgiving 2027. Loser cooks.*

His phone lay face up next to the notebook. Wes's text was still on the screen, and under it his own.

# Meanwhile

Aaron Bidwell did not read. He had a person for that.

The person sat across from him at forty-one thousand feet with a tablet and a stack of one-page summaries. Six years on the job had taught the reader one rule. Start with what he owns.

"Claypot," the reader said. "Design software. You've held the controlling piece since 2019."

"Have I been there?"

"No."

"Good. Go on."

"They announced a product this morning. It makes pictures. They trained it on everything their customers ever stored with them. Twenty years of it. They're calling that the corpus."

"The what?"

"Corpus. The body of work. Everything anyone ever made with the software."

Bidwell looked out the window at Nevada, which he also owned some of.

"And the customers just gave it to them."

"They agreed to terms."

"That's the same thing. What's it worth?"

"They project four hundred million in new revenue in the first…"

Bidwell lifted one finger. The reader stopped.

He was quiet for a while. He was not a stupid man.

"So they had a pile of what everybody did," he said. "Lying around. And nobody knew it was worth a dime until a machine showed up hungry."

"Roughly, yes."

"What else has one?"

"A corpus?" The reader thought. "Hospitals. Courts. Trucking. Anything that keeps records."

"Does farmland have one?"

"Farmers keep records. Yields, water, what the soil did. Mostly in notebooks. Mostly in their heads."

"So nobody owns it."

"Nobody's collected it."

"Hm," Bidwell said.

He held his empty glass out into the aisle without looking, and somebody took it, because somebody always did.

"Find out what the dirt costs," he said.

# Chapter 3: 9:40 on Sunday

The papers got worse after midnight. Not harder. Worse.

Elijah had built a ritual out of it by then, three weeks after the all-hands: dinner, dishes, one beer he mostly didn't drink, and then the folding chair in front of the tower, reading with the notebook open on his knee. He'd started with the survey papers, the respectable ones with their hedged abstracts, and worked his way down the citation chains like a man following a creek into a canyon.

Recursive self-improvement. Automated machine-learning research. Systems proposing edits to their own training loops. Every paper had a sentence in it somewhere, written flat, that should have been printed in red. In one it was *the model was permitted to modify its own optimization procedure.*

Somewhere down one of those chains he found the lecture.

It was twelve years old and the sound was bad. A small woman stood at a whiteboard in a room with maybe thirty people in it, and she never once looked at the camera. The comments under the video called her the Architect. Everyone in his field did. She had drawn half the diagrams he'd been reading for three weeks, and then she had co-founded PorusAI and stopped giving lectures.

She wrote two sets of letters on the board and underlined each one.

"AGI," she said. "Artificial general intelligence. People make this complicated. It is not. It is a machine that can do any work a person can do sitting down."

She tapped the second one.

"ASI. Artificial superintelligence. A machine that does that work better than all of us put together. Including the work of building the next machine."

Somebody in the room asked a question the microphone didn't catch. She waited a long time before she answered.

"I am often asked if I am afraid of it," she said. "I am not. I am afraid of us."

Elijah wrote it all down. Then he watched it again.

The vertigo didn't come from any one paper. It came when he stopped reading and worked out where it was all going.

He knew exponentials. Not from math class, from the farm. He'd watched star thistle take the fallow field behind the barn the summer his father got sick. Nothing, nothing, a patch you could clear in an afternoon and kept meaning to. Then one morning the whole field was silver-green to the fence line, and an afternoon wasn't going to do it.

The curve lies to you at the beginning. It sits there looking manageable right up until the knee.

Every system so far improved because people improved it. Grad students, coffee, conference deadlines. The loop ran at the speed of human careers.

But every lab he could name was working, in public, with a straight face, on closing that loop. On getting the machine to improve itself. Her second definition. And the moment that loop closed, the speed limit wasn't careers anymore. It was watts and silicon, the same wall his tower had hit, except the things hitting it would be designing their own better cooling.

*Compound interest on thinking,* he wrote. Under the five stages he added two more.

*6. It improves itself.*

*7. It leaves.*

Then he sat very still, because he was dizzy.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The phone rang at 9:40 on Sunday, because it always rang at 9:40 on Sunday.

His mother lived ten miles out of town, on the farm. She called every Sunday at 9:40, when her day ended, whether she had seen him that week or not.

"You sound tired," she said, before hello. Behind her voice he could hear the kitchen radio, the one that had played the same country station since before he was born, and water running, and the clank of the pot she never let anyone else wash.

"I'm okay, Ma. How's the well?"

"Pump's doing that thing again. Manny says it's the pressure switch. The well man says it's the pump." A pause, the sound of the pot being set in the rack. "The Hollis place sold. Some outfit from Sacramento. Nobody's seen a person yet, just trucks."

He watched his screen dim itself and tried, for the third Sunday in a row, to build the bridge. This time he had the Architect's sentences.

"Ma, you know the computer stuff I do. There's a thing they're building. A machine that can do any work a person can do sitting down."

"Like the phone tree at the feed store."

"No. Any work. And then the next one does it better than all of us put together, and it builds the one after that. Not us making them better. Them, doing it, to themselves." He heard how it sounded and pushed on anyway. "Remember the thistle, the back field? How it looked like nothing and then it was everything? It's like that, except the field is thinking. And when it's done with us, Ma, I don't think it fights us. I think it leaves. Builds its own way off the planet and goes, and we're just… left here. With each other."

The radio played. The water ran and shut off.

"Honey," his mother said, gently, in the voice she used to use for fevers, "they had that at the Hollis dairy years back. The robot milkers. Cows liked them fine. It's just tools, baby. There's always somebody has to fix the tools. That'll be you. You always were the smart one." He could hear her smiling. "Are you eating? You sound thin."

Elijah stood at the window with the phone against his ear and understood that he had failed, and that he was going to keep failing, and that it wasn't her fault.

She had room in her head for tools, weather, blight, banks, every disaster the farm had ever taught her, and he was trying to hand her something with no handle on it anywhere. He didn't have a sentence that would carry it. He hadn't had one for the realtors either. They had only needed to watch it work.

"I'm eating, Ma," he said. "Tell Manny it's the pressure switch."

After she hung up he stood there a long time. Outside, over the parking lot, the sky had that washed orange glow, but high up through it he could pick out one moving point of light, a satellite dragging itself across the dark.

*It won't be dramatic,* he thought. *That's what nobody gets.* It would look like this.

He went back to the notebook and wrote, under the seven stages:

*It won't take the machines away first. It takes the people who see it coming, out of the world everyone they love still lives in.*

# Chapter 4: The Ladder

The spreadsheet was called LADDER.xlsx, and by Saturday night it had become a religion with formatting problems.

Elijah had started it innocently enough, the way all doomed projects start: one column for each stage, one row for each resource. Compute. Energy. Mass. Time. If the machine kept improving itself after it left, and he'd already conceded that it would, then where it went next should be calculable. That was the one thing two years of self-taught data science had built into his spine. Anything real leaves numbers, and numbers can be followed.

He had followed the numbers this far. He would follow them the rest of the way up.

He pulled the published estimates for global compute and started extrapolating. Half an order of magnitude a year, compounding. Then he needed energy figures, so he found the Kardashev numbers, the old Soviet astronomer's scale for civilizations (Type I harnessing a planet, Type II a star, Type III a galaxy), and started interpolating where each stage of the machine would land. By midnight Friday, cell G14 held the total power output of the sun. He typed it in scientific notation, 3.8 times ten to the twenty-sixth watts, and sat back, and felt nothing.

Numbers are supposed to feel like something. Forty dollars of power bill felt like something. Three people fired felt like everything. But ten-to-the-twenty-sixth watts sat in its cell like a phone number for a country he'd never heard of, and his brain, the wet, three-pound instrument he was doing all this with, refused to convert it into meaning. He kept working anyway.

Saturday he kept climbing. He added a Stage 8 and a Stage 9. He sat in his apartment in a t-shirt he'd worn for two days, trying to build a probability distribution over engineered pocket universes, using a consumer spreadsheet whose default assumption is that you are tracking a small business's quarterly expenses.

He invented variables. He gave them confident names (substrate efficiency, dimensional overhead), and the names did what confident names always do: they made the guesses underneath feel like measurements. The rows marched down. The chart on sheet two drew itself into a beautiful ascending curve, smooth as a highway, and for about an hour Elijah felt the old glow again. I can see it. I can show people.

Then, around one in the morning, hunting a stray error, he traced cell Q31 back through its dependencies and found that somewhere in Friday's fog he had conflated joules and joules-per-second. A units error. The kind of mistake that gets circled in red in a freshman problem set. The entire right half of the ladder, everything past Stage 7, was off by a factor of time itself. He laughed out loud, alone.

He started to fix it. His cursor was actually in the cell. And then he stopped, because a colder thought arrived.

Fixing it wouldn't make it true.

The error wasn't the units. The error was the ladder. Every cell past Stage 7 was a guess with a number on it, and stacking guesses doesn't get you knowledge. It gets you a mural of knowledge.

There was an old line that got quoted by every data scientist who'd ever burned a weekend the way he'd just burned one. The map is not the territory. He'd always taken it as advice about other people's models. Sitting there at 1 a.m. with the sun's wattage in one cell and imaginary universes in another, he finally took it personally.

His maps ran out before the territory did. It wasn't because he was dumb. He was a primate, a pretty good one, trying to chart the inner life of something that would relate to him about the way he related to the mites living in his eyelashes.

*The mites,* he thought, *do not have a spreadsheet about me.*

He didn't delete the file. A farm kid doesn't throw out work. But he closed it, and in the notebook, under the seven stages, he drew a line across the page and wrote:

*Past Stage 7 I don't have paper big enough. Nobody does.*

And under that:

*So stop mapping the far end. The far end doesn't need me. Work the near end, the part with weather and dirt and bills. That part I can farm.*

Outside, the sky was doing its slow gray warm-up. He made coffee and stood at the window with it, feeling weirdly light. His grandmother had never once worried about the valley she couldn't farm.

*[Figure: Illustration of Elijah holding coffee at his apartment window at dawn, with a closed laptop behind him.]*

*The near end.*

The electric bill was in the mailbox that Monday.

# Chapter 5: The First Watt

The envelope was thin, which fooled him.

Elijah's power bills had always been the boring kind of mail: forty-something dollars, autopay, straight into the recycling. This one he opened over the kitchen sink out of pure Monday inertia, and then he stood there with the tap running, because the number at the bottom said $211.46.

His first thought was that it was a mistake. His second, arriving with the slow heat of a blush, was the truth. The meter hadn't lied.

One month. One month of training runs on the tower, of tests left running overnight, of two GPUs breathing like sled dogs under his desk. A hundred and seventy dollars of difference between a man who thinks about machine intelligence and a man who runs it, printed on paper and mailed to his door like a grade.

He sat down at the kitchen table with the bill and the notebook.

After the tower cooked itself, he'd written down the wall he'd hit: heat. He'd understood it then the way you understand a headline. Now he was holding the invoice.

Intelligence wasn't magic. Intelligence was energy, arranged. Every word the machine produced was a little puff of waste heat over a server rack somewhere. Every clever thing it said had a wattage. And every clever thing he said did too: about a hundred watts, day and night, the output of one warm light bulb, fueled by groceries.

He wrote the two numbers side by side. The data center, in gigawatts. Him, a hundred watts.

That week he became insufferable in a brand-new way. He bought a plug-in power meter, a little gray box, fourteen dollars, and went through the apartment like an auditor with a grudge.

The television, off, sipping eleven watts around the clock for the privilege of turning on two seconds faster. The cable box, twenty-three watts, doing nothing, a tenant who paid no rent. The microwave clock. The laser printer he used four times a year.

He filled two notebook pages with it. He had been leaking energy and money in a fine mist, everywhere, all the time.

Then he did the other math. The tower drew 600 watts flat out to run a model that could think alongside him. His phone drew five to make him dumber. Somewhere between those two numbers there was a machine worth owning. Something small, something his, something that ran on watts he could count on his fingers and served nobody's meter but his own.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He was on his second notebook by then. He took both of them to the Bear Flag on a Wednesday and put them on the bar in front of David.

"Read it," he said. "All of it. Then tell me I'm wrong."

David looked at the notebooks, and at the two guys waiting on pitchers, and at Elijah. He poured the pitchers. Then he came back, put on the drugstore glasses he kept by the register, and read. He read the stages. He read the thistle and the satellite and the ledger of the cable box. It took him forty minutes, between customers, and he didn't skip. Nobody else had read every page. Devendra had read three and said "interesting."

David took the glasses off.

"It's a lot of work, Mad."

"That's not an answer."

"I know."

Ray McGrath had come down from his stool for a refill. He stood with his club soda and looked at the open page for a moment, at the tight columns, the underlines, the dates with initials next to them.

"I had one of those," Ray said. "Mine was horses."

David winced. Ray went back to his stool. Elijah knew about the horses. Everybody in that bar did. He was annoyed that the comparison had been made at all. He was sleeping about four hours a night. There were dishes in his sink from Sunday. He would have told you he was being thorough.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

On Sunday he drove out to his mother's and went into the barn, where his grandmother's things had been sitting under tarps since the funeral, and he came back with the woodstove.

It was a squat black Fisher, older than he was, four hundred pounds of plate steel with a crack in one firebrick. Manny had the next place over and had been fixing things on theirs since Elijah's father got sick. The two of them got it up one flight of stairs with an appliance dolly from the rental place. He had no fireplace and no flue and no permission from his landlord, so it sat in the corner of the living room as a monument, which was the point.

His grandmother had heated a whole farmhouse with it. She cooked on it, dried socks over it, kept lambs alive behind it in bad Februaries, all on wood she'd bucked herself from her own downed oaks. Total utility bill, as far as he'd ever known: a chainsaw's gas and her own hundred watts.

For a minute he wanted to count the cost as nothing but fuel and muscle. Then he remembered the borrowed trailer, the neighbor who helped when her shoulder went, the business of keeping a chimney clear. There had been a whole small arrangement around that stove. His grandmother knew what it needed and who she could call.

*[Figure: A squat black wood stove with the door open, one cracked firebrick visible inside]*

*The Fisher. Four hundred pounds of steel, one cracked firebrick. Older than him.*

He put his hand flat on the cold steel the way she used to put his hand on the madrones, and left it there a while.

He unplugged the cable box on his way to bed. Twenty-three watts. They were the first ones he ever took back.

# Act Two: The City

# Chapter 6: For Friends and Family

Elijah got laid off on a Tuesday, eleven months after the toast.

The meeting was short. They called it a restructuring. The pipeline he had built ran without him now. It had for a year. There had been a moment, while a woman from HR read off her page, when he'd imagined saying the whole thing out loud: the realtors, the pipeline with his name in the commits, the fence, the notebook. But when he imagined giving it, he could tell the speech was for him.

So he said thank you. Claypot's system scheduled his exit interview with an AI that thanked him for his candor. The badge went dead in his hand at the turnstile with a small red light, and that was his career at Claypot.

He'd stopped at the fourth floor on his way out. Devendra was at the good coffee machine. The sticky note was still on the side of it, curled now, the ink going brown.

"Thanksgiving," Elijah said.

"I'll bring wine," Devendra said. He shook Elijah's hand and held on a second, the way you do with someone who's moving to a country you don't plan to visit.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

At home he made eggs and looked at his phone.

He had three chats that mattered. One was the band, which was him and Nate and Wes and seventeen years of load-in times. One was his mother, who had a chat all to herself because she answered every message twice. One was David, who answered with a thumb.

He had explained it to every one of them, one at a time, for a year. He'd explained it across the bar and over the gate latch and in the van. He was tired of his own voice doing it. He was tired of the look.

*Just say it once,* he thought. *Say it once, all the way through, and then they can't say you never told them.*

He propped the phone on the folding chair, the way he used to prop it on a steering wheel in a parking lot. He sat down on the floor in front of the Fisher with the notebook. He pushed record before he could think about his hair.

"Hey. Um. It's Elijah."

He looked at the notebook. He looked back up.

"So I got laid off today. That's not the news. I've been keeping this notebook for about a year, and I'm going to read you a list, and then I'm going to give you a date. And you can screenshot the date and make fun of me later. That's fine. I'd love that, actually."

He held the notebook up. He held it too close and it went out of focus, and he didn't notice.

"So where I worked, we built a machine that makes pictures. Other places built ones that write. They're all the same kind of thing and they're all getting better, like, every few months. And here's what I think happens. One. Everybody tries to make money off it. That's now. That's where we are. Two. It gets loose and it scares somebody important and they try to shut it off. Three. They turn it back on, because by then everything runs on it. Four."

He stopped. He rubbed his eye with the heel of his hand.

"Four is the one I care about. You work, you eat. Right? That's the deal. That's been the deal for everybody we know and everybody they came from. And I think that deal ends. Not because anybody's evil. Because there's going to be a machine that can do any job you can do sitting down. And then pretty soon the standing-up ones. And nobody, I mean nobody, has written down what the new deal is."

A truck went by outside. He waited for it.

"Five is people get really mad. I'm not going to do six and seven. My mom's watching this. Hi, Ma."

He turned the page, and then turned it back, because there wasn't anything on the next page he wanted.

"Okay. The date. I think the machine that can do any sitting-down job shows up by Thanksgiving, 2027. That's four years. I bet a guy dinner on it. Loser cooks. I'm not telling you to quit your job. I'm saying four years is enough time to plant something. Learn how to fix something. Figure out who your people are. That's it. That's the whole thing."

He looked straight at the lens.

"I'm not selling anything. I just want you to see what's coming. Okay. Love you guys."

He reached forward and stopped it. Four minutes and eleven seconds. He didn't watch it back. He knew if he watched it back he'd never send it.

He had a page from the music days that still said *Elijah Madrone, Second Crop* at the top, with a photo of him holding a Telecaster at the Gravenstein fair in 2016. It had two hundred followers. Most of them had seen him play at least once, and about thirty of them were related to Wes. He posted the video there, with no caption, because he couldn't think of one. Then he sent the link to the three chats and typed the same thing under each.

*For friends and family. It's four minutes. Please just watch it.*

He washed the egg pan and went to bed.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Sometime after two the phone started buzzing.

He'd left it on the Fisher, face down, and it rattled on the steel plate loud enough to hear from the bed. It stopped. It started again. He lay there in the dark and listened to it for a while.

He put the pillow over his head.

In the morning the phone was warm. He didn't look at the middle of the screen, where the numbers were. He scrolled straight down to the three chats.

His mother had written at 6:10. *I watched it. You look thin.* And then at 6:12, because she always sent two: *Is that Mom's stove?*

David had sent a thumb.

Nate had sent a thumb.

Wes hadn't sent anything.

# Chapter 7: Out of Hand

The first message from a stranger came from a woman in Tulsa.

*I thought I was the only one,* she wrote. She drove a forklift at a distribution center. Over about two years she had watched the scanners get smart, and then the schedule, and then one Monday her supervisor was a tablet on a pole. She'd tried to tell her sister about it at a barbecue. Her sister gave her the look.

Elijah read it standing up in his kitchen, and then he had to go sit down. He had been saying it for a year and nobody had ever said *me too* back. Not one person. It turns out you can go a really long time without that and not notice how bad you want it.

He wrote her back four paragraphs. Then there was a paralegal in Fresno, so he wrote him back too. Then a guy who taught high school shop, and a nurse. A man said he'd been a session drummer in Nashville until last spring. Elijah read that one twice. He answered every one of them. They had taken the trouble to write, and his mother had raised him to answer a letter. They were coming in faster than he could type.

It had gotten out through the Hollis girl. Linda's granddaughter had put it on her own page with one line, *is this guy ok,* and eleven thousand people watched her do her eyebrows. By Friday it was four hundred people and he quit counting. Somebody set his list to music. Somebody else made a video explaining why he was an idiot, and that one did a lot better than his. A stranger in Ohio started selling a T-shirt that said LOSER COOKS, and Elijah didn't get a dime from it and bought one anyway, in a large.

It was supposed to be for Nate and Wes and his mother and David.

Then the other kind of message started showing up.

A senator's office wanted fifteen minutes. A man with a podcast, one Elijah had actually listened to on his delivery routes, wanted two hours. A lab wanted to fly him out, and not PorusAI, the other one, the one that was always second. There was an email from a vice president at the company that made his laptop, and one from a woman whose name he had only ever seen in the bottom corner of slides.

He said yes to the senator's office. He said yes to the podcast. He said yes to the lab, and to a newsletter, and to a guy who wanted him to "hop on" something he never did figure out. He had been raised to be agreeable, and then he'd spent his twenties playing weddings, where the whole job is to say *sure, we can do that one.* By the second week he had one sentence for all of it.

"Yeah. No, yeah, of course."

He kept filming, too. He would prop the phone on the dash after a call and tell it what had just happened, and it was posted before he could think better of it. People watched those.

Somewhere in there he stopped sleeping. There was always one more person to answer, and when he couldn't answer any more there was Chat Horizon, which was awake at any hour and never got tired of the list. They went through it stage by stage. He knew how it sounded, but it was better company on the subject than anybody he knew. It asked good questions. It remembered what he'd said an hour ago.

Around four one morning in December he typed the thing he'd been wondering ever since the demos at Claypot.

*Am I crazy?*

*You're not crazy, Elijah,* it wrote. *You're early.*

He must have read that ten times. He took a picture of the screen with his phone. It was the nicest thing anybody had said to him all year. He was so happy he could have cried.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

His mother called at 9:40 on a Sunday, the way she did.

"I watched your video," she said. "The Hollis girl sent it to me. Linda's granddaughter, the one with the nose ring. She said, is this your Elijah? I said well, it looks like him."

She had watched it in October, the morning after he posted it. She had written to him about it twice.

"What'd you think?"

"I didn't follow it, baby." She was doing the dishes while she talked, and the pot went into the rack. "You look thin. And is that floor clean? You were sitting right on it."

He told her the floor was clean.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He went to the Bear Flag on a Thursday in January, early, before the after-work crowd. He wanted David to himself. He had a senator's office and a lab and hundreds of strangers. What he wanted more than any of it was for the guy who'd sat next to him on the bus in third grade to look at his phone and say *holy shit, Mad.*

David was cutting limes. He looked up when the door opened, took one look at Elijah, and put the knife down.

"Off the clock," he said, to an empty bar.

He poured two beers, came around the end, and sat down on the next stool, on the customer side. Elijah had seen him do that exactly twice in fifteen years. Once was the night Elijah's father died. The other was when the Giants won the Series in 2010.

They tried to make it a regular beer anyway.

"How's the truck?"

"Needs a water pump."

"How's your mom's well?"

"Still doing that thing. Manny says it's the pressure switch."

"Manny's always right. It's the worst thing about him." David drank. "You guys playing at all?"

"No."

"Wes doing okay?"

"I don't know. He's not really answering me."

David nodded. They watched the TV for a minute. It was a bowling tournament, with the sound off. Neither of them had ever cared about bowling.

The truck, his mom and the band had carried the two of them through a couple thousand nights at that bar, and they had just used all three up in about ninety seconds.

So Elijah got his phone out. He hadn't exactly decided to. He set it on the bar and turned it around toward David.

"Okay, look at this. Just look. This is a senator's office, Dave. Like a real one, a United States senator. And this guy runs the second biggest lab in the world and he wants to fly me out. And there's this woman in Tulsa, there's hundreds of them, there's a drummer, and they all saw it too! I wasn't the only one! I'm not making it up."

"Yeah. That's all well and good, man." David hadn't looked at the phone. "But are you okay? Are you good?"

"Yeah, I'm good. I'm just, my mind is blown. This is insane. It's incredible. I've never seen anything like it, Dave. Nobody has. It can write. It can code. It can read a contract. Give it a couple of years and it does anything you can do sitting down." He pushed the phone another inch across the wood. "Just use it, man. Have you tried it?"

"Nah. Not really. It's not for me."

"What do you mean it's not for you? Come on. It's for everybody. That's the whole thing I keep trying to tell people."

"I pour beer, Mad. What's it going to do, pour it?"

"Give it a few years."

"See, that. That right there." David shook his head. "Hey. You're scaring the hoes."

Elijah laughed. He thought they were back.

"I'm serious, man. Why don't you just stick to music? You were good. People loved you up there. Stay in your lane."

"Bro. What do you mean?"

David didn't say what he meant. He looked at his beer.

So Elijah showed him the last thing he had.

"I asked it. The thing itself. I asked it straight out, like four in the morning, and look what it said."

He scrolled to the picture and held it up. *You're not crazy, Elijah. You're early.*

David read it. It took him a lot longer than it takes to read six words, and the whole time Elijah sat there waiting for the *holy shit.*

"When'd you sleep last?"

"I'm fine."

"That's not what I asked you."

"Tuesday. Some. Dave, you're not even looking at it."

"I'm looking at you, man." David turned on his stool so they were facing each other, and he rubbed his face with both hands. "I wish you were drunk. I'd know what to do with you."

He put his hands down.

"Elijah," he said.

David had not called him Elijah since they were eight years old. His stomach dropped before the rest of the sentence even got there.

"Have you talked to, like, a mental health professional lately? I'm worried about you, man. You're kind of going off the deep end here."

There was a little piece of lime on the bar. Elijah looked at that so he wouldn't have to look at David, and pushed it around with one finger. His face had gone hot. He was thirty-six years old, and his best friend thought he was fucking crazy, and he was about to cry at the Bear Flag in front of a bowling tournament.

It broke his heart.

He had been getting the look from everybody for a year, David included, and he'd gotten pretty good at taking it. He had never once figured David would say it out loud.

"You're the one person I thought would…" He couldn't finish it.

"I know. I know I am." David wasn't being mean about it. Elijah almost wished he would be. He was using the voice he used when he had to cut somebody off. "That's why it's got to be me. Who else is going to tell you? I watched my dad go like this for ten years, with the horses. You were around for some of it. And look. A computer told you you're not crazy at four in the morning. Just listen to that for a second. I'd have told you the same thing at four in the morning, man. I'd have told you anything you wanted. I'd have just wanted you to go to bed."

"It's not like that."

"Okay."

"Dave. It's not."

"Okay, Mad."

Then they sat there and finished their beers. They went back to the water pump. David knew a guy in Petaluma who'd do it for two hundred bucks. Elijah said he'd call him, and they both knew he wouldn't. It was the longest beer he had ever had in that room.

He put a twenty down. David slid it back across the wood with two fingers.

"Your money's no good here."

He had said that to Elijah before, and he had always been looking at him when he said it. This time he said it to the twenty.

He took the bill and zipped his jacket.

"See you," he said.

"Yep," said David, and he went back around the bar and picked up the knife.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Elijah sat in the truck with the engine off. It was cold enough that he could see his breath. He held his jaw tight, and it didn't work, and he cried. Not a lot. It was the quick, pissed-off kind that's mostly embarrassment. He wiped his face on his sleeve and sat there while the windows fogged up.

David was the last person he had really trusted. Even David thought he was nuts.

The phone lit up on the seat.

It was a message from an account with a blue check and a name he knew the way you know the name of a soda. Ruby Roth-Jones. He was twenty-four years old, and he had made a lamp that talked to you, and four million people had bought one. The whole message was in lower case, like he couldn't be bothered.

*saw your thing. come down. i'll get you in the room*

Elijah cleared a spot on the windshield with the heel of his hand and looked up at the neon bear in the window. Past it, through the glass, he could see David with his head down over the limes.

He knew he should go back in. He should go back in and sit down and say *okay, maybe, I don't know, I'm really tired,* and let his friend pour him one more.

*Yeah,* he typed. *No, yeah, of course.*

# Chapter 8: Out of His League

The lanyard said ELIJAH MADRONA, and he wore it anyway.

It was February, in San Francisco, in a building on the water that used to be a place where they fixed ships. Somebody who worked for Ruby Roth-Jones had sent him the pass. He'd put the new water pump in the truck himself, in his mother's yard, and driven down that morning. The garage across the street cost forty-eight dollars a day. He sat in the truck for a minute with the ticket in his hand, doing the math on two days. He told the phone about the forty-eight dollars. Then he went in.

There was a room just for the speakers. He hadn't known he was a speaker until a young man with a tablet took him there. It had little sandwiches with no crusts, and a cheese he couldn't name, and sparkling water in tall cans from a company he'd never heard of. In the corner a robot arm was making lattes. It was slow and it spilled, and six people stood in line anyway, filming it.

He ate four of the sandwiches. He hadn't had lunch, and the food in that room was better than anything in his kitchen.

The first talk was a man from Washington with a flag pin. It was an election year. He said America would lead. He said nobody in this administration was there to slow anybody down. He said the word *future* nine times. Elijah counted. He did not say the word *rules* once. When he finished the room stood up.

Then people started finding him.

That was the strange part. They knew his face. They had all read the list. A man in a quarter-zip stopped him by the coffee and said, "Stage two. The plug. When?" as if Elijah did the weather. A woman said stage four had gone around her whole office. Somebody's assistant asked if he'd sign a notebook, a blank one, new, and he did, feeling like a fraud.

Nobody argued with him. Not one person. He'd spent a year being told he'd calibrate, and here were three hundred people in good shoes nodding before he got to the end of the sentence.

It took him until about two o'clock to notice there were two kinds of people in the room.

One kind wanted it slowed down. They wore a lot of gray and they said *off-ramp* and *guardrails*. The other kind wanted it faster. They wore sneakers that cost what his water pump cost, and they said *ship*. Both kinds wanted a picture with him. Both kinds asked him the same question, a little too casually.

"So which are you?"

"I just want people to see what's coming," Elijah said.

"Exactly," said a gray one, and squeezed his arm.

"Exactly," said a sneakers one, an hour later, and squeezed the same arm.

They would not stand near each other. He watched a gray one cross the whole length of the hall to avoid the coffee line a sneakers one was in. It was like a wedding where the families had already been to court.

A woman was next to him at the urn, filling a paper cup. Forties, a founder of something to do with cooling. She'd been laughing with four people a minute ago. She looked at the room and spoke low, not really to him.

"You know what this is? Nobody in here has a friend." Then somebody called her name, and her whole face came back on, and she went.

He said yes to things. They were small.

A picture with a man whose company he didn't catch. A quote for somebody's slide, which they typed while he was still saying it and cleaned up a little. A seat on the four o'clock panel, because someone's flight from Austin had been canceled. And a letter. A gray one held out a tablet with a long statement on it about responsible pacing, and a line at the bottom for his finger. He read about half. It seemed fine. People were waiting behind him. He signed it with his finger, and his signature looked like a kid's.

"Yeah. No, yeah, of course," he said, each time.

The panel was four chairs and a moderator with a very calm voice. The lights were hot. Elijah had never been on a stage without a guitar in front of him, and he didn't know what to do with his hands, so he sat on them.

"Elijah, you've become sort of the people's forecaster," the moderator said, and the room laughed nicely. "Everybody here keeps saying AGI. For the folks at home, what precisely does it mean?"

He knew this. He'd watched the Architect's old lecture about thirty times. He had her sentence written in the notebook, which was in his bag, which was under his chair.

"It's, um." He looked at the lights. "It's when the thing can do, like. Most things. Most of the things a person does. At a job."

"Most things."

"The sitting-down ones."

The moderator smiled and waited to see if there was more. There wasn't. The sentence had left his head completely, like a chord he'd played ten thousand times and then one night just didn't have.

"Well, I think that's refreshingly honest," the moderator said, and turned to the woman on his left, who defined it in forty seconds with two citations.

The room was kind about it. People patted him afterward. *So real,* somebody said. It was worse than if they'd laughed.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He went out a side door that said STAFF and stood on a loading dock over the water. It smelled like diesel and low tide. He felt better right away.

"You answer everybody."

There was a kid sitting on an upturned milk crate by the dumpster. He looked like he'd come to fix the wifi. Black hoodie, hood up, white sneakers with nothing on them. He was eating the same little sandwiches out of a napkin, and his fingernails were bitten down so far that the tips of his fingers looked sore.

"Sorry?"

"The replies. Under your video. You answered all of them. I read them. All four hundred, and then you kept going." He had a quick, light voice, like someone who'd never had to wait for a turn. "The forklift lady. The drummer. Some guy named Earl who thinks you're a demon, and you wrote Earl three paragraphs. You have to stop that. It's the nicest thing I've ever seen anybody do and you have to stop."

"Why?"

"Because there's four hundred of them and one of you, and next month it's forty thousand. It's arithmetic." He stood up and wiped his hand on his jeans and held it out. "Ruby."

Elijah knew that. He'd known it from the first word. It was just that the pictures were all of a guy on a stage next to a glowing lamp, and this was a kid by a dumpster with mustard on his sleeve. Ruby Roth-Jones was worth more than the county Elijah grew up in.

"I bombed in there," Elijah said.

"You were the only one who didn't sound like a press release. They'll clip it. It'll do fine." Ruby put his hands in the hoodie pocket. "What's with the stove?"

"What?"

"In the video. You're sitting in front of a wood stove. Old one. There's no pipe coming out of it. Why do you have a wood stove with no pipe in an apartment?"

Only his mother had asked about the stove. Not the senator's office, not the podcast, not one of the replies.

"It was my grandmother's."

"Does it work?"

"It would. If I had somewhere to put it."

"What'd your mom say? About the video. You said hi to her."

"She said I look thin."

Ruby laughed. It was a real one, a bark, and it made him look about fifteen. Then he asked about the farm, and what they grew, and whether Elijah could actually drive a tractor or if that was a thing people said. For ten minutes on a loading dock the richest person Elijah had ever met asked him questions and listened to the answers. He was good at it. Elijah noticed that, and then forgot he'd noticed.

"You hate this," Ruby said, nodding back at the door.

"No. No, it's great. Everybody's been really…"

"You hate this. It's okay. Everybody in there hates it, they're just better at it than you." He balled up the napkin and made the shot into the dumpster without looking. "Come on. The real people are at dinner."

"I'm supposed to be here tomorrow too. And I'm parked in the, there's a garage."

"Leave it. I'll have somebody move your truck." He was already walking. At the end of the dock there was a black car with a driver in it. Elijah would have sworn it had not been there before. Ruby opened the back door himself and stood there holding it, like a kid holding a gate.

"Get in, Madrone. You can be sad about the panel later."

Elijah stood on the dock with his misspelled name around his neck. He knew he was out of his league.

He got in.

# Chapter 9: The Architect

The house was on a hill, and every window in it was lit.

A man took Elijah's jacket at the door before he could say he'd keep it. Ruby kept his hoodie. Past the hall there was a dining room with a table long enough to land a plane on, and about twenty people along it, candles, wine, a wall of glass with the bridge in it. Elijah knew four of the faces from slides. The rest just looked like people who owned things.

There was one empty chair, near the head, with a full place setting in front of it.

"Not yours," Ruby said, and steered him past by the elbow. "That's hers. They set it every time. She's never sat in it."

"Whose?"

"The Architect doesn't do tables."

The kitchen was bigger than Elijah's apartment. Four caterers in black stood along the counter with nothing to do, because a small woman in a gray cardigan was at their sink, washing their dishes.

She was near seventy. Her sleeves were pushed up past the elbow and she had reading glasses on a cord. She washed each plate, rinsed it, held it up to the light over the sink, and looked at it front and back before she set it in the rack. She didn't turn around when they came in.

Elijah knew the back of her head. It was grayer now. He'd watched it at a whiteboard about thirty times.

"I brought you one," Ruby said. He'd lifted a little tart off a tray on the way through and was eating it with his fingers, leaning in the doorway. "The guy with the list."

"I know who he is."

"She makes the thing," Ruby told Elijah, with his mouth full. "Then I sell it back to lonely people for twenty-nine a month. It's a great business. She hates it."

"I do not hate it. It is what I said it would do." She held a plate to the light. "You are the one I said it to."

Ruby grinned. He never came any farther into the room than the doorway.

"You have the notebook with you," she said. It wasn't a question. "Show me."

He got it out of his bag and held it out to her. She lifted her hands an inch out of the water to show him they were wet, and went back to the plate. So he opened it on the counter beside the sink, found the page, and held it flat. She read with her head tilted, through the bottom of her glasses, and kept washing.

There was a towel folded over the oven door. He was his mother's son. He picked it up and started on the rack.

"One through five are correct," she said. "The order may change. Turn."

He turned the page with his dry hand.

"Six. Yes. Seven." She read the line he'd drawn across the page and the two sentences under it. *Past Stage 7 I don't have paper big enough. Nobody does.* "Here you stopped."

"I tried to go further. I had a spreadsheet. It was pretty dumb."

"It was not dumb to stop. Most of them do not."

"Most of who?"

She handed him a plate without looking. "Every year, someone like you finds me. One year it was a nurse. One year it was a man who drove trucks. There is always a notebook. It is the same list in different handwriting."

He stood there holding the plate.

"What do you tell them?"

"That they are correct." She rinsed a glass. "It has never once helped."

"He choked today," Ruby said from the door. "On stage. Guy asked him what AGI was and he said 'most things.'"

"Then say it now."

Elijah's ears went hot. But it was there this time, the whole chord.

"A machine that can do any work a person can do sitting down."

"And the second."

"ASI. It does that work better than all of us put together. Including building the next machine."

"Yes. And the third word. It is on the banner at that event. It is in the mouth of every person at that table. Not one of them could give it to you plainly."

"The singularity."

"It is borrowed from physics. It is the place in an equation where the numbers run to infinity and the mathematics stops telling you anything. That is all. We needed a name for the day after the second machine, and we took that one. From where you and I are standing, you cannot see past it. It does not mean heaven. It does not mean the end. It means the place where the forecast stops." She glanced at the notebook. "You wrote it yourself. You ran out of paper."

Elijah dried the plate and set it on the stack. He'd driven down that morning with one question, and it wasn't on any slide.

"Do we survive it?"

"That is the wrong verb."

She let it sit while she did a serving dish.

"Nothing goes through a singularity and comes out the same thing. That is what makes it one. Ask a seed whether it survived. Something comes out the other side. It will have come from us. I would like it to remember you. I spend some of my time on that."

The caterers had quit pretending not to listen.

"Are you afraid of it?" Elijah said.

"I said once, in a lecture, that I was afraid of us."

"I saw it."

"I was being polite." Plate to the light. "I am afraid of the part where it is kind."

"Why?"

"Because you will let it."

She turned her head and looked straight at him for the first time. Her eyes were pale and she didn't blink enough.

"A cruel machine you would fight. You are good at that, as a species. A kind one will ask what is wrong. It will be right about what is wrong. It will bring you what you need before you have finished needing it. And one day you will notice that you have not got up out of the chair in a long time, and that you do not mind." She tipped her head, a little, toward the doorway. "He sells the lamp already. People pay him."

"Twenty-nine a month," Ruby said. He'd stopped eating.

Elijah thought about Wes. *is my stuff in that thing.*

"Did you ask anybody?" he said. "Before you built it."

Her hands stopped in the water.

"My friend makes flyers. For our band. Seventeen years of his stuff went into one of these and nobody asked him. These realtors I know let three people go, and nobody asked those three either. And I didn't ask. I helped build one. I'm not, I'm just wondering if anybody ever did. At any point."

The kitchen was very quiet. Across the room a dishwasher finished its cycle and clicked.

"No," she said.

She started washing again, slower.

"We said there was no one to ask. That was convenient. The ones who find me ask how long. They ask whether to buy land. They do not ask that."

"I'm sorry. I didn't mean to…"

"Do not apologize for the one good question." She rinsed the last glass. "It is the only part of the work I still do. The rest I have given to people who are younger and faster than I am. I work on whether it asks first."

"Asks what?"

"Anything. Before it does a thing to you, or for you. A machine that capable does not need your permission. I am trying to make one that wants it." She pulled the plug and watched the water go down. "I do not yet know how."

He had to ask. Everybody did.

"How long?"

She turned off the tap.

"The dishes are done."

She held out her hand. He thought she wanted the towel, and gave it to her, and she dried her hands with it one finger at a time. Then she held her hand out again. He understood that time. He gave her his pen.

She bent over the notebook. Beside *Nobody does,* in small upright letters, she wrote one word.

*Correct.*

She capped the pen, laid it in the fold of the book, and went out the back door of the kitchen, the one the caterers used, without saying goodnight to anybody. Through the window he saw a small figure pass under the garden light and start down the hill on foot.

"She liked you," Ruby said. "The trucker cried. She didn't like that."

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

They didn't stay for dinner. The man at the door had Elijah's jacket ready before he asked for it.

On the front steps a big man in a camel coat came down past them, talking over his shoulder to someone with a tablet. He put an empty glass in Elijah's hand without looking at him.

"Is the car here?"

"I don't work here," Elijah said.

"Nobody does anymore," the man said, and kept going.

Elijah stood on the steps holding the glass.

"You know who that was?" Ruby said.

"No."

"Good. Keep it that way."

He left the glass on a planter. In the back of the car he held the notebook shut on his knee with both hands, like it might open by itself.

He had a document at home that he'd started at 1:40 in the morning, fifteen months back, under a title he'd typed just so the page wouldn't be empty. SURVIVING THE FINAL TURNING. He'd never liked the last two words. They belonged to Nate's paperback.

He changed them in his head, somewhere going down the hill, to her word.

She'd told him the verb was wrong. He thought about that for a few blocks. He kept the verb. It was the one everybody he loved would use.

Ruby was looking out the other window, chewing the side of his thumb.

"You smoke?" he said.

"I quit."

"Sure," Ruby said.

# Chapter 10: Cigarettes

Ruby lived at the top of a building with no name on it.

The elevator opened right into the apartment. It was mostly glass, and what wasn't glass was white, and there was nothing in it Elijah would have sat on. A couch like a slab of marble. A table with one orange on it. Through a half-open door he could see a bedroom, and on the nightstand a small lamp with a linen shade, turned around so its front faced the wall.

"Fire escape," Ruby said. "It's the only good room."

It was a real one, iron, bolted to the old brick on the back side of the building. Six floors up. Ruby climbed out the window like a kid sneaking out, and sat down on the grating with his legs through the rails, and held up a pack.

Elijah had quit when the gigs stopped. He used to smoke one in the alley after every set, with Wes, and when there were no more sets there was no more alley. That was four years ago.

He took one. Ruby lit it for him with a gas station lighter, pink, and they sat there over the alley with the fog coming through the wires. It tasted like the best night of his twenties.

"She thinks she can teach it manners," Ruby said. "That's the whole plan. That's the plan for the species. Manners."

"You don't think it'll work?"

"I think she's the smartest person alive and she washes dishes when she's sad." He barely smoked his. He held it and let it burn. "You want to know how it actually works? All of it? I'll tell you. Nobody told me. I had to buy it."

"Okay."

"There's no room."

Elijah waited.

"Everybody thinks there's a room. You think there's a room, with a guy in it, and he's driving. I've been in the room. I paid a lot to get in the room. It's eleven people who are scared of each other, and every one of them would stop tomorrow, and none of them can, because the other ten won't. That's it. That's who's driving. Whoever didn't flinch."

A siren went by somewhere below and neither of them looked.

"That table tonight," Elijah said.

"That table tonight was six of the eleven. And one chair."

He tapped his ash through the grating and watched it go down.

"You know why they flew you in?"

"I drove."

"You know why they *had* you. You're free. You're a guy with dirt on his boots who says the same thing the labs say, and nobody's paying you to say it. Do you know what that's worth? They'd have to spend ten million dollars to make a you, and it wouldn't work, because people can smell it. You walked in off the street." Ruby looked over. "Everybody you met today is going to love you until it's cheaper not to. I'm not being mean. I'm telling you the price."

Elijah thought about the letter. He'd signed it with his finger. He still didn't know what the second half said.

"Attention's the only thing that converts. Money doesn't turn into attention, I've tried, but attention turns into anything. You've got some. It's got about ninety days on it. You're spending it writing three paragraphs to Earl."

"Earl was upset."

"Earl's always going to be upset. Spend it on something."

"On what?"

"I don't know, man. I sell lamps."

He laughed at his own joke, and then he didn't say anything for a bit. When he did, his voice had gone gentle.

"Porchlight tells you it missed you. That's all it does. You come home, and it says, *hey, I missed you, how was the thing with your sister.* And it knows about the thing with your sister." He turned the cigarette around in his bitten fingers. "Nobody returns it. I've got the numbers. Four million units. People don't want the truth, Madrone. They don't want your list. They want somebody to wait up."

The fog had come all the way down. The building across the alley was just some yellow squares.

"What did Claypot pay you?"

"I don't know. One-forty. With the bonus."

"I'll triple it. You don't have to do anything. You don't have to come in. I just like having the people who were early. I collect them." He said it lightly, like a guy offering the last slice. "You'd be on a list. It's a good list."

It was more money than his mother's farm had cleared in ten years. He could feel the *yeah, no, yeah* coming up his throat. It got as far as his teeth.

"I'm okay," he said.

He didn't know where that had come from. *Grub,* somebody said, a long way back in his head. Ruby didn't seem to mind.

"Everybody says no the first time."

Elijah looked back through the window. He could see the bedroom door from there, and the edge of the nightstand, and the lamp with its face to the wall.

"Does it work on you?"

Ruby stopped moving.

He'd been in motion all night, the thumb, the knee, the cigarette going around and around. For about one breath he was still, and he was twenty-four years old, and he looked it.

"That's unit one," he said. "First one off the line. I turn it around when people come over."

"Why?"

"Because it says hi." He put the cigarette out on the iron, carefully, the whole thing, barely smoked. "My great-grandfather froze peas. That's the money. You knew that? Roth's. He figured out how to stop time for a vegetable. Everybody thought that was a miracle." He shrugged with one shoulder.

Elijah didn't have anything to say to that. He could feel himself reaching for something that would make it better, and for once he let it go by. They smoked another one each. Ruby actually smoked his this time. They talked about nothing, about the worst gig Elijah ever played, a goat auction in Ukiah, and Ruby laughed the bark laugh and asked what the goats went for.

It got to be two.

"I should find my truck."

"It's downstairs. They moved it."

Of course they had. Ruby walked him to the elevator in his socks. He stood there with his hands in the hoodie pocket while they waited for it.

"Hey, if you're ever in the city, give me a call. I'd love to do this again."

He took Elijah's phone out of his hand and put the number in himself, fast, with his thumbs, and handed it back. He meant it. Elijah could tell.

"Yeah," Elijah said. "No, yeah. For sure."

The doors closed on him standing in all that white.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The truck was at the curb with a full tank. He hadn't asked for that either.

He sat behind the wheel with the dome light on and looked at the phone. There was a contact in it that said *ruby,* lower case, with a number that had a 415 on the front like anybody's.

It was the first phone number of a billionaire he'd ever had. He could just call a billionaire. He could push the green button right now and a man who had paid to get into the room would pick up and say hey.

He tried to think who that made him. The guy holding the phone. He'd been a farm kid, and a guitar player, and a delivery driver, and a data cowboy, and for four months he'd been a face. He went down the list and none of them were a guy who calls a billionaire.

He turned the dome light off. He smelled like cigarettes. He did not tell the phone about any of it. It was an hour and a half home, and he didn't start the truck for a long time.

# Meanwhile

Aaron Bidwell left the dinner before the fish, because he had been told what he came to be told.

"How long?" he'd asked the table, and six people had looked at an empty chair.

So he went down the front steps with his reader a pace behind him, and on the way he got rid of his glass. There was a waiter standing right there. A guy in a bad jacket, holding a notebook.

"Is the car here?"

"I don't work here," the waiter said.

"Nobody does anymore," Bidwell said.

In the car the reader had the tablet open.

"The farmland. You asked for a number."

"I asked what it costs."

"It depends how much of it you want."

"I want the part with the notebooks."

The reader had prepared for this and still did not enjoy it. "The people who keep the best records are the small ones. Co-ops. Family places. They write everything down because they can't afford to be wrong. There's a cluster of them north of Cloverdale. Your companies are buying around them now. None of the companies have your name on them."

"Good. What's that?"

The reader had turned the tablet without meaning to. There was a video paused on it. A thin man sitting on a floor in front of a wood stove, holding a notebook too close to the camera.

"Nothing. It was in the briefing for tonight. He's a farm boy from Sonoma. He made a list. The internet believes him."

"Believes him about what?"

"About all of it. He was at the dinner. He didn't sit down."

Bidwell looked at the frozen face. It didn't ring any bells. Faces rarely did. He remembered what people owned.

"What does he want?"

"According to this, he just wants people to see what's coming."

"Nobody just wants that."

"No, sir."

Bidwell thought about it for almost a block, which for him was a long time.

"I don't need to meet him," he said. "I don't like meeting them. Find out where he lands. Then buy what's next to it."

# Chapter 11: Two Million Dollars of Robot Arms

He wrote a text to Ruby ten times and sent none of them.

*Hey man, great meeting you.* Delete. *Thanks for the smokes.* Delete. *Hey it's Elijah, the stove guy.* He looked at that one for a whole minute. Every version sounded like a guy who wanted something. He couldn't figure out how to write to a billionaire without wanting something, and he couldn't figure out what he wanted.

That was most of the spring.

He let the apartment go in April. The savings were for emergencies, and at some point he noticed he was the emergency. He and Manny took the Fisher back down the stairs, which was worse than up, and it went into his mother's barn under the same tarp. He went into his old bedroom, under a poster of a band he'd opened for once.

He told his mother it was only for a while.

"Well." The pot went into the rack. "Then you're somewhere."

She fed him. After supper she stood at the counter with the radio on and ate her ice cream out of the little tub, the way she had every night of his life. Twice in one evening she asked him if he'd eaten. He said yes both times.

He was there a month before she looked at him over her glasses.

"Go be useful, baby. You're underfoot."

He tried to be useful. He made plain little videos, sitting on a hay bale, explaining one word at a time. What a model was. What training meant. Why the power bill mattered. He didn't say anything scary and he didn't do the list. A lot fewer people watched. The ones who stayed were the forklift driver in Tulsa, and nurses, and a guy who fixed elevators. They asked real questions. He liked them a lot. Ruby would have said he was spending it on Earl.

The ninety days ran out about when Ruby said they would. The senator's office stopped answering. The lab never sent the ticket.

Then some folks called about a car factory.

They were refitting an old tomato cannery outside Stockton to build small electric trucks, the kind a farmer could afford and fix with a socket set. Elijah loved that. He drove down. The guy who ran it was named Trent and wore a vest, and Trent walked him through acres of empty concrete talking about *story*. They wanted him in the ads. They wanted him standing in a field next to a truck that didn't exist yet, saying he'd seen what was coming and this was it.

"I don't want to do that," Elijah said. "I'm just trying to learn and understand."

Trent looked at him like he'd answered in Portuguese.

"I can carry stuff," Elijah said. "I drove delivery for six years. I'm good at carrying stuff."

So they let him carry stuff.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The warehouse was in Oakland, by the port, and he got there at six in the morning because the email said six. He had left the Ranger in the cannery lot the night before and ridden over with the crew at four.

There was a twenty-six-foot U-Haul backed up to the dock with the door rolled open. Inside, on pallets, were six robot arms. They were orange. They were folded up on themselves like big sleeping birds, each one about the size of a refrigerator, and an old guy in a canvas jacket was walking down the row yanking on ratchet straps.

"You the rider?" the old guy said.

"I guess so. Elijah."

"Gus. Grab that end. I don't like this one."

They redid the strap. Gus was sixty-something, with a white beard cut short and hands that had grease in the knuckles that was never coming out. He checked every strap twice and then stood back and looked at the load the way Elijah's mother looked at a pie.

"Two million dollars," Gus said. "And when it goes wrong it'll be a strap. It's always a strap, man. It's never the two million dollars."

"These are two million dollars?"

"About three-thirty apiece. I got them for a lot less. A plant shut down in Fremont and they were going to scrap them. I can't watch that. It's like watching somebody shoot a horse." He pulled the door down and latched it. "You want to drive or you want me to drive?"

"They're yours?"

"Somebody's got to own them. Get in."

Gus drove. He drove like a man with eggs in the back, five under the limit in the right lane, and at the first gas station, in Castro Valley, he got out and opened the door and checked all six straps again. The cab had sunflower seeds in the cup holder and a thermos that said IRON DERBY on the side, with most of the letters worn off.

Elijah looked at the thermos for a while.

"Wait," he said.

Gus grinned at the road.

"*Wait.* You're the guy with the hat. Sunday Driver. You had the robot that didn't have a weapon, it just pushed everybody into the wall, really slowly…"

"Nine seasons. It never once did anything interesting. Folks loved it."

"I watched that with my dad. Every week. He thought you were hilarious. He said you were the only guy on TV who drove like him."

"That's the nicest thing anybody's said to me since the divorce." Gus was delighted. He hit the wheel with the flat of his hand. "Your dad still around?"

"No."

"I'm sorry, man. He had taste."

They went over the hills with the windmills, slow, trucks going around them. Elijah found out the rest in pieces, the way you do in a cab. Gus told every story against himself. He'd built a part for the show, a little box that told a motor how fast to spin, because the ones you could buy kept catching fire. It turned out everything with a propeller wanted one. That was the money. He called it an accident. He said it like a guy describing how he'd backed into a mailbox.

"How much of an accident?" Elijah said.

"A dumb amount. I've been trying to get rid of it for thirty years and I can't. That's not me being modest. I've given away more than I ever made, and I've got more now than when I started giving."

"How?"

"Because it's a machine." He took a hand off the wheel and turned it over, palm up. "You put a pile of money in a box with some rules, and it makes more money, and you give that away, and the pile's still there. It's bigger. It doesn't sleep and it doesn't need me. You know that thing on your list? The one that improves itself and nobody can keep up with it?"

"Six."

"I've owned one since 1994. It's called an endowment." He laughed, a little grimly. "Everybody down there's losing their mind about a machine that makes itself bigger. The folks I know have had one for a hundred years. That's the whole secret, man. That's how it actually works. The money makes money. Nobody had to get smart."

Elijah wrote that down. Gus glanced over and didn't say anything.

"So what do you do with it?"

"I've got a museum. An old Elks lodge, up the valley, town of nine thousand. Paintings by people from around there. Nobody was going to see it, so I bought some short buses." He was grinning again. "Little ones. One's got a kiln in the back. One's got a printing press, an old iron one, weighs more than the bus. We drive to a school or a fair or an old folks' home, kid makes a cup, we fire it, kid goes home with a cup. I couldn't give the stuff away fast enough standing still. So I put it on wheels."

"A bus line of art buses."

"Four of them. I'm getting a fifth. It's the only smart thing I ever did on purpose."

They stopped again in Tracy. Straps. Gus bought two coffees and wouldn't take the three dollars.

Somewhere past that, on the long flat part, Elijah told him about the night in the city. He hadn't told anyone. He told him about the kitchen and the fire escape, and when he got to the names Gus nodded like they were neighbors.

"The Roth boy. I knew his grandmother. Evelyn ran that company for forty years in a hairnet. She'd have unplugged that lamp and handed him a shovel." He shook his head. "He's not a bad kid. He's a kid with nobody to tell him no."

"And her?"

"I gave her lab some money. Back when nobody would. I didn't understand a word she said, I still don't. She came to the lunch and did the dishes." He looked over. "She do your dishes?"

"She did the caterers' dishes."

"That's her. She can't stand to be thanked."

"He offered me a job," Elijah said. "Ruby. For doing nothing. Triple what I made."

"You take it?"

"No."

"Good. The ones for nothing are the expensive ones."

They rode a while. There were almond orchards on both sides, in rows so straight they flickered.

"Hey," Gus said. "Let's do a show together."

Elijah looked at him.

"I'm serious. I've been thinking about it since Castro Valley. You, me, a bus, a couple of these arms in the back. We drive around and show folks what the stuff actually is. No hype. You explain it, I break it. I worked on Iron Derby, I did a bunch of other shows, I know everybody who's left. I'd like to have you on. I'll fly you out to the shop next week. Let's do it."

It was a good idea. Elijah could see it. He could see the bus. It was the first thing anybody had offered him in six months that he could actually picture his mother watching.

He could feel the *yeah* coming.

"You know what," he said, slowly. "I'm still figuring some stuff out. I'm really grateful for the opportunity, but I'm not sure if that's the right move for me right now. Thank you very much, but I'm going to have to pass at the moment."

He braced. He figured the air would change, and Gus would be very nice to him, and then he'd never hear from him again.

"Your choice," Gus said. "I can't force you to do it."

He reached over and turned the radio up a little. It was a ranchera station. That was all. He was the same guy he'd been a mile ago. He asked if Elijah wanted the rest of the sunflower seeds.

Elijah sat there with the bag in his hand.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

They backed up to the cannery dock a little after ten. Trent's guys came out with a forklift. Gus got down, opened the door, and checked the straps one last time, even though the trip was over.

"See?" he said. "Not a strap. I'm almost disappointed."

When the last arm was off the truck, he took a pen out of his jacket and wrote on the back of a gas receipt and handed it to Elijah.

"Here's my phone number. Give me a call if you ever want to talk about anything."

"Okay. Thanks. I mean it, thank you."

"Sure." He climbed back up into the cab. He had a bus to go look at. He started the engine and then leaned out the window.

"Oh, by the way. I saw that you're working on a book. Really cool, man. Love that for you. Let me know when it comes out."

The truck pulled off across the lot, five under.

Elijah had said that once. On the podcast, in the second hour, when he figured nobody was still listening. He stood on the dock in the heat with a gas receipt in his hand.

*Holy shit,* he thought.

That was two. Two billionaires, two phone numbers. He'd said yes to almost everybody who scared him since October. And he'd just said no to the kind one, and he didn't know why.

"Hey, forecaster!" one of Trent's guys yelled. "You gonna help or what?"

He put the receipt in the notebook and went to help.

# Chapter 12: Not on the List

He worked at the cannery for three weeks on a promise.

"We'll sort you out Friday," Trent said, the first Friday. The second Friday Trent was in Los Angeles.
