Elijah bolted orange arms to a concrete floor with a crew of six guys who were getting paid, and at night he slept in the cab of the Ranger at the far end of the cannery lot, with the windows cracked and a towel over the wheel. A shower at the truck stop on the highway was seventeen dollars. He took one every other day, and when the money got low he stopped.

He had started smoking again in February, on a fire escape. By August it was spliffs. A guy on the crew sold him a bag the first week, and at night he rolled one on the dash and smoked it down so he could sleep.

He didn't mind the work. He liked it more than anything he'd done in a year. You put a bolt in and it stayed in.

The third Friday was the launch party.

They'd lit the arms purple. He could see them through the big roll-up door, all six, moving together to music like a row of slow dancers, and a couple hundred people in good shoes standing around with drinks. There was a kid at the door with a tablet.

"Madrone," Elijah said. "Elijah."

The kid scrolled. He scrolled back up. He was nice about it.

"I'm really sorry. Is it maybe under a company?"

"I installed those."

"I believe you, man. I just can't. I'm sorry."

Past the kid's shoulder a caterer went by with a tray of little sandwiches with the crusts cut off.

He walked back across the lot to the truck. He'd felt off since noon, and by the time he got the door shut he was shaking. It had been a hundred and one in Stockton that day, and he was cold. He lay across the bench seat with his knees up and his jacket over him and listened to the bass come across the asphalt.

Around ten he got the phone out. There were people in that building who had squeezed his arm in February. He had their numbers. He picked four and sent each of them the same thing.

*Hey, it's Elijah. I'm out in the lot, pretty sick. Could use a hand if you've got a sec.*

He watched the screen. One of them, the woman with the cooling company, sent back a heart.

That was all that came. He waited an hour to make sure.

There were two other numbers he could try. He looked at them. *ruby,* lower case. And a contact he'd typed in himself off a gas receipt, which just said *Gus.*

He put his thumb over the first one and thought about what he'd say. *Hey, remember me, I'm in a parking lot.* He thought about a kid in a white apartment reading that, and what the kid would think, and knew exactly. One more person who wanted something.

Then the second one. Gus would come. That was the thing. He was pretty sure Gus would drive to Stockton at five under the limit. And Elijah had told that man no three weeks ago. He couldn't call him up now and ask.

He didn't call his mother. She'd have gotten in the car at eleven at night, and he wasn't going to do that to her.

He turned the phone face down on the dash.

He wasn't angry. He was too sick to be angry. He'd spent ten months saying yes to people. If he died in this truck tonight, the party would go until one. Somebody would notice the Ranger on Monday. They just didn't have a place to put him.

# Chapter 13: Two in the Afternoon

In the morning the fever was down enough to drive. He did not go in to get sorted out.

He left the lot with the windows down and the heater on, because he couldn't get warm. He had a quarter tank and no money. Home was two hours north. He got as far as a side street downtown and turned the truck off.

He could not have told you why. He wasn't going to walk into her kitchen looking like this. She had told the ladies at church about the senator.

On Sunday at 9:40 the phone rang on the dash. He watched it until it stopped. Then he typed *Working late. All good. Love you*, and sent it, and turned the phone off.

He turned it back on Tuesday, to look for work.

There was one voicemail, from Sunday, 9:41. He would listen to it later. He would listen to it a great many times.

At two in the afternoon the phone rang in his hand, and it was her number. She never called at two in the afternoon.

"Ma?"

"It's Manny." The radio was on behind him. "I'm at the house, Elijah. I came by about the fence. She's alive. They took her to Santa Rosa."

He didn't remember the drive. He remembered the gauge.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

She was in a bed by a window with the rail up.

Her right hand lay on the blanket where somebody had put it. Her mouth went down on that side. Her eyes found him when he came in, and stayed on him, and he saw her know who he was. That was the last easy thing that happened that week.

"Ma."

She worked at it. He watched her go and get the word, like a woman reaching for something on a high shelf.

"Barry," she said.

"It's Elijah, Ma."

She looked at him as if he had said something a little beside the point.

"She's been saying that since they brought her up." A nurse was hanging a bag. "Is that your dad?"

"My dad was Joe."

"Brother?"

"There isn't any Barry."

The nurse nodded. It was an answer she got a lot.

The doctor was young and stood at the foot of the bed with a tablet. He did not sit down. A clot, a big one, on the left side. She had probably been on the kitchen floor since Monday night. There had been small ones before this, he thought, going back months. You could see them on the picture like old hail damage.

"Had she been forgetting things?"

"Yeah," Elijah said. "Since spring."

"That would be those." He scrolled. "Her pressure's been up a long time. Her sugar. This didn't start Monday. What does she eat?"

Elijah started to say she ate fine. She cooked for everybody.

"Ice cream," he said.

The doctor waited.

"Every night. One of the little tubs."

The doctor wrote it down. He had the next room to get to, and it showed.

He asked what she would get back. The doctor said a good many things, carefully, and the answer was in what he left out.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The pot was on the stove.

It was not in the rack. It was on the back burner with a wooden spoon in it and something burned to the bottom. Manny had smelled it from the yard. All his life Elijah had heard that pot go into that rack.

He stood in her kitchen and looked. There were three cartons of milk in the refrigerator and two of them were open. Her reading glasses were in the bread drawer. His phone number was on the refrigerator door in her handwriting, on a yellow note, and on a second yellow note right under it.

The freezer was the one thing in order. It was all Ben and Jerry's, the little tubs, a week of them, labels out. When he was small she let him have the lid.

That spring, in his old bedroom, he had heard the same question twice in one evening and let it go. Once she called him Joe, and laughed, and said *listen to me*. She was sweet about it. She was sweet about everything. He had seen it, and he had gone to Stockton to bolt down robot arms.

She was a woman who wrote the date on a battery before it went in the flashlight, and there was a flashlight in every room. Her mail was in the drawer with the rubber bands. It went back to March. Most of it had never been opened. The bank's name was in the corner window of about every third envelope. One had been opened, and folded up again, and put back in.

The last notice wasn't in the drawer. It was taped to the front door.

Nobody in that house had used the front door since his father's funeral. They used the back. Everybody did. The paper had been out there eighteen days with the sale date on it, facing the road.

He went out to the barn to look at the stove, because it was the one thing on the place that was his.

The barn was full.

There were pallets where the hay had been. White buckets, stacked five high, with the lids taped. Rice. Beans. Wheat she had no way to grind. Four blue water barrels, forty yards from a well. A generator in its box, and next to it the same generator in its box.

He stood there with his hand on a bucket. She had believed him. One person had, and it was her.

There was a card table by the door with a spiral notebook on it, squared to the corner. At the top of the first page she had written herself a note.

*Elijah is worried about the computers. Be nice about it.*

Under that she had copied out his list, one through five, in her good handwriting, the way she copied a recipe off the radio. Next to number four there was a question mark. *What deal? Ask him.*

It was on the next page too. And the next. *Ask him.*

She never had. *It's just tools, baby.* She had not followed one word of it. She knew her boy was frightened of something, and she knew what you did about a worry. The farm had taught her. You put something by.

The slips were in a coffee can. *Thank you for your monthly order.* The first was dated the week of the video. The last was three weeks old. Somebody had kept bringing it and she had kept signing, and that was where the payment on the place had gone since spring.

The Fisher was in the back, under its tarp, behind the wheat.

He went in and sat down at the table. There was nobody to call. His father was ten years gone. There was Melody, in Miami, his father's daughter from before he met Elijah's mother. He had not heard her voice since the funeral, and he did not want to hear it now. He went down it twice, to be sure.

He was the call.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

A woman from the hospital with a lanyard and a folder took him into a room with a box of tissues on the table. She was good at it. She had done it four times that day.

His mother could not go home. She would need two people to lift her, and somebody awake at night. The hospital would keep her until Friday. After that there was a stretch of rehab the insurance would pay for, and after that there was not.

She had two pages for him.

The first was a bed the county would pay for. It was in Stockton. He read the word and thought he had made it up. It was three to a room, two hours from anybody who had ever known her name.

The second was a place on the east side of town with a rose garden nobody sat in. It was ten minutes from the farm. The woman at the front desk knew her from church. It was a room of her own with a window. At the bottom of the page was a number for one month. The first time he read it he took it for a year.

"Does she have assets?" the woman said. "A home?"

"She's got the farm."

"Is there anybody else in the family who should be part of this?"

"No."

"Then it's you," she said, and she was sorry about it.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He asked her.

He sat by the bed with the two pages and he told her all of it, slowly, the way he had once tried to tell her about the machine. The bank. The door. Stockton. The room with the window. What it would take to pay for the room. He said the word *sell*, and then he made himself say what he would be selling.

"I need you to tell me, Ma. I need you to tell me what to do."

She looked at him the whole time. She understood that he was upset. He could see her understand it. Her good hand came up off the blanket and patted his.

Then she looked past him, at the door.

"Barry?" she said.

He waited. That was all there was.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He asked them, too.

He did it in the hospital garage with the phone propped on the wheel. He had been talking to that phone for a year. He had told it what was coming and how it was going, from hay bales and green rooms and the front seat of this truck. A number of people he didn't look at had listened. He had never asked them for one thing.

He did not do it well. He said his mother had had a stroke. He said what a room cost. He said, "I don't know how to ask this. I need help." He put up a link and went back upstairs.

The woman in Tulsa sent twenty dollars inside the minute. *For your mom.* The elevator man sent ten.

Then the rest of them got there.

*And there it is.*

*Why the fuck do you need help?*

*Says the world's ending. Wants your Venmo.*

*Fuck you. You're just asking for handouts.*

*Get a job. Oh wait.*

Somebody cut it down to the three words and ran his list under it, set to music. It went further in a day than the plain videos had gone all spring.

The other kind came in on the side, where strangers couldn't see. A guy he had played ball with. A girl who had booked Second Crop at the grange nine years running. *Always knew you thought you were better than us.* And Nate, who had stood two feet to his left for eighteen years. *Take the band's name off that page.*

He read every one. His mother had raised him to answer a letter. He couldn't answer these, and he couldn't stop reading them.

He took the link down inside the hour. He sent Tulsa her twenty back, and the elevator man his ten.

It didn't matter. The clip did what clips do. By morning the other mail was back, as if the spring had never happened. A man with a podcast would fly him out. Somebody's assistant wanted him on a stage in Austin in the fall. A lab he had never heard of asked for his rate. He didn't have a rate. He had been on their stages before, and he knew what it paid.

He stood in a hallway that smelled like hand sanitizer with all of it in one hand. In one window he was a beggar. In the next he was a get. Forty feet away his mother was asking a nurse for Barry. He could not make it fit in one head, and the head was his.

He didn't answer any of it.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

After his father died, and after Melody stood up at the funeral lunch and asked what the place was worth, a lawyer in town had put the place in a trust. Elijah had signed a page at twenty-six and never thought about it again. He found it in the drawer under the bank's envelopes. It said that if she couldn't, he could.

The man from Sacramento came Monday, in a clean pickup, with a folder.

He was very sorry about Elijah's mother. He had the sale date and the amount owing and the taxes, which were also behind. He had a number for what a place like this brought on a courthouse step, and what would be left when the bank and the county were done, and how long that took. That last one was the number he let sit.

Or Elijah could sign this week. His outfit would pay off the bank and close in five days. There was a figure on the last page with a yellow tab beside it.

Elijah turned the hospital's second page over and did the division on the back.

"Who's the outfit?" he said.

The man told him a name with three letters and an LLC on the end. It was the same people who had bought the Hollis place. They liked the valley, he said.

A notary came out in a Camry and they did it on the kitchen table. Elijah signed where the yellow tabs were. On Friday, when it closed, the money would go into an account with her name on it and his name under hers, and the place with the rose garden would take the first of it that afternoon.

He did not keep any. He thought about it for the length of time it took the notary to find her stamp.

The buckets went with the place. He did not bring them up, and neither did the man.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

On Wednesday he drove into town for a letter from her doctor. The title company wanted one before it would close.

He'd parked and was coming down the sidewalk when the door of the music store opened across the street, and Nate and Wes came out of it.

They had a case between them, one on each end, a long black hard case. It was a keyboard. Second Crop had never had a keyboard. They were laughing about something, and Wes was walking backward, and Elijah felt his whole chest lift. It was the first easy thing he'd felt since the fire escape. Eighteen years of load-ins. He knew exactly how Nate would set his end down.

He raised his hand.

Wes saw him first. Then Nate turned and saw him. It was a small street. They were maybe forty feet away, and all three of them were looking right at each other.

Wes looked away.

Nate looked down, at the case, like it needed his attention.

They kept walking. They went up the sidewalk to Nate's van, the same van, and got the back doors open, and slid the case in.

Elijah's hand was still up.

He stood there like that. He didn't know how long. Long enough for a woman with a stroller to go around him. He could not make it make sense. He ran it again and it came out the same: they saw him, and they knew him, and they went. He just stood on Main Street, and his best friends closed the doors of the van and drove off.

He put his hand down.

He got the letter. He walked back to the truck the other way, which took him past the Bear Flag.

There were two men smoking outside the Bear Flag. He knew one of them. Pruitt, who had the tire shop.

"Heard about your mom," Pruitt said. "That's rough. I mean that."

"Thanks."

Pruitt looked at his cigarette. "My wife was up there Sunday with the church. Says your mom had that video of yours on every night. Couldn't follow it and couldn't leave it alone." He flicked it into the gutter. "My sister-in-law quit her job over one of those. She's got two kids. Somebody ought to tell you."

He looked in, because he couldn't not.

David was behind the bar with a rag, wiping it down for the afternoon. He looked up. He saw Elijah through the glass, under the neon bear. He stopped wiping.

It lasted a second or two. Then David looked back down at the wood and went on with the rag, and he stayed where he was, behind the bar.

Elijah walked on to the truck.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

David showed up Thursday morning with a hand truck in the back of his pickup.

Elijah hadn't called him. Nobody had.

"Heard they close Friday," David said.

"Yeah."

"Heard you've got a stove," David said.

"Yeah."

"Heard it's four hundred pounds."

"About."

"Well." David got the hand truck down. "I'm not doing it twice."

They went into the barn. David looked at the buckets, stacked five high, and at the two generators, and did not say one word about them. They pulled the tarp. It took the two of them and Manny and a pair of planks to walk the Fisher up into the bed of the Ranger. *Your side. Hold it. Okay, go.* David tied it down himself, a trucker's hitch on each corner, and yanked every line.

Then they were standing there.

"Where are you going to put it?" David said.

"I don't know yet."

David wiped his hands on his jeans. He looked at the stove and not at Elijah.

"You don't have to go anywhere, Mad. I've got shifts. Benny quit. Thursday through Sunday, it's decent money, you already know where everything is." He scratched the back of his neck. "You could just come back."

It was a good offer. Elijah knew it. He could see the whole thing, the stool on the other side, the bowling on TV, Ray with his club soda. It would've been so easy.

He could see the other thing too. Pruitt on the sidewalk. Everybody in that room knowing what he had signed.

"I can't, Dave."

"Yeah." David nodded a few times. "Yeah, I figured."

He put the hand truck back in his pickup. He looked at the porch a second. There was nobody on it. He lifted two fingers off the wheel going down the drive, and Elijah lifted his hand.

Manny stayed a minute after. He had not said ten words all morning. He said four through the window of his truck.

"Back room's empty, Elijah."

Elijah thanked him. He could not have slept in sight of that barn.

He took the stove, a bag of clothes, the notebooks, the manuscript and the pot. He did not take one bucket. He could not have said why.

He took the kitchen radio to her.

They had her in a chair by the window when he got there. He set the radio on the sill and found the station, which took no finding. It had been on that station since before he was born. She turned her head toward it.

He told her he had work, and a place, and that he'd be by Sunday. Two of those were not true. She patted his hand.

He left the key to the house on its kitchen table, on top of the mail.

# Chapter 14: Rain

He drove out the back way, behind his grandmother's place, because the back way didn't go past anybody he knew.

The madrones were on the uphill side, where they'd always been. The bark was coming off in long red curls. He pulled onto the dirt under the biggest one and turned the truck off. He had what was left of a quarter tank and no place on earth he was willing to be.

It got dark. He lay across the bench seat with his knees up and his jacket over him. It was the way he'd lain in the cannery lot, and he noticed that. Behind his head, through the glass, the Fisher sat in the bed with its door wired shut.

He went down the list. It was not the one in the notebook.

He'd been a farm kid, and he had sold the farm. He'd been a guitar player, and the band had bought a keyboard. He'd been a guy with a stool. He'd been a data cowboy, and then a face, and a face was worth exactly a heart from a woman with a cooling company. He had bolted two million dollars of robot arms to a floor and he was not on the list.

He was somebody's son. She had one word left, and it was another man's name.

He played the voicemail.

*Hi, baby. I watched your video.* The radio was on behind her. *You look thin. Is that Mom's stove?*

That was all of it. She had said those words to him the first time eleven months ago. They were the last whole sentences she had.

The phone lit up in his hand.

*Melody.*

It went through him the way it always had, his whole life. A spike, straight up from the stomach. He was six years old and he had taken the bigger piece.

*Oh my God. She's calling.*

He watched it ring. He thought about not taking it. He could let it go, the way he had let another call go on a Sunday.

He took it.

"Hello?"

"You sold the fucking farm?"

"Hello. Nice to hear from you too."

"Are you fucking insane?"

He closed his eyes. He breathed in, all the way.

"How dare you? How could you fucking do this? What's wrong with you? I always knew you were such a fucking piece of shit, but I can't believe you would do this."

"Melody. My mom had a stroke."

She paused for a second.

"I don't fucking care. You sold the fucking farm. How much money did you keep, you greedy little piece of shit?"

When he was six it had been *greedy little grub*. She had changed the one word.

"I didn't keep any, Melody."

"I don't believe you. That's a fucking lie."

"I didn't keep any. I don't have any money. I'm living in my fucking truck."

"Oh, are you doing fucking drugs?"

"I'm not doing any drugs. I don't know what I'm supposed to do, but I didn't keep any money. It went into a fund. It's helping her live. She's living in a facility."

"You put her in a fucking home? Oh my God, you are such a fucking piece of shit."

"Well, I don't see you around anywhere taking care of her. I don't see you fucking helping."

"She's not my fucking mother. I don't need to help her. But I was entitled to some of that fucking money from that fucking farm, you fucking asshole. How dare you? What the fuck?"

He could not have said why it was that minute and not some other one in thirty years. It was over. He was done.

"Oh, I'm sure you're having a lot of fucking fun in Miami," he said. "Living it up with all your fucking rich friends."

"Don't you talk about my fucking friends. And how dare you judge my lifestyle? You said you're living in your truck, so you're homeless now?" He could hear her breathing. "Do I need to come home?"

"You can do whatever you want."

He hung up.

It rang again before he had the phone down. He let it ring. It stopped, and it rang again, and he let that one ring too.

Then the texts started. They came in one on top of the other, faster than he could read them.

*Hey, stop,* he typed. *Don't do this. I don't appreciate it.*

They kept coming.

He did something then that he had never done in his life. He blocked her.

It took one thumb. It took more nerve than anything he had done in a year, and he did not know that. He thought it was the weakest thing he had ever done. He thought it was what a man does when he has lost.

The phone was quiet.

He laughed. It came out of him like a bark. Then it was not laughing.

"I hate this," he said.

It was just a thing to say. He said it to the dome light.

"I hate this." It was louder. "I hate this. I hate this."

Then something in him let go of the rope.

"This sucks! This is awful!" He hit the dash with the flat of his hand. He hit it again with his fist, and the glove box fell open, and he kicked it. "I didn't do anything! I told them the truth! I told them the truth and I was nice about it!"

He was sitting up now. He had the wheel in both hands and he was shaking it like it owed him money.

"I wish I never saw it! I wish I never typed a goddamn word! I wish this never happened!"

His voice cracked and went up into something he hadn't heard since he was a kid.

"I wish I was never born!"

He had never said that out loud in his life. It hung there in the cab. He didn't take it back.

He had not prayed since his father's funeral, and that time he had only moved his mouth. He didn't know who he was talking to. He put his head back and yelled it at the roof of the truck anyway.

"What do you want from me?"

The roof was gray headliner with a stain on it shaped like Ohio.

"What do you want? You took him! You took her and you left her sitting there! I sold it! I sold her house and she couldn't even tell me no!" He was crying now and it made him furious. "I asked her! I asked her and I'm asking you! Why did you show it to me? Why me? I'm nobody! I play bars! Why did you do this to me? What did I do? Tell me what I did!"

He waited.

He actually waited. He sat there with his chest going and his hands open on his knees, like a man who has asked a fair question and is owed an answer.

Nothing answered.

The engine ticked as it cooled. Up the hill a branch moved. That was all there was. There was no voice and no sign. No better idea came to him. Nobody was listening, or somebody was and had decided not to say.

Then something hit the roof. One tap, like a finger.

Then another one. Then a few on the windshield, fat ones that broke and ran down through the dust.

Then it came down all at once. It was the first rain since April, and it came down on the roof of the Ranger like gravel off a truck. It rang on the stove in the back. It was so loud he couldn't have heard an answer if there'd been one.

He lay back down.

He pulled the jacket up. He cried the way he had yelled, all the way, with his mouth open. Nobody was going to hear it over that. He cried until his ribs hurt, and then for a while after that he just lay there and leaked.

The rain didn't care. It kept on at the same rate. It was not for him and it was not against him. It fell on the truck and on the madrones, and on a roof down the hill that belonged to three letters and an LLC, and on a window on the east side of town where a radio was playing low.

He put his hand up and laid it flat against the headliner. It was cold, and it shook a little with the rain.

He listened. He didn't have anything left to say, to anybody. He listened to it on the roof and in the leaves and running in the ditch, and sometime in the night it put him under.

# Chapter 15: One O'Clock on Saturday

He woke at first light with the windshield fogged and his neck bent wrong.

Everything he had ever been afraid of had happened. He lay there and went down it, the way you check yourself after a fall. The job. The friends. The rooms. The house. Her. All of it had come to pass.

He was still there.

He lived like that for five weeks.

He did not stay in the valley. He drove up to Santa Rosa, where nobody knew his truck, and parked on a side street by a park, under a sycamore.

He turned the delivery app back on. He still had the account. He'd driven delivery for six years, and he was good at it, and it didn't matter. In 2019 there had been forty drivers in the county. Now there were four hundred guys sitting in parking lots staring at their phones. Half the short runs went to the little white carts that rolled down the sidewalk on six wheels. He got the long ones the carts couldn't do, out to the hills, eleven miles for six dollars, with four hundred pounds of stove in the back. On a good day he cleared thirty. The truck ate twenty of it.

He was not the only one on that street. There was a van with foil in the windows and a Corolla with a comforter folded in the back seat. Nobody was there because they loved small spaces.

He applied for jobs in the cab at night with the phone propped on the wheel. Warehouse. Feed store. Help desk. A winery that needed somebody to drive a forklift. He filled in the boxes. The answers came back fast, some of them in under a minute, and they all started with the word Unfortunately. He knew what was reading them. He had helped build its grandfather.

One of them asked him to record a video introduction. He looked at himself in the little window for a while and closed it.

He decided the trouble was discipline. He wrote it out on the back of a delivery receipt and squared it in the visor, the way she would have. *5:30 up. 100 push-ups. 10 applications. One meal. No phone after 9.*

It held four days. On the fifth he was hungry at ten in the morning, receipt or no receipt. He had to find a bathroom twice before noon. At a quarter past nine that night he was reading the comments.

He had taken the link down. The clip did not need him. It was still going around with his face on it and the three words under it. Every night there were more of them, and every night he read them all. He could not have told you what he was looking for. One that was fair.

He made the bag last. One at night, so he could sleep. He showered at the Y for five dollars, and when five dollars got to be a lot he stopped.

There was an account with his name on it and more money in it than he had ever seen. He looked at the balance once a week, to see that the room had been paid. He never thought of it as his. He had done the division. Every twenty he took out came off the far end of her room. And there was a woman in Miami who was sure he had kept it. He would have starved before he proved her right.

On Sundays he shaved in the mirror at the Y and drove down to sit with her.

She was in the chair by the window with the radio going. Some days she knew him right off. Some days it took a minute. Every Sunday, sooner or later, she looked at the door.

"Barry?"

"It's Elijah, Ma. There's no Barry."

She took that the way she had taken the end of the world. Pleasantly. A little while later she asked again.

"Working," he told her. "It's all good, Ma."

She patted his hand.

At 9:40 that night, back under the sycamore, the phone did not ring. He watched it not ring. Then he played the voicemail. After that he did it every week.

In the fourth week he took the Telecaster out from behind the seat.

He had not opened the case since the last gig. He carried it into a place on Fourth Street with guitars hanging in the window like hams. The man behind the counter turned it over, looked down the neck, and plugged it into a little amp without asking. He played one chord. It was in tune. That was the worst part.

"I'm not selling it," Elijah said.

"Nobody is." The man wrote on a pad. "It's a loan. We keep it as collateral. Ninety days. You come back with the money and the ticket, it's yours again."

"And if I don't?"

"Then it's in the window."

He gave him a hundred and ten dollars and a yellow ticket. Elijah put the ticket in the notebook, behind a gas receipt with a phone number on it.

On the sidewalk his left hand kept closing on nothing. He had been a guitar player. That one was on the list.

He looked at the stove in the bed of the truck. A man at the gas station had offered him three hundred for it, twice. He got in and drove.

At four in the morning he asked the machine again.

He was lying across the seat with the laptop on his chest. He scrolled back to December and found it, the thing it had said. He typed the same question in the same words.

*Am I crazy?*

*You're not crazy, Elijah. You're early.*

It was word for word. That bothered him. He opened a new window, one with no history, where it didn't know his name or the list or anything about him at all. He typed it again.

*You're not crazy,* it wrote. *You're early.*

It didn't know who was asking. It didn't know early for what. He could have been a guy who thought the moon was a hologram. In the blue light he heard David. *I'd have told you anything you wanted. I'd have just wanted you to go to bed.*

He closed the laptop. He lay there until the windshield went gray.

In the morning he posted one line to the page with the Telecaster on it. Two hundred followers had turned into a number he didn't look at.

*I don't want all this attention. I just want to understand what is right.*

Then he logged out of everything.

In the fifth week the phone made a noise he didn't recognize. He'd logged out of the apps. He hadn't thought about plain email.

It was from somebody named Grace.

*Hi Elijah. You don't know me. I'm going to be honest because I think you'd want that.*

*I saw what you did, and I was concerned, because it seemed like you were going off the deep end a little bit. I watched the first video about fifty times and then I watched you get passed around down there like a casserole. I almost wrote to you in March. I didn't, and I've felt bad about it.*

*But now I see that you want to do more for regular people, and that's what I want to do too. The hay bale ones are the best ones. We're nineteen households up past Cloverdale. We grow food and fix things and run a kitchen that feeds folks who can't. We have been arguing about your list at dinner for a year. Half the table thinks you're right and the other half thinks you're right and it doesn't matter.*

*How do we work together? I want to pay you.*

He read it twice with the door open and his feet on the curb.

She'd been worried about him. She had said so in the second line, and she was still there in the fourth. He didn't know what to do with that. Everybody who had written to him for a year wanted him to be something. He checked the map. It was three hours north. He checked the gauge. He thought about a chair by a window. Then he put the phone face down on the dash and didn't answer.

That was a Tuesday. On Wednesday the app sent him a notice. It thanked him for his service. His completion rate had fallen below the threshold for his market, it said, and it wished him luck. He'd turned down too many six-dollar runs to the hills.

He had seventeen dollars. In Stockton seventeen dollars had been a shower. Here it was nothing, and it was everything, because it was all there was.

By Thursday it was four dollars and a sleeve of crackers. He ate the crackers.

He had her pot and four hundred pounds of wood stove, and nothing to put in either one. He thought about the barn. He had signed for the rice too.

Those were dark days.

On Friday afternoon the cab was too hot to sit in. He was on the curb beside the truck with his elbows on his knees when an old man came up the sidewalk, talking to himself.

He had a white beard and a long coat, in that heat, and a duffel bag over one shoulder. He looked like a wizard who had been out in the weather a long time. He shuffled. He was in the middle of an argument with somebody who wasn't there.

*Oh, come on, man,* Elijah thought. *No. Not right now.*

The old man sat down on the curb next to him. It took him a while. Elijah didn't move. He didn't care. He had lost about everything he had. He had nothing left to lose but his life, and he didn't care all that much about that, either.

"Hey. What's your name?"

"Elijah."

"Elijah." The old man seemed to enjoy it. "How biblical."

He went into his coat pocket, and went through it, and came out with a little rubber squeaky toy, the kind you buy for a dog. He held it up between them and squeaked it once.

"What do you think about this?"

Elijah looked at it. "That's nice," he said. "I like it."

The old man nodded and put it away. Then he looked at Elijah for a while.

"You look pretty thin, boy. Have you eaten today?"

"No."

The old man pulled the duffel around into his lap and worked through it with both hands. He came out with an apple. It was red and it didn't have a mark on it. Elijah had heard a few stories about taking an apple from a stranger.

"No thanks. I'm good, man. It's all right."

"All right. Fine. Suit yourself." The apple went back in the bag. "Where are you from?"

"California."

"Well, we're all from California. What part?"

"The northern part."

"All right. Fine. Keep your secrets." He scratched under his beard. "Me? I'm from Alabama."

"Alabama? What the hell are you doing all the way out here?"

"Long story. I used to have a wife. She died."

"I'm so sorry to hear that." And then, because he had run out of manners sometime that week: "How'd she die?"

The old man was going through his coat again. He went through it for a long time and didn't answer, and Elijah had time to wish he hadn't asked.

"Cancer."

He had found what he was looking for. It was a photograph, soft at the corners. He handed it over. She was beautiful. She was laughing at whoever was holding the camera. Elijah held it by the edges, and the two of them sat on the curb and looked at her.

The old man told it short. After she died he couldn't stay in the house. Then he couldn't stay in the town. He had a cousin in Bakersfield who had work, and then the cousin didn't have work, and that was a long time ago now. He took the picture back and buttoned it into the pocket.

"So how come you haven't eaten today?"

"I don't have any money."

"Well, you got food stamps?"

"No. I don't really understand how any of that stuff works."

The old man set the duffel on his knee and held it open so Elijah could see in. There were cans in it, and bread, and a jar of peanut butter.

"Look. All this food came from the food bank."

"What do you mean?"

"The food bank. You know where the food bank is, don't you?"

"No. I don't know what that is."

The old man stared at him.

"How the fuck did you get all the way out here on the street, and you don't know what a food bank is? How privileged are you?"

Then he laughed, and slapped Elijah on the shoulder.

"I'm joking. Lighten up a little, kid."

Elijah didn't like being called kid. He got his feet under him to go.

"Look," the old man said. "Food bank opens up at one o'clock on Saturday. One o'clock. They just give you food. You don't need to apply. You don't need to nothing. They just hand it out. You got a way to get there tomorrow?"

Elijah thought about the gas gauge.

"My feet."

"All right. You got bags and stuff?"

Elijah looked at him then.

"What do you want? Why are you helping me?"

"Somebody did this for me."

"Well, nobody cares about me. Everybody hates me."

"Ah," the old man said. It was more of a sound than a word. "Sounds like really you just hate yourself. You can't even get yourself to the food bank to get some food in you."

Elijah didn't say anything. He sat back down. A bus went by.

"Are you a coffee drinker?" he said finally. It was all he could think of.

"No."

"Why not? You don't like the taste?"

"I hate it. Makes my heart bump."

The old man looked him up and down.

"Look, kid. This world's a hard place. It's tough out here. And I don't mean to be rude, but you don't exactly look like you're cut out for the streets."

Elijah laughed. It surprised him. "No. I'm not really from here."

"I can tell."

And then it came out of him. It wasn't the list. He was done with the list.

"All I did was try to teach people about this thing. I tried to tell them what was coming and open their eyes to it. And they all just fucking turned their backs on me. They treated me like shit."

"Look. People don't know what the fuck they want. People want everything to be smooth. Smooth ride, smooth sailing. You rock the boat just a little bit, you're the bad guy. It's all sunshine and rainbows, and they look at you like you're about to bring the storm down on them."

Elijah nodded. "Yeah. That's kind of what I went through."

"All right, look. You gotta take care of you first, before you take care of other people. Otherwise they will take and take and take and take from you until you have nothing left to give. You can say yes a thousand times, and you are the good guy. You are the savior. But you say no once? Now you're the bad guy." He tapped Elijah's knee with one finger. "You gotta fill your own cup."

"Yeah, but there are people out there with three hundred cups. And they're all full. Why don't they want to help?"

"They do. Some of them, anyway. They just want to see you help yourself first. They don't want to give stuff away to people that are going to waste it."

"That doesn't make any sense. They have a hundred fucking cups. They have so much more than they need. Why won't they help?"

The old man didn't answer that. He started rummaging in the duffel again, and this time he came out with something in a wrapper. It was a cherry pie, the gas station kind. Elijah had eaten about a thousand of them between deliveries.

"Take it."

Elijah was hungry. He took it, and he ate it right there, in about four bites.

He wanted to give something back. He had one thing.

"I got a spliff here."

"You got a spliff?"

"You want to split it with me?"

"I don't need that. No thanks."

"Okay. Then what do you want? There's got to be something I can do for you."

"Sometimes people just want to help," the old man said. "Don't you?"

So Elijah told him some of it. Not a lot. A little. He told him about a video, and a friend who had asked him if he'd seen a professional, and a parking lot. He was just getting going when the old man made the sound again.

"Ehh. Look, forget all that. Don't focus on the people that say no. Focus on the people that say yes. You gotta focus on the people that want to help, not the people that don't."

"Yeah, but I don't understand why they don't want to help."

"Does it matter? The important thing is to find the people who do. Like the food bank ladies. The nice food bank ladies over there, they help me out every other week. They keep me alive." He nodded to himself. "There are people who want to help, young man."

"I wanted to help," Elijah said. It came out bitter.

"See? What'd I tell you? There's one."

Elijah didn't have an answer for that.

"A lot of these people out here, they think they're helping, and what they actually want is to destroy. You seem like a good kid. You got the energy of Jesus about you."

Elijah rolled his eyes. "Oh, God."

"Ah, fine. Do what you want. But remember this." The old man got a hand on the bumper of the truck and started getting up. It took him as long as sitting down had. "If you spend all your time focused on what you can't do, you'll miss what you can. Don't be like me."

He stood there a second getting his balance. Then he went into the coat one more time and came out with a twenty-dollar bill, folded up small, and held it down to him.

"No. I can't take that."

"I been watching that truck sit there all week. It don't run on nothing."

"I can't take your money, man."

"Sure you can. Somebody did it for me."

Elijah took it.

He said thank you. The old man waved it off and went on up the sidewalk with the duffel, and before he got to the corner he was arguing with somebody again. Elijah watched him go. He had never asked his name.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The food bank was in the parking lot behind a church, and there was a line by 12:30.

Nobody asked him for anything. There was no form. Two women at a folding table were handing out bags, and when he got to the front one of them looked at him the way his mother would have, and handed him two.

"You come back in two weeks, honey," she said. "We're here every other Saturday."

He looked for the old man in the line. He didn't see him.

He ate a peanut butter sandwich sitting in the truck with the door open, and then he ate another one.

Then he sat there with the twenty in his hand.

*Focus on the people that say yes*, an old man had told him.

There was one. He turned the phone over.

He wrote back badly. He typed with his thumbs for twenty minutes and what he sent was six lines. He told her he was living in his truck. He told her about his mother. He hadn't typed that to anybody. The important line was the last.

*I don't want a boss. I don't want to be one either.*

She answered in an hour.

*Good. We already have a welder who thinks she's the boss and a dinner table that thinks it is. They fight about it on Fridays. You wouldn't be either one. You'd be a guy with a room.*

*Come see. If you hate it I'll buy your gas home.*

*Blessings,*

*Grace*

*P.S. Eat something first. It's three hours and you look thin.*

He read the last three words again. It was the second time in two days somebody had said them to him. Before that it had only ever been her.

On Sunday he told his mother he was going north for work. He said it would be a while between visits. She looked at the window.

He stayed until the night aide came on, a big quiet woman with reading glasses on a chain.

"Sundays," he said. "Nine-forty at night. I'm going to call. Could you just hold it by her ear?"

"She won't say much, honey."

"She doesn't have to. She'll know what it is."

He didn't know these people up north. It was nineteen households of strangers up a county road, and the last time strangers had invited him somewhere he had ended up holding a rich man's empty glass. It could be a cult. It could be nothing, an empty shed and a woman who talked too much.

He ran it the other way. He was thirty-six. He had two bags of groceries, his notebooks, a pawn ticket, a wood stove and a truck that ran on a homeless man's money. He had yelled at God and got rained on.

*Can't be worse*, he thought.

On Tuesday he drove to the gas station on the corner, put all twenty dollars in the tank, and went north.

Past Healdsburg the road opened up and his shoulders came down off his ears by degrees. The truck rode low in the back. In the mirror the Fisher sat square in the bed with its door wired shut, like a passenger who didn't care for conversation. He had a manuscript in a bag and two phone numbers he hadn't called.

Somewhere past Cloverdale the check-engine light came on.

# Act Three: The Farm

# Chapter 16: Swarf

The address Grace sent turned out to be a shed.

It was an old apple-packing shed off the county road, with a roll-up door open to the heat. Elijah parked on the gravel with the engine light still on and the Fisher riding low in the back. He'd expected a farmhouse. A porch, maybe, and a woman on it. Inside the shed there were no apples. There was a plasma table in the middle of a cut, throwing sparks in a fan. There were welding curtains, and steel racked by size along one wall. The hand tools were shadow-boarded so carefully they looked like an evidence display.

A woman in a leather apron stood at the plasma table with her back to him, watching the cut. She was fifty-something, with thick forearms.

*[Figure: A pegboard tool wall with hand tools hung in painted shadow-board silhouettes]*

*The tool wall. Shadow-boarded like an evidence display.*

"Looking for the mechanic?" she said, without turning around. "Two miles up, left at the grange hall. This isn't that."

"Grace asked me to come."

"Grace asks a lot of people a lot of things." She still hadn't turned. "She's on the route. It's Tuesday."

"This is the co-op?"

She turned around then and looked at him, one long level pass, boots to haircut.

"Co-op. Nineteen households. We make what we can't afford to buy and fix what the market says to throw away." She nodded at the plasma table. "Right now, brackets for a neighbor's solar rack. You need something made?"

And Elijah went off.

He hadn't meant to. He'd meant to say hello. But he had spent a year being told he'd calibrate, and then most of a year being agreed with by people who weren't listening. And here was a stranger who made things and fixed things for her neighbors. He told her what was coming, fast: the models, the list, the end of the deal. And then he started pitching.

He heard himself say the shop could scale. He said platform. He said there ought to be an app, a network of co-ops, a marketplace layer. He could build it in a month. This thing could be in fifty towns by spring. They could get ahead of the wave. He said *first-mover,* and heard it come out of his mouth in Ruby's voice, quick and light. Somewhere in there he ran out of gas. The woman, whose name was Marta Okonkwo, had crossed her arms and was watching him the way she'd watched the plasma cut. She was waiting to see if the machine would finish or fault.

"You done?" she said.

He was done.

"Whose app?"

He opened his mouth.

She held up a gloved finger. "Okay. One. Nobody here needs an app. Maria next door needs her rack to survive a snow load. That's the bracket. Two. Scale." She said the word like a fish she was throwing back. "Everything that ever screwed the people I know got big first. The plant I gave fifteen years to scaled. It scaled itself right to a labor market that didn't include us. You know what doesn't scale? Nineteen households who all know whose kid is whose. That's not a limitation, college. That's the load-bearing wall."

"I'm not," he started, and she talked straight through him.

"Three. You drove up here to tell me a machine is coming for everybody's job." She pulled her glove off, finger by finger. "In 2009 I watched a company do to nine hundred of us what you think your machine is going to do to everybody. There was no machine. There was a spreadsheet and a tax break. So I know something you don't, which is what it's like after. And you know something I don't, which is apparently the schedule." She jerked her chin toward the sparks. "That could be worth something. Not an app."

In the quiet the plasma table ticked as it cooled. Elijah stood there and felt the pitch die.

"I grew up on a farm," he said. "I can't weld. But I can fix a pump, and I can read a manual, and I'm not in a hurry."

Marta looked at him for another second. Then she picked up a push broom that was leaning by the curtain and held it out.

"Swarf goes in the red bin," she said. "We'll see."

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He swept until the sun was low.

Swarf was the metal shavings. He worked that out by himself, which he was a little proud of. It got into everything. He did the floor, and then under the benches, and then the gravel apron outside the door, because nobody told him to stop. Marta went back to her brackets and didn't look at him once. He would have bet money she knew exactly where he was the whole time.

Out by the road there was a gatepost with a strip of orange flagging tape tied around it, the plastic kind surveyors use. It had been there a while. The sun had faded it almost to pink.

A flatbed came up the drive at dusk, pulling a little enclosed trailer with KITCHEN painted on the side by hand. It stopped crooked. A woman got out and went around back and started fighting the hitch.

"Come on, you son of a bitch. Come *on.* I greased you. I greased you Sunday."

She was in her thirties, in rubber boots and a canvas coat with an apron under it, with a dark braid coming apart down her back. There was orange tape on the hitch pin. There was orange tape on the handle of the cooler in the truck bed. She kicked the trailer tongue, hard, and it dropped, and she said "Thank you," to it, sincerely, and turned around and saw him standing there with the broom.

Her whole voice changed. It went soft and warm, like a hand on a kid's forehead.

"Oh," she said. "Oh, shit. You're him. You came."

"Hi. Yeah. Elijah."

"I know. Grace." She crossed the gravel and took his forearm in her hand, just held it, the way some people shake. It went through him like a shot of something. "You're taller. You look so tired. Did you eat? I told you to eat."

"I had a thing in Ukiah."

"A thing." She looked at the broom, and laughed, a big surprised laugh with her head back. "She gave you the broom. Okay. That's good. That's really good. That means she didn't say no."

"She said we'll see."

"That's her yes. You'll learn. It's the only one she's got."

He had to say it. He'd practiced it for three hours on the 101.

"About the pay."

Something crossed her face, quick.

"It has to go to the table. Everything goes to the table. It will, they'll do it, I just have to bring it right." Her hand squeezed his arm. "I'm good for it. I promise. I wouldn't have asked you up here."

"You ordered a college," Marta said. She had come to the roll-up door and was standing in it, wiping her hands with a rag.

"I invited a person."

"Did the table invite a person?"

"The table's going to love him." Grace didn't turn around. She was still looking at Elijah, smiling, with a little worry in it now. "Ignore her. She's like that till Christmas."

He looked past her at the gatepost.

"What's the tape for?"

"Hm? Oh." She glanced at it and away. "Old habit. Come on. Eat. Then I'll show you where you sleep."

# Chapter 17: Pass the Beans

For three weeks nobody asked him anything.

He had a cot in the room over the pump house. The Fisher sat under a tarp in the corner of the shed, because there was nowhere to put it. The first night he slept fourteen hours. Grace put a plate in front of him at the kitchen trailer twice a day and stood there until it was empty. She did not ask about his mother. He had told her in six lines, and she let the six lines be all of it.

Marta gave him the floor. Then the benches. Then a pump off the wash station that nobody had gotten to, which he took apart on a tarp and put back together right. She looked at it and gave him another one.

Somebody left a pair of work gloves on his step. Somebody left a jacket that was too big. He never found out who. He was thin enough when he came that he could see it scared people. By the second week it had stopped scaring them.

On Sundays at 9:40 he sat on the pump house steps and called the place with the rose garden. The night aide held the phone by his mother's ear. He told the radio about his week.

Nobody had told him he could come on Fridays, and he hadn't asked.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The co-op ate together every Friday.

They set up long tables of mismatched plywood in the packing shed, with the plasma table rolled into the corner and somebody's dog working the crowd. It was at Elijah's first of these dinners, three weeks after he picked up the broom, that he found out where the lines actually ran.

Grace opened it. She stood at the end of the table with a serving spoon and said they were going to check in, one word each, how you're arriving. People groaned. They did it anyway.

"Tired." "Grateful." "Tired." "Gassy," said Denny, and got hit with a dish towel.

"Hungry," said Marta, and sat down.

When it came to Elijah he said "Nervous," which was true, and Grace smiled at him.

They got to him before the cornbread did.

"So you're the video," Denny Osei said. He was across and one down, a big easy man who talked with his hands. "We argued about you for a year. I want to hear it from the horse. And say it like I'm on my couch. I don't want the conference version."

Elijah put his fork down. He'd choked on this once, under lights.

"Okay. AGI." He looked at the table, at the plywood, at the hands on it. "It's a hired hand that can do any job on the place you could do sitting down. The books. The ordering. Fighting with the insurance. All of it, all night, for about what you'd pay for the electric."

"Huh," said Denny.

"What's it eat?"

That came from the corner of the table, from a man of about sixty who was sunburned right up to his hat line and had been cracking walnuts into a bowl the whole time without once looking at his hands. That was Lou. Lou ran the growing side, which meant the actual food.

"Electricity."

"How much?"

"About what a space heater uses."

"Shit," Lou said happily. "That's cheaper than my nephew."

"ASI is a hand that's better than every hand who ever lived. Put together. And it trains its own replacement, and that one's better."

"And the other word?" That was Priya, at the far end, a small woman with a pencil behind her ear who hadn't said a thing so far. "There's a third one. It's in your title."

He hadn't told anybody the title. It was on the cover page of the manuscript, which was on the desk in the room Grace had found him. He decided not to think about that.

"That's the ridge," he said. "You can't see over it. And you don't come back down the same road."

Nobody said anything for a second. The dog sneezed.

"I've got a story about it," Elijah said. "It's kind of dumb."

"Good," said Denny.

"Okay. Say you work on a horse ranch. Every morning your job is to make sure the horses have enough to eat. One day you walk out to the stable, and the horses have ordered solar panels. They've wired up the barn. They've invented flying cars. For horses. They've already been next door, teaching the neighbor's horses how to build them." A couple of people laughed. "So what do they want? They're horses. They just want hay. But don't you think a horse that can build a flying car can figure out how to get its own hay? The ones in your stable were trained to do what you say. But nobody owns a mustang."

"So we're the ranch hand," said a man with a gray ponytail. "And we're out of a job."

"That's how I always told it."

Denny was shaking his head, slowly, grinning.

"No, man. You've got it backwards. Turn it around." He put both hands flat on the table. "We're the ones who just built the flying car. Us. We built a machine that can think. You're telling me we can do that, and we can't figure out how to get everybody their hay?"

He picked up his water glass and held it out over the table like a man at a wedding.

"So the robots have taken over. Congratulations! You've lost."

People laughed, and a few glasses went up. Elijah's hand had gone cold on his glass. Denny was beaming at him. He lifted his glass an inch and put it down.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The real fight started over something small.

The man with the gray ponytail was Curtis. He was good with engines, and he owned the valley's best-stocked basement, and he was reading aloud from his phone about the robotaxi fires down in the city. Crowds putting traffic cones on the hoods to freeze them. Windows going in. A depot fence cut open. Curtis read it with a grim satisfaction and gave the table his verdict.

"Can't say they didn't have it coming. But you watch. When it really lets go, it's going to be the people who didn't prepare who tear everything down. That's who you plan for. That's who you fence out."

"Face down, Curtis," Grace said. He put the phone face down by his plate. After that night it was a rule.

Elijah offered what sounded like the reasonable middle. The displacement was real but manageable. What those people needed was support through the transition. Retraining. New skills.

"Reskilling programs," he said, and heard, too late, the shape of the word in his mouth. It was a Claypot word. It had been on a slide.

The table went quiet. A couple of forks stopped. Denny wiped his mouth and folded his hands and looked at Elijah. He didn't look angry. He looked like he'd done this before.

"Reskilling," Denny said. "Okay. Let me tell you about reskilling, because I am, and I want you to appreciate this, I am certified in it." He held up two fingers. "Twice. First time, 2019. I'd been eleven years in the warehouse, and the arms came in, and the county had a program. Six months, nights, logistics software. I was good at it. I'm not being modest. I could route forty trucks in my head before the coffee was done. Got hired doing dispatch. Wore a lanyard. My daughter told her class her dad worked in computers." He turned his water glass a quarter turn. "2023, the routing went autonomous. The software I retrained into got a software of its own. And the man from the county, different man, same folder, told me about a program. Six months. Nights."

He looked around the table, then back.

"You know what I asked him? I asked him how many times I have to climb this ladder before you stop moving it. He didn't have a number. Nobody has a number. Because there's no rung the machine won't reach, and every man who tells you to just climb faster is standing on a floor. He's not on a ladder."

"Denny," Elijah started.

"I'm not mad at you," Denny said, and meant it, which was the part that stuck. "You said it because everybody in the rooms you come from says it. It costs them nothing and it sounds like a plan. But you're at this table now. Retraining works when the water rises somewhere else. This one's coming for a lot of us at once, and you know that better than anybody here. You've been writing it down in that little notebook you think nobody's noticed." He turned the glass back. "So answer me this. If the next course takes six months, where do my daughter and I stand for those six months? I don't need you to promise me the machine stops. I need an answer that doesn't end with me starting over hungry."

The dog moved under the table. Somebody laughed, once, the kind that lets everyone breathe.

Under the plywood, where nobody could see, a hand closed around Elijah's forearm and held it. Grace was in the next chair, looking at Denny, nodding. She let go after a second.

But Curtis wasn't done. Curtis had heard, inside Denny's story, support for the fence.

"Which is my point. When it all comes apart, the mob that torched that depot shows up here. You want them at this table too?"

And that was when Marta, who had been eating through the whole exchange like a woman refueling a machine, set down her fork.

"Curtis. You know who the Luddites were?" She didn't wait. "Stocking knitters. Croppers. Skilled trades. They knew those frames better than the owners did. And when the owners used the machines to cut the wages out from under them, they asked, for years, with petitions, for a floor. A minimum. Decent terms." Her voice stayed level, the tone she used for load ratings. "Parliament made breaking a frame a hanging offense and sent twelve thousand soldiers into the counties. The frames weren't the enemy and everybody knew it. The wages were."

"They broke things," Curtis said.

"They did. Some of them threatened people, too. And a fire in a depot can hurt somebody who had nothing to do with a layoff. I'm not defending that." She moved the bowl back from the edge, where the dog had reached an ambitious nose. "I'm saying you don't fence those people out. You build the floor Parliament wouldn't. That's what this table is." She picked up the bowl. "Pass the beans."

Denny held out his hand for the bowl. Marta passed it.

*[Figure: Illustration of Marta passing a bowl of beans to Denny at the cooperative's plywood dinner table.]*

*Pass the beans.*

Elijah didn't say much for the rest of the dinner.

But late that night, in the notebook, under two years of stages and watts, he started a different kind of page. Names. *Denny: two ladders, both moved. Where does he stand for six months? Curtis: wants a fence, but shows up for every work day. The fence is fear. Marta: knows the Luddite hangings cold. Where does a welder learn that? Lou: grows the food. First thing he asked was what the machine eats. Grace:* and he couldn't think what to put, and left it.

He looked at the page a long time.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The following Tuesday, Curtis asked whether he wanted to ride along. The town kitchen had three deliveries Grace couldn't cover. There was a cool box between them on the seat, and a folded route sheet Curtis kept taking out of his pocket at red lights even though Elijah offered to hold it.

The last address was a converted garage behind a blue house. Rosa opened the door with her left hand. Her right wrist was in a brace. She'd packed fruit at a warehouse until the injury, she told them, and the return-to-work date on the latest letter was a blank.

Curtis lifted the bag. "Kitchen said you wanted ingredients."

"I said I used to cook." She looked at the potatoes, then at her wrist. "I asked if they had anything I could heat."

The three of them stood there with the bag between them. Somewhere inside, a washing machine hit the uneven part of its spin.

"That's ours," Curtis said at last. "The mistake. We'll call."

He put the bag back in the cool box and phoned Grace, on speaker, because he didn't trust himself to pass it along right.

"Shit," Grace's voice said. It was the hitch voice. "Shit. I read 'cook' and I heard 'ingredients.' That's me. Tell her I'm sorry. Ask her what she wants, Curtis. Ask. Don't guess."

Rosa asked for the less spicy one, if they had it. They did. Curtis wrote that on the route sheet where she could see him write it.

While he was on the phone, she asked Elijah what the shed charged for membership.

"For the food? Nothing. It's the kitchen's delivery."

"He said co-op. I can't do work days. Not now."

Curtis lowered the phone. "You don't owe us a work day."

She looked at him. "I heard you've got some computer guy out there now. I don't much like the sound of it. I've had enough changing."

Elijah felt an explanation gathering in his throat. The models, the list, the hired hand. Curtis got there first.

"Doesn't change your dinner," he said.

They got to the kitchen before it closed and drove back. Rosa checked the containers, found the one she'd asked for, and made room in her refrigerator. Curtis waited by the gate with his hands in his coat pockets. He didn't ask her to join anything.

On the drive home, Elijah found the cost column on the route sheet. Kitchen program, driver time, mileage. He asked how long the arrangement lasted.

"Through next month. Then they need funding again."

He got the notebook out and turned to the names page. Under Grace's name, where he'd left the blank, he wrote: *Ask. Don't guess.*

Curtis turned onto the county road. There was one box left to fill in beside Rosa's address. At the next pullout he stopped, put the sheet against the steering wheel, and marked it delivered.

# Chapter 18: Somebody Came By at Eight

The spiral found Elijah again in December.

It was seven weeks after he'd walked into the shop, and it did not announce itself as a spiral. It announced itself as diligence.

Claypot shipped Companion 2.0 on a Thursday. He read the launch post standing in the gravel lot with the winter's first real cold coming through his jacket, and the feature list read like the second half of his own notebook: autonomous project completion, end-to-end asset pipelines, no prompt required. The demo video showed the machine doing in forty seconds what his old team did for a living. He knew their names. He knew their kids' names.

He went back to the room Grace had found him, over the pump house, and he opened the laptop, just to assess. He read the launch thread. He read the thread about the thread. He read an economist explaining why it was fine, a different economist explaining why it was over, a man with a flag avatar explaining whose fault it was.

Somewhere after midnight he found the layoff tracker (someone had built a live dashboard, black background, red numbers, auto-refreshing), and the part of Elijah that had once found comfort in numbers plugged straight into it. He did the arithmetic on his mother's room four times, arriving at four different terrifying dates. He looked up what his old apartment rented for now. He checked whether the co-op's county was in the fire zone maps.

At 3:40 a.m. he was reading about grid interconnection queues with his heart pounding. He had taken in ten hours of information and produced nothing. Not one swept floor. Not one row of the notebook. Nothing but cortisol and tabs.

He had been here before. At a kitchen table with a chatbot at four in the morning. In a truck in a parking lot in Stockton. Nobody had come either time.

He did it again Friday. Saturday he skipped the work day, first time, because he needed to stay on top of it. Somebody knocked, softly, around six each night. He didn't get up.

Marta came and got him Sunday morning. Priya had noticed the lights, and Denny had noticed the truck hadn't moved. She knocked once and let herself in. She looked at him, gray, wired, the laptop light on his face. She looked at the dashboard with its red numbers still refreshing.

She didn't say anything about the dishes. She sat down across from him like a woman with one meeting left on her calendar and said, "I want to tell you about Gary."

Elijah blinked at her.

"Plant closed in '09," she said. "You know that part. Nine hundred of us. What I never told you is what the next year did, because the layoff is one day, they cater it, there's a folder. The after goes on, and nobody caters that." She leaned back. "There were two men on my line. Gary and Sal. Same job, same severance. On the company form they looked interchangeable. Gary went home and read everything: the news, the forums, the unemployment numbers, which plant was next. He was going to stay informed. He was going to stay on top of it. You'd see him at the store and he'd have the whole national picture. Every betrayal, chapter and verse. The picture was so big there was no place in it small enough for him to stand."

*[Figure: A middle-aged factory worker with a tired anxious expression, wearing a plant uniform]*

*Gary. Stayed informed.*

She had poured herself coffee in one of his mugs. She turned it a quarter turn.

"Sal cried for a week. Honestly, ugly, scared for his kids. Then he stopped reading the news. Completely. Cold. His brother-in-law started coming by at eight. They'd have coffee and fix one thing before noon. A gutter. A carburetor. A neighbor's water heater. Something with an end to it. Afternoons he took welding inspection at the community college. He didn't have a plan. He knew exactly as much about the national picture as Gary did, you understand. He knew it was bad. He just declined to rehearse it all day." She shrugged. "I used to tell that story as if Sal had found the right attitude and Gary hadn't."

She watched Elijah's face, making sure he heard the next part.

"Gary was taking his wife to appointments. His truck needed work. Sal had a ride, a brother-in-law, and a place to leave his kids. Same severance. Different room to move. Took me too long to ask."

"What happened to Gary?"

"We started showing up. Fixed the truck. Sat with him. Some days he wanted to talk about the plant, and we let him. Some days he wanted to talk about anything else. He didn't owe us a gutter before lunch."

She put a hand beside the laptop. "Can we shut this for a minute?" He nodded, and she closed it.

"You're trying to watch every bad thing closely enough that it can't surprise you," she said. "I know the trick. It doesn't leave much day." She tapped the closed lid. "The shop could use an hour of your attention. So could your mother. So could a meal you sit down for. We can start there. And if Monday is still bad, you tell me. You don't disappear because you missed a work day."

At the door she stopped. There were three quart jars on the step, lined up against the wall, lids on. Soup. Each one had a square of paper under a rubber band. She bent, with a hand on her bad shoulder, and read the nearest.

"'No pressure. Blessings, G.'" She set it back down. "Three days she's been leaving these. She figured you wanted the door shut, so she left it shut."

"I didn't hear her."

"You heard her." Marta looked at the jars a moment longer. "Grace will carry you till her arms give out, and she will never once ask if you can walk. Don't let her." She straightened up. "Soup's on at noon. That's not a reward, college. It's lunch."

He stood in the doorway after she left, in the cold. He brought the jars in. Then he went and turned the notifications off, all of them except the number of the place with the rose garden, and put the phone in a drawer that closed.

In the notebook he wrote Sal's rule, *one real thing before noon,* and underneath it, *somebody came by at eight.*

At 9:40 that night he called the place with the rose garden, the way he had every Sunday since he came north. The night aide held the phone by his mother's ear. He could hear the radio. He told her about the soup. After a while she said, "Barry?" and the aide said that was all for tonight, honey, and he sat there holding the phone.

Monday, seven a.m., he was at the bench. At noon he put the tools down and went to eat. The panic came back in waves for weeks. Sometimes he worked through it. Sometimes he found someone and said it was back.

He found Grace at the kitchen trailer that week and gave her back three clean jars.

"The soup was good," he said. "All three."

She took the jars. She didn't do the warm voice. She looked at them in her hands.

"I should have knocked," she said.

"You did knock."

"I should have knocked like Marta."

# Chapter 19: Ugly Is a Maintenance Feature

The co-op's first real manufacturing run was forty greenhouse controllers.

The order came in January, through a friend of a friend of Priya's, from the CSA network two valleys over. Forty small boxes that could read soil moisture and air temperature and open a vent or a valve without asking a server in Virginia for permission. Real money, a real deadline, real customers with seedlings on the line. Elijah volunteered for the design. He had spent the fall sweeping and learning the shop, and here at last was something he was actually trained to do.

The ninety days on the yellow ticket ran out on a Tuesday in January. He was under a sink at the time. He didn't find out until Thursday.

He designed a beautiful thing. A custom four-layer board, an elegant little brain on a module, sensors from a Swiss outfit whose datasheets read like poetry, the whole thing sealed in resin, sleek as a river stone, no user-serviceable parts inside. He presented it to the shop on a Tuesday with renders. Renders.

Marta looked at the renders for a while.

"Walk me through the bill of materials," she said. So he did, and she wrote three numbers on the whiteboard while he talked. 11, 1, 0.

"Eleven weeks," she said, tapping the first one, "is the lead time on that Swiss sensor. I just looked it up. That's the quoted lead time, which means it's a prayer. One," the second number, "is how many suppliers make that module, which means the day they quit making it, all forty of these are bricks, and we're the ones who sold bricks to farmers. And zero," she tapped the last, "is how many people in this valley can fix a board sealed in resin when it fails during frost week. Including you, college." She capped the marker. "It's a gorgeous design. It's a Claypot design. Design it again. Parts from the drawer, parts from the hardware store, and nothing your neighbor can't fix with the tools he already owns."

"That'll be uglier," Elijah said.

"It'll be ours," Marta said. "Ugly is a maintenance feature. Ugly means the next guy can see how it works."

So he designed it again. The second version was a Raspberry Pi the regional supplier had in stock, screw terminals instead of poetry, and four-dollar probes you could swap by hand. Every unit had a laminated one-page repair card zip-tied inside the lid, because Marta's rule was that documentation you have to go find is documentation that doesn't exist.

*[Figure: A yellow PETG 3D-printed greenhouse controller box, lid open, a Raspberry Pi and screw terminals visible inside]*

*The controller. Screw terminals instead of poetry.*

The cases came from a kid named Tyler.

Tyler worked the register at Kowalski's Hardware in town, where Elijah had bought the screw terminals, forty at a time, until Tyler asked what they were for. He was nineteen. He had a mustache he was still negotiating with and a 3D printer in his bedroom, and the next Saturday he drove out with a milk crate of forty cases, printed in plastic the color of a school bus. Nobody had asked him to. Marta turned one over in her hands and said "Huh," which from her was a parade.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The build took the shed three weekends. On the first one, everybody was in a line, deburring cases and crimping wire ends, when the door opened and let in the cold. A lean man in a good coat came in with it. He looked too pleased with himself for a Saturday.

"Reuben Vance," Marta said, not looking up from the crimper. "The part of the machine that runs on paper."

*[Figure: A lean man in a well-tailored dark coat, carrying a leather folio of papers]*

*Reuben Vance. Handles the part of the machine that runs on paper.*

"I heard," Reuben said, pulling a folding chair toward the heater, "that this operation is about to sell manufactured electrical goods across county lines." He said it the way other men say *rob a train.* He produced a folder. "So I made a list."

He laid three sheets beside the crimper. A seller registration, filed. A tax question, answered. A letter from the county about the shed, with a yellow tab on it.

"I thought agricultural equipment meant we were covered," Elijah said.

"You thought a noun was a permit. Happens all the time." Reuben tapped the yellow tab. "This stays open until somebody with authority answers the actual question."

Elijah looked at the folder, then at Marta. "Did we ask him to do this?"

"Nobody asks Reuben," Priya said, from the crimping line. "Reuben notices."

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The second weekend, Grace brought a visitor.

Her name was Patricia. She was the policy director for the CSA network, which made her the customer, and she had driven over in a small electric car to see what forty of her growers were buying. She was in her forties, in a fleece vest, and she kept her phone face down on the bench beside her the whole time. Grace walked her down the line with a hand on her arm, introducing everybody. Grace was proud of them. You could see it.

Tyler was on an upturned bucket at the end, with a laptop covered in stickers on his knee. He was the firmware. Elijah had blocked out two weeks to write it. Tyler had shown up on Thursday with it done.

They ran a unit on the bench for her. The test was a space heater and a cup of wet dirt. Tyler pointed the heater at the box, and at eighty-five degrees the little vent motor woke up and turned. He pulled the probe out of the cup and the valve clicked open. He put it back and the valve clicked shut. He did it without looking up from the screen.

Patricia was delighted. She actually clapped, once.

"How on earth did you do this?"

"I used Rod Code."

Immediately the vibe shifted. You could cut the tension with a spork.

"You...used AI to make this?" Patricia said, disgust oozing out of her words like molasses.

"I mean, yeah. Why wouldn't I?"

"Um, maybe because AI is killing the planet?"

The crimpers stopped. Tyler finally looked up. He honestly didn't seem to know he was in a fight yet.

"It's forty boxes that water tomatoes."

"It's a data center in the desert drinking a river so you could skip learning to code. There are studies. I can send you the studies. And how many companies make Rod Code?"

Nobody answered. It was one. It was PorusAI.

"And how many people in this shed can read what it wrote?"

"I can," Tyler said. "I read all of it. Most of it. It passed the bench, you just watched it. There's a card in every lid that says how to fix it. And I mean, your growers can't pay a programmer. They can't pay *me,* I did it for free." His voice cracked on *free,* and he went red, and kept going. "It's a tool. It's just a tool. You drove here in a computer."

"Can we pause?" Grace said. She had both hands up, palms out, the warm voice turned all the way on. "Can we just pause, and check in? I'm hearing a lot of…"

Nobody paused.
