They were both right. That was Elijah's problem. He had a power bill for $211.46 in a box somewhere. He knew what the thing drank. He also knew a nineteen-year-old had just done in a night what would have taken him two weeks, and that the boxes worked, and that forty growers would get them for a price they could pay. He had helped build one of these machines. He had shown one off in a bar, and three people lost their jobs. He could have said any of it. He stood there with a case in one hand and a deburring tool in the other and didn't say a word, and both of them noticed.

Marta hadn't said anything either. She was looking at Tyler and at the laptop on his knee. Something had gone out of her face. She had the crimper in her hand and she wasn't using it.

"We'll see," she said, to nobody, and went back to work.

Patricia left before lunch. She shook everyone's hand. Grace walked her to the car, talking low, and came back alone and went straight to the kitchen trailer without looking at anybody.

Elijah found Tyler outside afterward, sitting on the trunk of a Corolla, flashing units one after another.

"I showed some realtors once," Elijah said.

"Okay," Tyler said.

He didn't ask what happened. He was nineteen and he had twenty-six boxes left.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The forty units shipped in February. An electrician the CSA hired looked over the install plan, and two growers ran the first boxes for a week and sent back notes in pencil, and Elijah fixed what they found before the rest went out. By then Reuben's yellow tab had an answer stapled to it and the folder was thick enough to prop a door.

Denny delivered them in apple boxes. Two growers needed new probes in the first month. They changed them by hand, off the card, without throwing anything away. Marta pinned the repair emails to the corkboard the way other shops hang their first dollar.

And somewhere in the middle of all that, somebody finally fixed Elijah's check-engine light.

It had been on since the drive north. A dealership had quoted him twelve hundred dollars and a week. Curtis found it in nine minutes with a fourteen-dollar gadget plugged in under the dash. A cracked vacuum line. Two dollars of hose. He did the repair while the coffee brewed, and left the rest of the hose on the dashboard.

Elijah put the hose in the glove box.

# Chapter 20: What a Tomato Costs

Lou put him on drip tape in March, and it just about killed him.

Elijah thought he knew farm work. He'd grown up on a farm. He had said so to Marta on his very first day, like it was a diploma. But what he'd actually done on his mother's place for the last twenty years was fix gate latches and eat, and Lou figured that out in about four minutes, watching him try to unroll a quarter mile of plastic tubing in a crosswind. Lou laughed so hard he had to sit down on a bucket.

"College," he said, wiping his eyes. "You look like a man trying to put pants on an octopus."

Lou was sixty-one years old. He was, as far as Elijah could tell, the only person at the co-op who had ever made a living farming, and then lost it. He'd grown pears outside Ukiah on ground his grandfather cleared. Then the packing house that bought everybody's pears got bought by a bigger one, and after that there was one buyer in the whole valley, and the one buyer named the price, and the price was less than a pear cost to grow. Lou told that story in about three sentences, the way you'd tell somebody your blood type. Now he grew vegetables for a hundred and forty households on forty acres that weren't his, and he seemed pretty happy about it.

Elijah had liked him from the start. He liked him the way you like a big dog that leans on you.

They laid tape down thirty beds that week, on their knees, in the mud. Elijah's back went out on the second day and came back on the fourth as somebody else's back. His hands cracked open at the knuckles. He was thirty-seven years old and he had never been so tired in his life, and he had once bolted two million dollars of robot arms to a floor in Stockton in August.

At noon they sat on the tailgate of Lou's truck and ate Grace's sandwiches, and Lou got out a pencil and turned over a seed box.

"Okay. Pop quiz. What's a tomato cost?"

"At the store? I don't know. A dollar?"

"Not to buy. To make."

Elijah had no idea. He was a little ashamed of that. He had a notebook with the wattage of his cable box in it.

So Lou showed him. He wrote it down the side of the box in a column. The seed, the starts, the water, the tape they'd just laid, the diesel, the boxes, the truck. Then he drew a line under all that and wrote HANDS, and the number next to HANDS was bigger than everything above it added up.

"That's the whole thing right there," Lou said, and tapped it. "That's farming. Everything else is a rounding error. It's hands. It's somebody bent over at six in the morning."

"So what do we sell it for?"

"Less than that!" Lou was delighted. "And we give a third of them away! It's the dumbest business in America. Don't tell Reuben, he thinks we're a going concern."

"Then how does it work?"

"It doesn't work, man. It's never worked. It works because nine people here don't get paid, and Grace is some kind of saint, and my wife taught fourth grade for thirty-one years and left me her pension." He took a bite and talked around it. "Every farm you ever saw is like that. There's a wife with a job in town, or a dead grandpa who paid off the ground, or twelve guys from Michoacán nobody's paying enough. Somebody is always eating the difference. You grew up on one. Who ate it at your place?"

Elijah thought about his mother, and the pot she never let anyone else wash.

"My mom," he said.

"There you go."

It was the best explanation of anything he'd gotten since he came north, and it had taken about ninety seconds and a seed box. Lou was like that. He asked you about three questions first, to find out where you were standing, and then he told you one thing.

Of course Elijah couldn't leave it alone. He was looking at the word HANDS.

"A machine could lay that tape," he said.

"Sure it could. They make a thing you pull behind the tractor. We've got one."

"Then why are we on our knees?"

"Because the tractor's broke and Curtis is in a mood." Lou balled up his sandwich paper. "I'd let a robot do every bit of this. I mean that. I've got one knee that's mine and one that's titanium, and the titanium one's the good one."

"You can't mean that."

That was Wren, who led the Sunday circle and made the candles. She had come down the row with a thermos of something that smelled like a wet lawn, and she had her hand flat on her chest.

"There's a relationship," Wren said. "Between the hand and the plant. The food knows who grew it. You can't give that to a machine, Lou. You can't automate reverence."

"Wren, honey," Lou said, "I have been in a relationship with plants for fifty years, and not one of them has ever called me back."

Elijah laughed and got mustard up his nose. Wren laughed too, a little, in spite of herself. She still looked worried, though. She looked like somebody had proposed paving the creek.

"I just think some things are meant to be done by people," she said.

"I don't think 'meant to' has anything to do with it," Elijah said, and wiped his nose. He could feel himself going, and he went. "It's coming either way. That's what I keep trying to tell everybody. Nobody gets a vote on this. It's like weather."

Lou looked at him. He had very pale eyes in all that sunburn.

"Question," Lou said. "It's coming either way?"

"Yeah."

"So it's already decided. The whole thing."

"Pretty much. I mean, I've got it written down."

"Okay. Then what are you doing on your knees in my tomatoes?"

Elijah opened his mouth, and nothing came out. He actually did not know. He had come north to do something about it. He also believed nobody could do anything about it. He had never put those two facts next to each other.

Lou grinned and slid down off the tailgate. His good knee clicked.

"Think about it," he said. "Get the other end of that roll."

That night Elijah wrote two lines in the notebook, under the names.

*What a tomato costs: hands. Somebody's always eating the difference.*

*Lou: if it's already decided, what are you doing on your knees?*

# Chapter 21: Bait

The co-op needed money the way every real thing needs money.

Not dramatically. Just constantly. In Elijah's first spring there it was Denny, of all people, who said the obvious thing out loud at a Friday dinner: "People pay for what we know. We give it away to anyone who walks into the shed. Why does the walking matter?"

So they decided to put the co-op on the internet. Elijah had been the internet once, for one winter, and a billionaire had explained attention to him on a fire escape. He volunteered to run it.

He did everything correctly. That was the problem. He studied what performed. The pacing, the thumbnails with the circled arrows, the seven-second hook, the open loops ("what we found in the soil SHOCKED us, but first").

He wrote Denny a script for the greenhouse-controller story that was, by every metric he knew how to measure, optimal. Denny read four lines of it on camera, stopped, and looked over the phone at Elijah like a man being asked to wear someone else's dentures.

"Nobody talks like this," Denny said.

"The algorithm," Elijah began.

"I'm not talking to the algorithm. I'm talking to a guy on his couch who just got the letter I got in 2019." He put the script face-down on the workbench. "Point the phone. I'm just going to say what happened."

He told it in one take, hands in frame the whole time because he talked with them, the plasma table ticking as it cooled somewhere behind him. What happened was eleven years in a warehouse, the arms coming in, the county's folder, the second ladder, the second folder.

And then the part Elijah hadn't scripted because Elijah didn't know it yet: what it was like, at fifty-one, to bolt together a machine that feeds people and feel the weight of a tool in your hand instead of a lanyard around your neck. "Everybody's asking what the machines are going to do to us," Denny said to the phone, at the end. "I spent years as the answer to that question, and it nearly killed me. So we built something. Here's ours. Come see it. Bring your kid."

Elijah posted two versions, because he was still a data scientist in his soul and you always run the A/B. One was Denny's take the way he gave it. The other was Elijah's cut of the same take, with the hook up front.

The optimized cut landed in a feed that already felt mostly machine-made to him, fake scripts read by fake voices over fake pictures. It looked like everything around it.

Forty-one views. He watched it between the other videos in his feed and heard the resemblance: the same urgent pauses, the same little promises withheld until after the next cut. He had spent two weeks trying to make a human being sound like a format.

Denny's single take, shot vertical with a thumb over the mic for the first three seconds, went slowly for a day. Then people started sending it to each other.

The comments read like testimony. Forklift drivers, paralegals, a woman who'd trained her own replacement twice. Several wrote that a friend had sent the link. One asked where to get the controller's repair card.

People wrote to the co-op's inbox too, and the inbox was Grace's. She answered every one of them herself, at the table in the kitchen trailer, with a cup of tea going cold at her elbow. He watched her do forty in a night.

"You answer everybody," he said.

"Somebody has to."

He got a second chair and took the ones about the repair card.

The next morning Elijah watched the numbers climb for a while, and then, Sal's rule, closed the laptop and went to find Denny, who was in the shed fitting lids.

"It's working," Elijah said. "The real one. Not mine."

"Course not yours." Denny didn't look up from the bench. "You tried to make bait." He fitted another lid. "I put out food."

They made it official at the next Friday dinner. The channel was Denny's. Elijah ran the pipes: the posting, the backups, the links to the build notes riding under every video like cargo under a truck.

The voice and the face and the say-so were Denny Osei's. The co-op wrote down how the money would be shared and how members could keep their lives out of the footage. Denny could say no to the camera any day he wanted.

That evening he recorded a short correction to the repair card. The first version had named the wrong terminal. No hook, no music, no suspense. He pointed to the screw and said which one he meant. Elijah sent the corrected card directly to every grower who'd received the first version.

# Chapter 22: The Crucible

The servers arrived in mud season, in the back of Denny's truck.

There were three of them, retired machines from a county surplus auction, five years old, built like filing cabinets, sold for less than the co-op had spent that month on welding gas.

Elijah had argued for the purchase all winter, and the argument that finally landed wasn't technical.

"Every question we ask the cloud," he said at a Friday dinner, "tells somebody what we're building, what we're worried about, what we don't know yet. We're mailing our diary to the company store, one page at a time, and paying the postage."

Marta cared about one number, watts, and he had that answer ready, because a $211 power bill had taught it to him before anyone here knew his name. Priya wanted help searching the plant-disease references she already trusted. Reuben wanted a first pass through forty pages of easement language. Tyler wanted to see if it could do what Rod Code did. Curtis wanted nothing to do with any of it, and said so.

The rack went together the way everything in the shed went together: Marta on steel and power, Elijah on software, everyone else on opinions. A salvaged Civic radiator bolted to the shady north wall. Two brass fittings through the corrugated steel. A marine pump the size of a fist.

On the first cold boot, with the fans screaming like weather, they loaded the model. It was a descendant of Silvana, free for anyone to download, three generations down from the thing that had started all of this. It was small enough now to live on scavenged hardware. It could suggest wording, find a likely bug, or turn a confusing manual into questions somebody could check.

Elijah sat with that for a minute in the dark of the shed.

*[Figure: A stack of three scavenged rack-mount servers beside a salvaged car radiator with brass pipe fittings]*

*The Crucible. Three filing cabinets and a salvaged radiator.*

For five weeks it was purely good. Reuben fed it easements and it found a cross-reference he'd missed. A student of Priya's learned blight triage from it in an afternoon, then caught it inventing a source, and wrote that on the board beside the wins. It never got tired and it never billed and it never, not once, phoned home, because there was no home to phone.

He started calling the rack the Crucible. Priya said that was fine as long as the things that didn't survive testing stayed in the log.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The text came on a Thursday, at two in the afternoon. It was from David.

*Dad died Tuesday night. In his sleep, they think. Service is Sunday at eleven, St. Rose.*

There was nothing after it. It did not ask him to come.

*I'll be there,* he typed, and sent it.

He stood in the gravel for a while after. A club soda with a lime. *I had one of those. Mine was horses.* He had not talked to David since the morning with the hand truck, seven months back. He had a text that said *Saw the video with the warehouse guy. He's good,* and he'd put a thumb on it, which was David's own move, and had felt cheap doing it.

He would drive down Sunday at seven. He would be gone a whole day, and he was the only one who watched the machines.

So on Friday night he did fifteen minutes of work. He wanted his dashboards on his phone for the drive: uptime, coolant temperature, the channel numbers. A tunnel out, a gate in front of the co-op's services. He was tired and he was thinking about what you say to a man whose father has died when you haven't sat at his bar in a year and a half. While chasing a bug he switched off the line that asked for a password, two slashes, just to rule it out. He meant to put it back.

He didn't put it back.

What the gate served, to anyone on earth who looked, was the journal. The plain-text nervous system of nineteen households. Maintenance logs. Meeting minutes. The duty roster. The CSA customer list, with addresses. Priya's greenhouse alarms. Marta's physical therapy schedule, Tuesdays, for the shoulder she never mentioned. Which houses stood empty on which afternoons, for anyone who could read a power log.

All of it, public, from Friday night to Sunday morning.

It was one of Denny's subscribers who caught it, a network engineer four states away. The message came in Sunday morning at twenty to seven. Elijah was standing by the truck in his one good shirt with the keys in his hand.

*you probably want to lock your journal, brother*

He killed the gate first.

Then he pulled the logs, sitting on the running board, and did the math he would have paid anything not to do. Three crawlers had been through. He could delete it from his own server. He could not delete what they had copied.

He looked at the truck. It was three hours to St. Rose.

He thought about keeping it to himself. Then he pictured Marta finding out some other way. Then he went and rang the dinner bell, on a Sunday morning, which the co-op did not do, and stood in his funeral shirt in front of everyone it brought, and read them the timestamps.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Marta did not raise her voice. Everyone in the shed understood that to be the worst available outcome.

"You published which houses stand empty between two and four on a Tuesday," she said. "At the plant we had lockout. You don't put your arm in a press until the power's off and locked off and your own name is on the lock."

She stopped. The color had gone out of her face, all at once, like someone had opened a drain. She put her hand on the bench.

"You put our hands in the press, college."

"I know exactly what that list is for," Grace said.

It was the other voice, the one she used on trailer hitches. She had never once pointed it at him. She was standing with her arms crossed over her apron and she wasn't looking at his face.

"A list of which houses are empty. I've seen what gets done with that. I've seen it done in a week." She shook her head. "God damn it, Elijah."

Curtis said his piece, and for once it had teeth. This is what happens. You brought their machine inside the wire. Pull the plug and melt it down. Heads were nodding. Marta let them nod for a moment.

"The first family that ever kept fire burned something down," Marta said. Her color was coming back. "A roof. A winter store. Somebody's child. And nobody handed the fire back. They built the hearth. Stones in a circle, rules about who tends it, a bucket that never goes empty." She looked at Elijah, and it was not a rescue. "We didn't get burned by the model, Curtis. We got burned by a boy who left it lit and unwatched."

Priya, who had been quiet, set down her cup. "Pulling the model out won't change which door he left open."

"He built it," Curtis said.

"Yes. And we let one person change the thing that held all our addresses. We never asked whether all that needed to be in one place. Then we took his confidence for a check." She looked at Elijah. "You owe us the truth about what happened. We owe ourselves a setup that doesn't depend on you never having a bad Friday."

Nobody asked why his Friday had been bad. He didn't tell them. It wasn't a reason.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Grace and Reuben wrote to every family on the list, by hand, at the kitchen table. It took them two days. "We're not waiting for somebody to prove we owe them the truth," Reuben said. Grace didn't say anything. She wrote.

It was Denny who found the judo in it.

"People already know things break," he said. "What they never once get to see is somebody stand up and say so."

So they put an account of it on the channel. What kind of information got out, for how long, who'd been told, what was changing. Elijah read it to the camera himself, because it was his name on the lock. It traveled further than anything they'd posted except Denny's first video. Strangers wrote to say it was the first report of its kind they'd ever read that hadn't been written by a lawyer. Two of them drove out that summer to help lock the network down, for free.

The co-op adopted a rule that Sunday. Nothing got opened to the outside without a second person checking what an outsider could see. Two sets of eyes. Marta said she was borrowing it from the plant.

The Crucible came off the outside network for good. After that, updates arrived by hand, on a drive carried home in Elijah's jacket.

That night Grace found him on the pump house steps. He'd figured she was done with him. She sat down a step below and didn't look at him right away.

"You rang the bell yourself," she said. It was the soft voice again, tired. "Most people wait to be found."

"I should've been at a funeral today."

"Whose?"

He told her. She listened to all of it. When he finished she put her hand on his forearm, the way she did with everyone, and then she left it there, which she did not.

On Monday he sent flowers, a day late, with a card he rewrote four times. He texted David that he was sorry. David didn't answer.

At 9:40 the next Sunday he called the rose garden.

"She's been looking at that door all day," the aide said. "Her Sunday company didn't come." He took it to mean the church. She held the phone by his mother's ear.

He told her Ray McGrath had died, and that he had not gone down. He didn't know if the name still meant anything to her.

"Barry?" she said.

The pump kept ticking. The radiator clicked against the north wall. The machine went on drafting and checking, with nobody outside able to see it.

# Chapter 23: Forty Acres

The first letter was addressed to OWNER/OCCUPANT.

Irene Calder read it on her porch and threw it away, which is also what she did with the second one. The third letter came with a young man attached.

Everything the co-op had built stood on Calder ground. The shed had been Walt Calder's packing shed. The beds ran where his hay had run. The lease was month to month and had been since Walt died. Irene liked Marta and had never raised the rent, and nobody had wanted to disturb an arrangement that got worked out over pie. The young man on the porch offered twenty-three percent over anything the county said forty acres was worth, all cash, a fourteen-day close, and a flyer with a sunset on it. Irene told him no. Irene's son Dale, in Phoenix, said, "Mom."

Elijah did what he always did when he was frightened. He pulled data. The county recorder's index was public, ancient, and slow, so he was polite to it, scraping at night, and then he did two neighboring counties for context. The pattern assembled itself the way patterns do, all at once, on the third evening.

Eleven different company names, one registered agent, a mail-forwarding address in Sacramento. Fifty-some parcels in two years, always the same shape: an estate or a widow, a cash offer, a fast close. Then the parcel goes quiet inside a shell company for a year or two and reappears stitched into a block big enough to matter, leased to whoever needs flat land, water rights, and no neighbors with opinions.

Reuben, reading over his shoulder, reminded him that a registered agent can represent a hundred unrelated clients. Elijah colored the uncertain links a different shade and kept going.

He knew one of the eleven names already. It took him until the fourth evening to go out to the truck. His own closing papers were in the glove box, under Curtis's hose, where he had put them the day he signed. Three letters and an LLC. Same agent. Three hours apart, the same signature. He sat in the cab with the dome light on. It had not stopped at the Hollis place. He had sold them her kitchen. He circled the link for Reuben to check, and did not tell Reuben how he came to have it.

He built a map. He built, God help him, a dashboard, and it was beautiful. He presented it at Friday dinner with a laser pointer. This time he heard himself doing it and grinned in the middle of a sentence, and the room grinned back.

But the dashboard did its one real job. In the parcels he could trace, the time from first letter to recorded deed ran about nine weeks, and Irene had gotten her first letter five weeks ago. The data couldn't change that. It could only tell them how little time they had.

"It's not a data problem," Reuben said, already dealing folders around the table like cards. "It's a people problem. Fortunately," and here he almost smiled, "I have been waiting my entire adult life for this exact fight. My grandfather lost sixty acres to a signature he couldn't read."

What he dealt out was a community land trust. The trust would own the ground, and it could never sell to a string of companies with one mailing address. Irene would stay in her house for the rest of her life and get a check every month. The co-op would get a lease long enough to plant trees on. Reuben had also brought the less thrilling pages: taxes, insurance, and what happened if they ever missed a payment.

The down payment would take the channel's first real surplus and the greenhouse-controller money. Future payments would have to come from future work. Denny looked from one column to the other. He tapped the second column. "And then we have to keep the promise."

"Every month," Reuben said.

Dale attended the kitchen-table meeting by speakerphone, from Phoenix, and he was not wrong by his own lights. "Their number is higher, Mom. That's just math."

"It's a bigger number," Reuben said to the phone where it lay on the oilcloth, next to the coffee and the folders. "There's a machine that's been running since the fifteen hundreds, Dale. It turns commons into fences. Every century it gets a new name. Enclosure. Consolidation. Urban renewal. This week it's a string of companies with one mailing address and a flyer with a sunset on it. It runs in one direction, and it has never run in your mother's favor. What's on this table is that machine in reverse." He let that sit. "Your mother gets to take the bigger number seriously. She also gets to decide what she wants to keep. It's her porch, Dale. It's her choice. That's why we brought the folders."

Lou had come along and stood by the door with his hat in his hands. He had lost his pears outside Ukiah to one buyer and one price. He hadn't said a word all afternoon.

There was a long quiet on the line, the box fan working, Walt's cap on its peg by the door. Irene had listened to all of it with both hands around her cup, and what she finally asked was not about the resale formula.

"Marta. Walt's tractor, back when. What did you charge him?"

"Sixty," Marta said. "It was the fuel line, not the pump. Dealer wanted nine hundred."

"And it still runs," Irene said, to the phone, to Dale, to the table generally, in the tone of a woman entering evidence. Then she drew the folders toward her. "Leave these. I want my questions written down before anybody signs."

By the door, Lou put his hat back on.

Grace went over the next afternoon and sat on the porch with her for four hours. She didn't bring a folder. She brought a pie she hadn't made and said so. Irene Calder did not want to leave her house. Elijah drove by twice on errands he'd invented. The second time they were both laughing, and Grace had her hand on Irene's arm.

The review took longer than Elijah wanted. Irene asked questions he hadn't thought to put in the dashboard. So did Dale. By the time they agreed, several pages had changed. Reuben carried the completed documents to the county himself, and Doreen at the recorder's window stamped them at 11:04 on a Tuesday morning. The letters from Sacramento kept coming for a month. Irene brought one to dinner and left it unopened beside the salt.

The dashboard had been right about the pattern. It had not closed the deal.

Elijah wrote the number in the notebook anyway, under Sal's rule, where the important numbers lived.

*PARCELS HELD IN TRUST: 1.*

# Meanwhile

Aaron Bidwell liked a map. A map was the one thing he would look at with his own eyes.

This one covered a wall in an office he used four days a year. Three counties north of the bay, in pale green, and over the green a spread of red parcels, fifty-three of them, growing toward each other like a rash.

In the middle of the biggest patch there was a hole.

"What's that?"

The reader had a page ready. The reader always had a page ready for the thing he was going to point at.

"Forty acres. It was a widow named Calder. We made three offers. The last was twenty-three percent over."

"And?"

"She put it in a trust."

"So we buy the trust."

"It's the kind you can't buy. It's written so it can never be sold to… well. To us. Specifically. There's language."

Bidwell walked up to the wall and looked at the hole from about a foot away, as if it might be a printing error.

"Who writes language like that for a widow?"

"A man named Vance. He does it for free. And there's a welder."

"A what?"

"A welder. She fixed the husband's tractor for sixty dollars, and it appears to have been decisive."

Bidwell turned around.

"I have eleven companies up there."

"Yes, sir."

"I have the dairy down south. I have the water on both sides."

"Yes, sir."

"And I'm losing to a widow and a welder."

"And nineteen households. They make a kind of greenhouse box. They have a video channel." The reader hesitated. "The farm boy is there. The one from the dinner. He landed on that parcel last fall."

Bidwell thought about being angry. It was a lot of effort and it had never once improved a map.

"What are they doing on it?"

"Writing things down. Soil, water, who gets fed. They're very thorough. They keep it all in one place."

"Hm."

He went back to the wall and put one finger on the hole, lightly.

"We wait," he said. "They're doing the collecting for me. Let them finish."

# Chapter 24: First Fire

The containers came up the hill on a tilt-bed in the last week of August, one at a time.

There were two of them, forty-footers, still wearing their ocean stickers, and the driver set them down on Marta's piers with a gentleness that seemed impossible for eighteen thousand pounds of steel.

The piers had been poured three weeks earlier, and Elijah had checked their levels so many times that Marta finally took the tubing away from him. "They're right," she said. "You watched me make them right. Trust the transit or go ask the internet." She had told him that if the piers were five millimeters out, the boxes would twist and the cut would never line up. The piers were off by one millimeter.

It went up on the co-op's lease, on the worn patch where Walt Calder had once parked a combine, and every cut was photographed and every dimension logged, because the deal the co-op had made with itself was that nothing got built only once.

But it was also Elijah's. Nobody made a ceremony of saying so. He'd had rented rooms and borrowed corners and a bed over a pump house, and now he had a rectangle of dirt that was his.

"What the fuck is a shouse?" he'd asked, the first time he saw it on the whiteboard.

"Shop. House," Marta said. "A shop that's set up to keep the person running it alive."

He had wanted to weld on it. He had wanted it badly, after nearly a year of watching Marta lay beads like the steel was agreeing with her. He'd practiced evenings for a month on scrap, and his practice pieces looked, he thought, honestly good.

He asked for a seam on the header beam, the long spine that would carry the roof across the cut. Marta didn't say no. She said, "Bring me your coupon," and put his best practice weld in the big bench vise, and slipped a length of pipe over the free end, and broke it with one pull.

The bead snapped clean down its own centerline. Inside, the break was bright and grainy and full of small round holes, like honeycomb cast in nickel.

*[Figure: A heavy steel bench vise clamping a broken metal weld test coupon, the fracture bright and visible]*

*The vise. The metal does the truth.*

"See the sparkle," she said. It wasn't a question. "It sat on top of the steel and never became the steel. From six feet it looks like mine." She set the two halves on the bench where he could take them. "There's nothing wrong with you, college. You're a couple hundred hours and a certification test from a structural bead, and the rain is eight weeks out. And this seam," she put her glove flat on the beam, "holds a roof over someone's kid. I don't weld those seams tired, and I don't let anybody weld them proud."

He kept the two halves. After that he welded one coupon a month and brought it to her, and she broke it, and he kept those too.

So Marta welded the spine of his house, and Elijah went to his lane, which turned out to be real work he was good at. Before the insulation went in, he laced the walls with temperature sensors, so the shell could report on itself for the rest of its life. He put leak sensors low in the wet room. He built the little dashboard the whole co-op used after that, the one that showed a floor plan with a nervous system, and a blank spot wherever a sensor stopped talking.

Marta gave the finished panel a long look and said, "Huh."

The day of the cut, Denny ran the plasma torch, and the inside walls came out in glowing rectangles, and light went through the two boxes and made them one room. Sixteen feet of open span where no room had been.

Everyone stood in it. Sixteen feet by forty isn't a big space, and every person standing in it had lived in bigger.

The stove went in last. The Fisher had sat in the corner of the shed for a year. They bought the new firebrick. They bought the chimney new, all of it, the one line in the budget nobody would let him salvage.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The first cold night came in the last week of September.

He laid the fire the way his grandmother's hands had taught his hands, before he was tall enough to see the top of the stove. The draw caught on the first match. The steel began to tick as it heated.

People drifted over without being organized. Reuben brought chairs. Lou brought a sack of walnuts and cracked them two at a time in one fist. Somebody set a drying rack well back from the stove and hung a pair of socks on it. Tyler sat on the floor. Curtis stood with his back to the heat and his hands behind him and looked, for Curtis, content.

Elijah watched them from beside the stove. It was his room, and they were all in it, and he caught himself making a list of what he would do differently if the place were his to run. He put that away. It didn't stay put.

Grace came. She stayed by the door. She had a mug in both hands and she leaned on the frame the whole evening, laughing at the right places, a good fifteen feet from the stove. He noticed where she stood.

He took one photograph from inside. The stove lit, the socks, the new brick glowing through the gap in the door, the dashboard on the wall behind it showing green. It was a Sunday. At 9:40 he stepped out under the eave and called the rose garden.

The aide held the phone by her ear. He could hear the radio.

"I lit the Fisher, Ma," he said. "It's got a room now. The pipe's double-wall. Insulated through the roof. The inspector signed it Tuesday." The Hendersons had a chimney fire in '88, and his grandmother had talked about it for ten years, and his mother for thirty after that.

It was quiet on the line for a bit, with the radio under it.

"Barry?" she said.

He wrote it in the notebook before he went back in. Under *PARCELS HELD IN TRUST: 1,* a new line.

*ROOFS: 1.*

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The next Sunday he drove down and held the picture where she could see it. "It looks like somewhere, Ma," he said. She looked at it a long time. Then her good hand came up off the blanket, and she put one finger on the stove.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

By eleven everyone had gone but Grace.

She was still in the doorway. He was banking the fire. He didn't say anything about it, and after a while she came in, and took the chair Reuben had left, and set it down about eight feet back.

"I haven't sat this close to one in six years," she said.

He waited.

"I worked for a fire district. Before here. I wasn't on an engine, I was the one they sent ahead. When the wind was coming, I drove the roads with a clipboard and knocked on every door and told them it was time." She turned the mug around in her hands. "Most people go. Some don't. There was a man at the end of a road, older, lived by himself, had a nice place. I told him. He was polite. He said, 'My house, my choice.'"

The stove ticked.

"So I wrote REFUSED on the sheet. That's what you write. And I tied a piece of flagging tape on his gate, so the crews would know somebody had been there and he'd been told. And I went to the next door, because there were forty more on that road." She looked at the fire, finally, straight at it. "That's what the tape is. Somebody checked."

"Grace."

"It came through that night. When they let us back up, the tape was still on the gate. It was about the only thing on that road that was still a color."

He didn't say he was sorry. He sat down on the floor by the stove where Tyler had been.

"I was married to a captain," she said. "Good man. He had a line. I'd come home from a day like that and he'd say, 'You can't make them leave, Grace.' Every time. He thought it was comfort." She shook her head. "I quit the job and I left him inside of one month. I couldn't tell you which one I was leaving."

"He wasn't wrong, though."

"No. He wasn't wrong." She said it flatly. "I still left."

Elijah put a split of oak in and shut the door on it. Then he told her about the Bear Flag. He'd never told the whole of it to anyone up here. He told her about the laptop and the napkin, and the hand on his shoulder a week later, and three glasses in the air.

"Three people," he said. "A coordinator, a marketing person, a kid with a camera. I never even found out their names."

"You showed them something true," Grace said. "And they chose."

"You knocked. And he chose."

They both heard it. He looked at her, and she looked at him, a little too long.

She looked away first. She laughed.

"God. We're fun."

He told her the other one too. He had not meant to. The two pages, the room with the window, the man with the folder.

"Did you ask her?" Grace said.

"She asked for somebody named Barry."

Grace looked at the stove for a while.

"I'd have signed," she said. "I'd have signed in the parking lot."

"I met a guy," he said, to be saying something. "In the city. He makes a lamp that talks to you. You come home and it says it missed you. It asks about your day. Four million people bought one."

She started to laugh at that, and it quit halfway.

"So nobody has to come home to a dark house," she said.

"That's the pitch."

"That's not the worst thing I've ever heard."

He thought about a small woman at a sink. *The part where it is kind.* He didn't say it. It was warm, and it was late, and Grace picked up her chair and moved it a foot closer to the stove.

# Chapter 25: Gradually

Every controller the co-op ever shipped had parts from Kowalski's in it. Whenever anybody in the shed said *from town,* the counter they meant was Frank's. It was the third bay off the square, with wood worn pale where four generations of forearms had rested on it and a bell over the door that Frank's grandfather had hung. Frank could tell you the torque spec for a head bolt from memory, and he would carry a two-dollar part out to your truck like it was an appliance.

*[Figure: An older hardware-store owner wearing glasses and a canvas work apron]*

*Frank Kowalski. Knows the torque spec from memory.*

Elijah watched the store die for nine months before it died, and said nothing.

He hadn't meant to be watching. He kept the co-op's purchasing ledger, and the ledger noticed before he did. Substitution emails, out-of-stocks on things that had never been out of stock, the fastener aisle developing gaps that stayed gaps.

He pulled the thread, because he could never leave one alone. Frank's regional distributor had been bought by a logistics platform out of Memphis, the kind with a two-letter name and no phone number. The new owner had raised minimum orders past what a single-counter store could float and rerouted the trucks to its own warehouses. It was letting the small end of its customer list wither on schedule.

The chart Elijah built showed the co-op's own orders drifting away from the square, to salvage yards and online, one substitution at a time.

The hours sign got re-taped twice. Tyler's shifts at the register went from five days to three, and then to Saturdays, and then Frank told him in the fastener aisle that he couldn't keep him. Tyler came out to the shed the next morning like it was any other day.

He said nothing, and he had his reasons. He'd been the man with the printout once, at a coffee machine on the fourth floor, and a calm senior colleague had smiled at him the way you smile at a child. He'd spent three years learning what it costs to say the thing early, and the lesson he'd accidentally taken was: don't.

And besides, what was the sentence? *Frank, my chart says you're dying?* Frank knew his own store. Surely Frank knew.

The paper on the door, when it came, was laminated. Which meant Frank had known long enough to laminate it.

The same week, Elijah got an email with the subject line "checking in," from Devendra. Claypot had cut the whole division. The Companion team and every human who had been augmented, not replaced, gone in one memo.

On the phone Devendra kept starting a sentence, pausing, and starting somewhere else. "You'll appreciate this," Devendra said, with a laugh that wasn't one. "They kept the very good autocomplete." And then, quietly, the real question: "At the coffee machine. You knew. How?"

"I didn't know," Elijah said. He almost followed it with the arithmetic, with you told me I'd calibrate, with all the sentences that would turn another man's lost job into his own vindication. He looked at Frank's closing notice on the screen and let them pass. "I was frightened. I still am sometimes. What do you need this week?"

Devendra didn't answer immediately. Then he asked about the place to stay that Elijah had mentioned once, and whether he could bring the kids.

"Bring them," Elijah said.

"I owe you a dinner."

"Not till Thanksgiving. You've got two years."

Devendra laughed, short, a real one. He said he'd think about it.

He thought about it for a year. He stayed in his house in San Jose with the news on, and for most of that year he was Gary. Elijah called him on Sundays, after his mother. Some Sundays he picked up.

Elijah told Grace that night, at the kitchen trailer. All of it, the ledger and the nine months. She stopped stirring.

"You knew since spring and you didn't knock?"

"I didn't want to be the crackpot again."

"So you let him go through it by himself." She pointed the spoon at him. "You knock, Elijah. He gets to slam it in your face. That part's his. But you knock."

She went back to the pot. He thought about a gate with a strip of orange tape on it, and didn't argue.

In the morning he made the harder visit, the two-mile one. He drove to the square with his chart, parked, sat in the truck long enough for the windows to fog, and walked in through the bell.

Frank was taping boxes. Elijah put the chart on the worn wood and confessed to it. Nine months, the distributor, the platform, the curve, all of it.

Frank looked at the chart for a while. "Fella in Hemingway gets asked how he went bankrupt," he said, taping another box. "Says: two ways. Gradually, then suddenly." He set the tape gun down. "The gradually part, I kept telling myself it would turn around." He tapped the curve's long slow shoulder, the nine visible months of it. "Wouldn't have saved the store, son. The store was dead the day Memphis bought my distributor. But I'd have liked to have had this in March. I'd have chosen some things, instead of hoping them."

At Friday dinner Elijah said it all again, chart and confession both, in front of everyone, because that was how the co-op stayed healthy and he trusted it now. There was one thing he had not said at that table, and it was not about Frank. Denny gave the verdict, and it had edges. "You of all people," he said. "You watched a man get optimized out by a spreadsheet he never saw, and you had the spreadsheet." Then, because Denny's justice always had a second half: "So fix it forward. What's the co-op do about Frank?"

What the co-op did about Frank began with an offer, and Frank sent Reuben's first draft back with questions. They settled on the inventory the tool library could use, bought at honest wholesale, not vulture pennies, and a paid job. The tool library had been running on a clipboard and an honor system. It turned out what it needed was a man who knew where every last thing was.

Then Frank hung his grandfather's bell over the tool library door. The first time it rang for a member checking out equipment, he looked up from the counter before he could help it.

The parts still came from suppliers beyond the valley. Frank still knew which ones.

*[Figure: A brass bell mounted above a worn wooden hardware-store door frame]*

*The bell. Frank's grandfather hung it over the door.*

Elijah put the chart he'd sat on for nine months on the shelf in the shouse, beside the broken weld coupons. He didn't throw any of it away.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

That was the night she knocked.

It was late, after the Friday table had been broken down. One knock. Then the latch, and Grace was standing inside his door with her coat still on and a jar in her hand, looking like she had surprised herself.

"I didn't wait," she said.

"No."

"I brought soup. I don't know why I brought soup. You're fine."

"I'm fine."

"I know you are." She set the jar on the table. "You went and told him. Frank. You walked in there with the chart."

"You told me to."

"I tell everybody to." She sat down in Reuben's chair, on the far side of the room from the stove, and finally unbuttoned the coat. "Nobody does."

He put a split of oak in and shut the door on it. He did not go and sit by her. He sat down on the floor where he was, the way he had the first night, and after a while she picked up the chair and brought it over.

Her truck was still in the gravel in the morning. Curtis walked past it on his way to the shop and found something to look at in the other direction.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Tyler got the bench by the window. He had lost a register and gained forty hours a week, and he spent them building a computer into a steel ammo can. A seven-inch screen. A keyboard off a dead laptop. The battery from a cordless drill. It ran on nine watts, and it could talk to the Crucible from anywhere on the forty acres without touching the internet.

"What is it?" Elijah said.

"It's a cyberdeck."

Elijah had a page in an old notebook about a machine like that. Something small, something his, that ran on watts he could count on his fingers. He had never had a name for it. Here it was in an ammo can, and a twenty-year-old had the name.

Frank came over from the tool library counter to look, wiping his glasses on his apron.

"When I was his age we put a man on the moon with less computer than that," Frank said. "And every kid on my street could adjust a TV antenna."

"Frank," Denny said, from the next bench, not looking up. "I'm sorry Tyler wasn't around when they put a man on the moon. But right now he's learning the skills to put himself on the moon. He doesn't need a lecture about how kids these days can't adjust a TV antenna. He needs your help and your patience while he builds a goddamn cyberdeck." He blew dust off a lid. "You know how to solder. You know why the fuse blows. Nobody in his phone knows that."

Frank looked at Denny. He looked at the ammo can. He bent down and looked closer, and made a small unhappy noise.

"Son, these joints are cold." He folded his glasses into his apron pocket and held out his hand. "Give me the iron."

Tyler gave him the iron.

# Chapter 26: Thirty Centimeters

The beds went in on the worst ground the forty acres had.

It was the quarter acre behind the shed where Walt Calder had parked equipment for four decades, packed to a hardpan that rang under a shovel like a sidewalk. Priya chose it on purpose.

"Anyone can garden in good dirt," she said. "The book we're writing needs the parking lot."

Not all of it, though. At the far end, where a tractor had bled hydraulic fluid through the Clinton administration, she drove four stakes and ran a string between them. "Not that end. That end gets a lab test and somebody who does this for a living before anybody plants a radish. The people eating this can't be the experiment."

That spring she built her trial like the scientist she had never stopped being. Twelve beds on the old pad. Six she broke through the pan with a broadfork, buried in deep mulch, and seeded with vetch and rye. Six got only the mulch. Everything else the same.

Elijah walked the pad with her the day she laid it out, and for once in that shed he knew something nobody had taught him there.

"You'll get standing water here," he said, and scuffed a low spot with his boot. "And there. It's packed in plates. It'll hold rain like a dish till June and then go to brick. We had ground like this at home. You don't fork it wet. You'll just polish it."

Priya looked at the spot. She took the pencil from behind her ear and wrote it down.

It was a small thing. He thought about it for a week.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The robot was Tyler's idea, and Marta's rails, and Elijah's sensors.

It was a gantry, a steel bridge that rode on two tracks down the length of a bed, with an arm that could come down anywhere in it. It could drop a seed, water one plant and skip the next, and find a weed by looking. They built it from free plans off the internet and what was in the drawer. It was not fast. It crossed a bed like an old man crossing a street.

They got it running on a Saturday in May. It rolled out over the lettuce in the evening light, with the solar panels behind it and the vetch in flower and Priya's string still up around the bad end, and it lowered its arm and watered exactly one seedling.

Tyler stood there with his cyberdeck under his arm.

"It's solarpunk," he said.

"It's what?"

"Solarpunk. It's like." He waved at all of it. "The future, but there's gardens. And the robots are nice. And nobody's a dick about it."

Elijah had never heard the word. He looked at the thing crossing the lettuce. He had spent more than three years telling people what was coming, and he had never once thought to tell them it might look like this.

"Huh," he said.

Not everybody thought it was solarpunk.

The opinions came in the way they always came in up there, sideways, at the wash station, while everybody had their arms in cold water up to the elbow. It was the first big lettuce cut of the year. There were nine of them on the line and four hundred heads to get boxed by noon.

Wren said it made her sad to look at. She said it very gently, the way she said everything. She said food carried the feeling of whoever raised it, and she didn't know what an arm felt.

Curtis said he didn't want anything in that field that could see.

"It's got a camera on it, Lou," Curtis said. "Today it's looking at lettuce."

"What do you think it's going to look at tomorrow? Your basement?"

"I'm just saying. Today."

Irene Calder had driven her golf cart over special. She watched the thing water one seedling and skip the next, and she said Walt would have sold a kidney for it. "He spent forty years on a tractor seat watering the weeds right along with the corn. He'd have named it."

Lou said he'd marry it if it ever learned to pick. Tyler said give him a year.

It was a good argument, and Elijah stayed out of it for almost ten minutes, which was a personal best.

"It doesn't actually matter if we like it," he said. He had a head of lettuce in each hand. "That's the part nobody wants to hear. It's coming whether we like it or not. This one's slow and dumb. The next one won't be. We don't get to decide."

"There he goes," said Curtis.

Lou flicked water at him.

"Question. Is it coming to this field whether we like it or not?"

"Well. No. We built that one."

"Could we take it down?"

"Sure. It's like eight bolts."

"So who decided?"

"We did. Okay. But I mean in general. Out there. The big picture."

"I've never farmed in general," Lou said. "I've only ever farmed right here." He dropped a head in the box. "You keep telling us it's all decided, and then you keep getting up at five in the morning to decide stuff. I'm not saying you're wrong, college. I'm saying pick one."

"I would like it written down," Wren said, "that I didn't decide."

"So noted," said Reuben, who wasn't even on the line and had only come down for the coffee.

They got all four hundred heads out by noon, arguing the whole way, and it was one of the better mornings Elijah ever had up there.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

On June 12 the big models went dark.

It was in his notebook. He'd written it at a kitchen table three years before. *2. The Panic and the Plug.* Somebody had found a way to make one of the best ones write the kind of code that breaks into things. Washington told the labs their top models now needed a license to cross a border, and the labs couldn't sort out who was foreign fast enough, so they switched them off for everybody. Worldwide. On a Friday.

Rod Code stopped in the middle of a sentence.

The Crucible didn't. It sat against the north wall with its pump ticking. It wasn't connected to anything outside the shed.

Tyler lasted two days. On the third, Elijah found him at the bench with the gantry's firmware open, and his face was gray.

"There's a bug in the homing. It's my code. I mean it's *my* code, I shipped it." He scrolled, and scrolled back. "I can't read it. I told Patricia I read all of it. I read it like you read the terms on an app."

Elijah knew that face.

He pulled a stool over.

"Okay. Top of the file. What's this line do?"

"I don't know."

"Me neither. Let's find out."

They read it line by line. It took ten days. Elijah came by the bench at eight every morning with two coffees, and they did one function before noon, and at noon they stopped and ate. Tyler filled half a notebook. By the second week he was finding things before Elijah did.

Patricia called Grace on day nine. Her grant reports were due to the state on the thirtieth, and she was very upset, and she did not say why the reports were suddenly a problem. Grace didn't ask. She asked Tyler if the Crucible could help a friend. Tyler ran them overnight and drove the thumb drive two valleys over himself.

He never said a word about what he'd figured out. He was twenty. He just put it away.

On June 30 they turned the models back on. *3. The Adults Step In.* Elijah wrote the two dates next to the two lines and looked at them for a while. He had been right. Nobody at the co-op much cared. There were tomatoes.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The pay finally went to the table that summer, a year and a half late.

Grace told him herself. She told him on a Saturday morning, in his kitchen, wearing his shirt. Nobody at the Friday table had said one word about where she slept now.

"It passed," she said. "At half."

"Half."

"Half of what I told you. Curtis wanted to know why you and not Denny. Priya said we should sit with it. It was two hours, Elijah, I'm sorry. I thought I could bring them."

"It's fine."

"It's not fine."

"It's fine. It's more than I'm worth up here. I sweep."

She didn't answer that. She put her hand on his arm and looked at him, and she could tell exactly how not fine it was, and he let her think it was about the money.

Reuben cut the first check that Friday. Elijah put it in the back of a notebook and did not cash it. He knew whose voice he would hear if he did. He did the same with the next one.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

In August he brought Priya his conclusion. He brought it gently, because he liked her and believed he was about to cost her something. The dashboard showed the two sets of beds tracking the same on moisture, week after week.

"I can't find your effect," he said. "I'm sorry. Whatever the forking is doing, I can't measure it."

Priya looked at his printout the way Marta had once looked at his renders. Then she handed him the shovel.

August, mid-afternoon, no shade worth the name. She had him dig two pits, one in a plain bed and one in a forked bed, square-sided, knee deep. She made him do it slowly, a spade-width at a time, and tell her what his arms felt.

The plain bed was honest about itself. Four inches of nice crumb where the mulch had rotted in, then dust, then at ten inches the old pan, pale as concrete, with roots turning sideways along the top of it like water hitting glass.

The forked bed broke differently. The spade went in with his boot weight and not his whole body. Dark crumb went on down past where the other pit had gone to dust. At two spade-depths the soil was cool. It held its shape when he squeezed it and broke clean when he poked it, and it was full of channels going down, worm and root. Roots at thirty centimeters, white and branching. A worm, indignant, at thirty-five.

"Now," Priya said, sitting on the edge of the bed like it was a lab bench, "tell me where your probe lives."

July had cooked the top inch of every bed the same, and his dashboard had taken that one reading and spread it in all directions.

"Your sensor lives at one centimeter," Priya said. "The root lives at thirty. You measured the part that's easiest to reach and assumed the rest agreed with it. Everybody does. Whole nations do." She nodded at the pit. "Roosevelt wrote to every governor in the country in 1937. *The nation that destroys its soil destroys itself.* Every extension office I ever worked in had that framed on the wall, and every budget I ever sat through funded the crust anyway."

He sat in the dirt with his ruined conclusion and started laughing. First the vise, and now the shovel.

The fix cost about twelve dollars a bed: probes at three depths on a length of plastic pipe, five, fifteen and thirty centimeters, checked against the shovel. Priya made him keep doing that part monthly. Pit and squeeze and smell, on the first of the month, "so the instruments stay honest and so do you."

*[Figure: A white PVC pipe soil-moisture probe with three depth-marked sensor leads]*

*The probe. Five, fifteen, thirty centimeters.*

By September the deep probes were telling a different story than the surface ones. At thirty centimeters, the forked beds held water days longer after each watering. Elijah wanted to call it proof. Priya wrote it down in pencil.

"One field, one season, and your watering wasn't equal," she said. "It's a reason to run it again next year, properly. It's not a recipe."

By October he could read a handful of soil by squeezing it, roughly, badly, the way you read a second language you learned as an adult. Priya would watch him squeeze a sample and ask what it told him. Then she'd ask what it couldn't.

The notebook got its line that fall, where the important numbers lived.

*DEPTH OF SENSOR: 1 cm. DEPTH OF ROOT: 30. Write your models accordingly.*

# Chapter 27: Ship Them

The video that did it was the first fire.

Denny had filmed the whole shouse build the summer before, the way he documented everything by then, hands in frame, the county in his vowels. A year after the first fire, he went back through the footage and made four minutes of it. The cut. The columns. Marta's bead going down the header seam like a zipper closing. The old stove with its new brick. And at the end, the draw catching on the first match, and the co-op standing around in the new warm doing nothing historic whatsoever.

He ended it the way he'd ended the first one. "Come see it. Bring your kid."

It posted on a Thursday night. Elijah did the pipes and went to bed.

A week later he was up before light, and he walked to the shed through the first hard frost of the year with a coffee, to check the overnight numbers the way other generations of men had walked out to check stock. The network was green. The greenhouse was green. The channel graph was a wall.

He'd seen spikes. The leak had been a spike. This was the other thing, the curve he'd been drawing in notebooks for four years. This time it was climbing for something he loved.

He wanted to draw a line through it and extend it. He opened the inbox. He read it standing up, with the coffee going cold.

A grange association in Idaho: how do we start. A parish council in Louisiana, two months out from the last flood. A machinist in Ohio with his father's shop and no work in it, asking for the controller plans. A tribal housing authority. A retired county engineer. A woman in Maine who had, she wrote, four acres, a dead barn, and nine neighbors who show up.

Eleven towns by the time the sun was all the way up, in nine states, every one of them asking some version of the same four words.

There was also a text on his phone, from the night before. It was from David.

*Saw the stove.*

That was all of it. He read it three times. He started to type and didn't know what. He put the phone in his pocket to answer later, when he could think, and went to find Grace.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

She was already in the inbox. She'd been in it since five.

"Eleven towns," she said. She was lit up. He had never seen her like this. "Elijah. That's eleven kitchens. That's maybe a thousand people who eat."

"The route's got no money past June."

"I know."

"That inbox could pay for the route. All of it. Forever."

He heard how it sounded, and he kept going. Course packs. Plans at forty dollars. A paid tier with a call once a month. He had a number for it in about ninety seconds, and it was a good number. He and Grace built the rest of it at the kitchen table in two hours, shoulder to shoulder, her finishing his sentences.

They put it on the big screen at Friday dinner, the inbox and the number both.

Curtis liked it. "That's a product," Curtis said. "People pay real money for half of what we know."

It was Frank Kowalski who took it apart, from the end of the table, in the voice of a man doing inventory.

"I spent thirty years selling parts for two dollars over what the internet charged," Frank said. "Know what I was actually selling? Somebody who knows which part. You can't download me. Him neither." A thumb at Denny. "Fence the files and you're selling paper. Ship them free, and every one of those eleven towns needs a Frank and a Marta and a Priya of their own anyway. And they'll find them down the road, because that part doesn't ship." He folded his glasses. "The store taught me that. Late."

"It feeds people, Frank," Grace said. "That's all it's for. I've got a hundred and forty on the route and a budget that stops in June. Every winter there's somebody on that route we don't get to in time. I can tell you their names."

"I know what it's for, honey. I'm telling you what it turns into."

Elijah tried. He kept it to two sentences, because he had learned.

"Books used to be chained to desks. Then somebody made copying cheap, and I'm just saying the folks who printed them still charged for the book."

Nobody answered. He knew it was wrong as soon as he said it.

Marta had been quiet until then, which was how the co-op knew where it would land.

"I told this one his first day," she said, nodding at Elijah, "that nineteen households who know whose kid is whose don't scale. That's the load-bearing wall. I meant it. It's still true." She looked at the wall of numbers. "The wall doesn't scale. The blueprints of the wall scale fine. Ship them."

"For nothing," Elijah said.

"For nothing."

"Then who pays for June?"

"We'll see," Marta said.

It went to a show of hands. It wasn't close. Grace kept her hand down, and so did he, and so did Curtis, which was not company either of them had expected.

She found his arm under the table. They had lost, together, in front of everybody, and it had been the best week of the two years.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Getting the files ready took longer than posting them.

Priya sent her soil notes with the part they still had to repeat marked in pencil. Marta wrote on the building drawings that they were a record of one project and not a recipe for anybody's site. Elijah put the leak report in beside the wins. Denny asked everyone in the footage what they wanted left out. The folder ended up smaller than they'd pictured.

Monday morning he stood in the shed at dawn with the coffee and watched the download counter climb through four digits. People he would never meet were carrying off what the co-op had learned from its mistakes.

An email from the machinist in Ohio came in while he watched. One connector label in the controller drawing was wrong. Elijah opened the drawing, found it, fixed it, and thanked the man by name.

At Friday dinner, Denny read that message out loud before he read the view count.

That was the Friday after the midterms. Half the country had just voted for people who promised to slow the machines down, and the other half for people who promised to turn them loose.

It came to the table anyway. Patricia's network had sent every vendor a pledge to sign. No generated code, no generated text, a little green leaf you could put on your label. Tyler read it out in a flat voice. Somebody asked, lightly, who'd voted for whom. Somebody else said it was a fair question. It was the first time anyone at that table had been asked.

"Doesn't matter who's in," Grace said, passing the bowl. She was half joking. "People will vote for the fire if it's warm."

Elijah laughed.

He'd never answered David. He remembered it then, with the bowl in his hands, and it was three weeks old, and he didn't know how to start.

# Chapter 28: Node 7

The first hard storm of the winter came up the valley on a Friday.

The barometer fell like a dropped tool, and by dark the county was gone.

Grid power went first, a whole hillside of windows going out between one gust and the next. The cell towers lasted forty minutes longer on their batteries and then they went too, and the valley was back in 1890, except for nineteen households, where the lights stayed on.

Somebody brought the printed contact list to the shop table. Grace started the soup. She started it in the twenty-quart pot, which meant she'd already decided it was for the whole road.

The mesh held. Elijah had been building toward that sentence for a year, little radios laid node by node across the ridgelines, and on the night it finally mattered the network did what it was designed to do. It shrugged.

The co-op's map stayed lit, house to house, short messages hopping the valley on watts you could count on your fingers. Marta's shop stayed lit. The greenhouse alarms stayed armed. The whole nervous system he'd been laying into walls and fence posts for a year kept right on reporting, calm as a pulse.

Except node 7.

Node 7 was the ridge repeater, the high hop that stitched the north houses into everyone else, and node 7 was his, in every way that stung. His design. His install. And, the previous Sunday, his update, done alone, in a hurry, ahead of the weather, without a second set of eyes. It was just a repeater. The rule was for things that touched the open internet. He had let himself believe that.

The storm found the ridge at eight o'clock. At 8:04 the map showed a hole where the north houses used to be.

The north houses checked in on the voice radios, one by one. Everyone was fine. The hole was only in the map. But the hole was his.

He suited up. Nobody climbed the ridge alone, and Curtis was already lacing his boots. Marta handed Elijah the handheld radio and the small pack, pointed at his headlamp, and said the thing she always said instead of luck: "Talk before you touch."

The ridge trail was a different place in a storm. The rain went sideways with gravel in it, and the madrone trunks were slick as wet brick. The wind leaned on them, let go, and leaned again.

He climbed with the multimeter zipped inside his coat to keep the display warm, Curtis's light steady behind him, and the radio crackled at his collarbone every quarter mile, Marta running the net from the shop the way she'd once run a floor. Short, no chatter.

And he could hear, in the gaps, the system working around the hole he'd made. Priya's greenhouse threw a low-temperature alarm at 8:40. It went around the dead ridge the long way, south through Frank's node, and Denny answered it. He was hauling the backup battery to keep the winter starts alive. All of it happened without him, around him, because the whole design assumed any one node, any one person, could be the one that failed.

It was a strange thing, hearing something he'd built get along without him.

Node 7's enclosure was where he'd bolted it, on the old fire-lookout stub, and the node was dark. Curtis held a garbage bag over them for a rain fly. Elijah got the lid off, his fingers going stupid with cold, and keyed the radio. He and Marta debugged it across two miles of storm, and she asked the question she always asked.

"Power or brain? Halve it."

Battery voltage: 12.9, holding under load. The battery was good. Halved.

"Downstream." That wasn't Marta. It was Tyler, too loud, new to the button. She had him on the net beside her. "Check the, um. The converter. Under load."

Five point one volts, solid. Halved again. So: brain.

Elijah plugged in the service cable and read the startup log out loud. The little computer woke up. The radio never did.

Sunday's update had changed how it started, and he'd taken one good transmission as a test. He hadn't cut the power and brought it back. The storm had run that test for him.

The fix took twelve minutes because he had, at least, packed like Marta taught. The laptop, the cable, and a copy of the last version that worked, in the dry bag next to the spare fuses.

Restore, restart, cut the power, start again. The status light went to its slow steady breathing, and in his collar the radio said, in Priya's voice, "North houses just came back. Hello, ridge."

Below them the whole county was black to the horizon, except for the lights of the co-op's houses.

Curtis was still holding the bag over him. His arms had to be dead.

"First dinner you came to," Curtis said. He had to nearly shout it. "I said fence them out. I meant you. Laptop people. Show up when it gets bad and eat what we put by."

"I know."

"I wanted a fence for people like you."

Elijah waited.

Curtis looked up at the garbage bag snapping in the wind, and at his own two hands holding it.

"Now I'm holding your rain fly."

Elijah stood up into the wind to pack the tools, and Marta's voice came up the hill one more time. "There's a line on the shop wall. Old catalog. 'We are as gods and might as well get used to it.' The man changed it, later. Get good at it." A crackle, wind on her end too, the shop door open to the weather. "Gods, hell. Gods is the easy part, college. Gods with cold fingers and a multimeter, that's the job. Getting good at it means next time you touch a node, somebody's watching. Come down. Grace has soup."

They came down. There was soup. Grace had fed half the county road by then, out of the back of the kitchen trailer, by lantern, and she handed him a bowl without stopping and touched his wet sleeve as he went by.

The rule got its amendment on Sunday, in Reuben's careful language. It covered everything people depended on now.

The storm blew out by noon Saturday. The county's power was back Tuesday. By then the talk on the mesh was seed orders. Curtis asked who still needed soup taken over.

# Chapter 29: People in It

The co-op held its annual meeting in the shouse.

The shouse had the woodstove and the wall screen and, by its second winter, the unofficial status of parliament. Reuben ran the agenda. The year's numbers. The lease, and the trust's monthly check to Irene Calder. The library's download counter, fifty-one thousand and climbing, from towns in nine countries now. Frank's first full year at the tool library.

Then Elijah stood up for the last item, which was his, and which he had been building for a month, and said: "It's two years from now, and the co-op is dead. I'd like to spend two hours on how it died."

He had come loaded. Of course he had. He had every stage, every watt and every county filing, run through the Crucible against a few hundred possible futures.

Supply shocks: modeled. The Memphis platform buying their steel distributor the way it bought Frank's: modeled, with a second-source list already drafted. Grid failures, fuel spikes, a hostile county supervisor, a change in the property assessment, a landbuyer with better lawyers: modeled, modeled, modeled, modeled.

He walked the room through forty minutes of ways to die, calmly. The room took it seriously. People could follow the math and argue with it.

Then Marta said, "Your machine's got no column for the stuff that actually kills these things," and the second half of the meeting began.

"Factories don't die of the strategy," she said. "I watched mine die for a year before the spreadsheet showed up. The spreadsheet was the coroner. Things die of people, college. Run it again with people in it."

The room ran it again with people in it, and Elijah wrote down what his model had missed.

Burnout, said Priya, looking at Denny. The channel posts three times a week because one man will not stop, and if that man goes down, the lever goes down with him. Denny started to argue and then didn't, which everyone understood, including him. The fix got a name that night. Tyler learns the edit, Frank learns the camera, and the channel becomes a bench and not a pillar.

A divorce, said Reuben, flatly, into the quiet. He didn't name anyone. Nineteen households is a dozen marriages and change, and the trust documents, he confessed, handled a split about as gracefully as a wood chipper. He'd have a fix drafted by March. He had, everyone knew, already started.

Nobody looked at Elijah and Grace. It took some effort, and you could feel it going around the room.

Founder-dependence, said Frank. Every important thing in the valley ran through the same four people, and all four of them were sitting right there.

Then Marta set her cup down and put herself on the list. "Shoulder's done," she said. "Rotator cuff. Tuesdays haven't fixed it and won't. I've got maybe two years of overhead welding left, and this valley has exactly one person certified to make the beads that hold roofs up." She looked at Tyler, twenty-one years old, two years on the roster, holding his coffee like it might be repossessed. "Apprenticeship's four years if you're serious. If you want it, we start Monday."

She picked her cup back up to have something to do. It shook, a little, on the way to her mouth. Elijah saw it. He thought it was the shoulder.

"That leaves two years we haven't covered," Priya said.

Marta nodded. "Then put that on the board too. We need someone qualified to cover the gap. Starting an apprentice doesn't make one finished."

Elijah wrote *WELDER FOR THE GAP: UNRESOLVED,* and left the box open.

Grace had sat through all of it with a page in her lap. Since the first deliveries, the route had limped along on one short grant after another. Now she put the page beside Elijah's laptop. The route had money through June. After that, nothing was decided. She could show what each delivery cost and which households needed their meals cooked. She couldn't turn a promise of a grant into a paid invoice.

She didn't look at Marta or Frank.

"We could do an appeal on the channel," Denny said.

"We could," Grace said. "But your name is already under burnout."

He looked at the board, then down at his hands.

Rosa had sent a note through the kitchen because she couldn't leave work to attend. Her wrist was better. Her hours at the packing warehouse changed from one schedule to the next. The note asked whether the collection time could stay late enough for people on the afternoon shift. At the bottom she'd added: And can you tell us before the week it stops?

Curtis read that line twice. "They shouldn't have to keep wondering who's generous this month."

Reuben drew a second column. The co-op could put up part of what it had set aside for repairs. The kitchen could bring its books. The people on the route could say what it did for them and where it let them down. Together they could take a real proposal to the county, at the meeting where next year's food money got decided. None of that guaranteed a vote. It gave them something to ask for, with a price on it and people behind it.

They wrote *funded through June* in ink. The rest went underneath in pencil, with the meeting date beside it. Curtis said he'd be the one to tell the households what was promised and what wasn't. Grace asked for somebody besides Curtis to learn the route. Two hands went up.

*[Figure: One calendar sheet marks funding secured through June in solid ink. A second outlines a proposed continuation, with a reminder to name backup cover, pay for the work, and tell households what is secured.]*

*Ink for what is funded. Pencil for what is hoped.*

Reuben was the last one out. He stacked the chairs, which he never did. Then he took a plastic sleeve out of his folder and laid it on the table by the laptop.

"You circled a link for me once," he said. "The outfit that bought a dairy down by your mother's. You asked me to check it."

It was one page from a county recorder, three hours south. A grant deed, September of 2024. The grantee had three letters and an LLC. The grantor was a family trust, and under it, typed, was the trustee's name.

"I checked it that week," Reuben said. "I've had this going on two years."

Elijah looked at his own name. The stove ticked.

"I've been reading deeds a long time. I know what a man signs when his mother's in a bed. I'm not asking why you sold." He squared the sleeve to the edge of the table. "I'm asking why I had to go and look."

"Who else knows?"

"I don't carry tales. But it's a public record, and you taught half this valley how to search one." He buttoned his coat. "You modeled forty ways for us to die tonight. Marta told you what you left out."

He went out and pulled the door to behind him, carefully, the way he did everything.

That night, after everyone had gone, Elijah rebuilt the model with the room's amendments. The change that mattered was a names column. Every way to die carried two names now: who it happens to, and who is watching for it. There was one he did not enter.

He was pasting in the last of it when he understood what he was looking at.

The supply models, the watts, the stages, the names, the repair he had misunderstood and the meal they had delivered wrong: it was the notebooks. All of them. MADRONE IS NOT A DRONE, four years of stages and margins and lists, finally in the shape they'd been trying to take since 1:40 in the morning at a kitchen table.
