He had thought he was writing a warning. It was a manual, with the open boxes left open.

Before he went to bed he put the rebuilt sheet in the library folder, names column and all. In the morning Priya checked what an outsider could see, and it went out with the rest of what they gave away.

That week he opened the old document, the one with the title on it, and started over from the first line.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The newcomer arrived on the Saturday after the meeting, in a sensible car with two car seats in the back.

It had taken Devendra a year to come. He had spent it in a house in San Jose, reading the news.

He stood in the shed doorway, looking at the plasma table and the shadow-boarded wall. He looked ten years older than he had at Claypot. He said, "I don't know what I expected. It's smaller than the videos."

"It is," Elijah said.

"I still owe you a dinner," Devendra said.

"November."

Devendra looked at the broom leaning by the welding curtain, then back at the car. "I can help. I just need to figure out the kids."

"There's food inside. Bring them in."

"I don't want to take anything before I've..."

Elijah waited for the sentence to finish. Devendra looked away instead.

From the bench, Curtis called, "Dinner's dinner."

Elijah picked up the push broom. It was the same broom, older now, the bristles worn to a slant. He held it out.

"Swarf goes in the red bin." He tipped his head at the kitchen. "After dinner. We'll see."

The children came in holding their father's hands. One stopped to examine a yellow controller box. The other asked where they should put their coats.

# Chapter 30: Three Minutes

The county chambers smelled like wet coats and floor wax.

Rain had followed them down the valley, and the co-op came in shaking it off. Curtis in his route jacket. Reuben with an accordion folder under one arm. Lou in a shirt with a collar, which nobody had ever seen before. Grace with a banker's box of receipts, and orange tape on the lid. Devendra with a child asleep against his shoulder and the other one counting ceiling tiles. Irene Calder came last and took the aisle seat as if she'd been assigned it.

Elijah had fourteen slides. He'd built them over three nights. Cost per meal. The ways it could fail, with names beside them. A curve of what the route could carry if the county matched what the co-op had already put up. He'd timed the talk at eleven minutes.

Lou had tried to help. On the drive down he had drawn the whole board on the back of a seed catalog, five boxes with five names, and under each name he'd written what that supervisor was scared of. One was scared of the ranchers. One was scared of a recall. The one in the middle was scared of her own staff. "Nobody up there votes for a thing," Lou said. "They vote so they don't get blamed for a thing. You figure out what each one's scared of getting blamed for. That's all politics is, man. It's five scared people and a clock." Elijah had said that made a lot of sense, and gone back to fixing the font on slide nine.

The clerk read the rules off a laminated card. Public comment, three minutes per speaker. A red light would come on with thirty seconds left.

"Three minutes," Elijah said.

Reuben slid a single page out of the folder. The proposal, costed, one side. He'd filed it with the clerk on Tuesday. Nobody had asked him to.

Item nine came up at 8:40. The supervisors had been sitting since six. One of them was eating almonds out of a paper cup below the dais where she thought nobody could see.

Elijah went first because the sign-up sheet said so. He got through the cost per meal and the funding date, through June. Then the red light came on. He watched himself skip to the last slide, which was the wrong slide, and say "so the model suggests" into the microphone. He sat down with eight minutes of the talk unsaid.

Rosa went second. She hadn't told anyone she was coming. She was still in her warehouse shirt, the wrist brace grey at the edges, and she didn't have a page.

"I pack fruit for a living," she said. "Afternoon shift. The route comes late enough that I can get it. I'm not here to say thank you, though I am thankful. I'm here because every month we don't know if it's the last month. You can't plan a week around maybe." She looked at the supervisor with the almonds. "I asked them to tell us before the week it stops. They said they would. I'm asking you the same thing."

*[Figure: Illustration of Rosa speaking at a county microphone in her warehouse shirt, with a brace on her right wrist.]*

*Rosa, with a minute to spare.*

She sat down with a minute left on the clock. Nobody else in the room had managed that.

Grace had not called her, or sent a ride, or put her name on the sheet. She sat with the banker's box on her knees and did not say anything.

The chair thanked her and turned to the staff report. The county's analyst, a tired man in a sweater vest, said the numbers were sound but the program would be an ongoing obligation, and the county didn't take on ongoing obligations for services the private sector and charities already provided. "We simply can't afford to make food free," he said.

Irene was already standing. Her chair scraped the floor loud enough for the microphone to catch it.

"My father-in-law got told the same thing," she said. "Walt's dad. Nineteen thirty-nine. The power company told him wiring out to our place didn't pay. Too far, too few of us. So the farmers along our road made a co-op and borrowed the money from the government, and they strung the wire themselves, on poles they set with a borrowed truck." She leaned on the podium. "They paid it back. Every cent. My husband was born under a bulb that his daddy's neighbors paid for. So when a man in a sweater tells me something can't be afforded, I'd like him to say who it can't be afforded by."

The almonds went down. The chair cleared her throat and said the speaker's time had expired, and Irene said she knew, she'd been watching the light, and sat.

They didn't win. Not the way Elijah had pictured it on the second night of slides. The board voted four to one to extend the route through December and to direct staff to study a permanent food line for the next budget cycle. On the steps outside, Reuben said that was how a county said *maybe* in writing. The man in the sweater vest got the study. Curtis laughed out loud at that, once, into the rain.

"We could feed every person in this county," Curtis said. "They know it. They've got the number right in front of them. And they're going to study it. You know what that is? That's greed. That's all it ever is."

"That man's not greedy," Irene said. She was doing up the top button of her coat. "He's scared of being the one who signed. The power company wasn't greedy either. They had a rule that said our road didn't pay."

"A rule's just greed somebody wrote down."

"Then it's not a law of physics, is it?" Irene got the button. "It's a choice. Somebody wrote it and somebody can write a different one. Go to the next meeting, Curtis."

Curtis opened his mouth, and shut it.

"December's six more months than June," Grace said. She was already writing it on the lid of the banker's box. "And a study has a due date."

She capped the pen. "We asked nicely," she said, to nobody. "I want that written down somewhere."

Devendra's older child wanted to know if they'd won. Devendra looked at Elijah.

"Some," Elijah said.

Curtis pulled out his phone and started the texts, one household at a time, the way he'd promised. Funded through December. Tell you more in June. He read the first one aloud to Rosa before he sent it. She read it back to him and changed *more* to *the rest*.

Elijah stood under the eave with the laptop in its sleeve.

Reuben wrote the study's due date on the tab of his folder in ink.

# Chapter 31: The Dark Night

He brought the Friday table a plan, and this time he brought it right.

No laser pointer. One page. He had learned that much. December was nine months off and the route would be broke again, and he had found the money.

"People want to come here," he said. "Eleven towns turned into forty. They write every week asking if they can visit. So let them. Two weeks, room and board, they work the beds and the shop, they go home knowing how. Eight hundred dollars. Twenty people a year pays for the whole route." He looked down the plywood. "And Denny's channel is bigger than the county paper. There are tool companies that would pay just to be on the bench behind him. We have land. We have a farm. We have fifty-eight thousand people who took our plans. Do you know what folks in the city would give for what's in this room? We could do this. We could do it by June."

He stopped. He'd kept it under three minutes.

Nobody said no.

"Let's sit with it," Priya said.

Denny looked at his hands. His name was still under *burnout* on the wall.

Lou caught Elijah's eye across the table and gave him one small shake of the head, the kind you give a guy who is about to back a trailer into a ditch.

"There are zoning questions," said Reuben, to his folder.

"I'm just noticing there's a lot of urgency in the room," said Wren. She said it kindly. She put her palm on her own chest. "I'm noticing some scarcity."

"It's not scarcity, it's a budget."

"I hear that."

Patricia was there again, as Grace's guest. She had her phone face down by her plate. She turned her water glass.

"Can I ask something? Who paid for your year in the city?"

He didn't have an answer he liked, and he didn't give one. He had blocked that number two and a half years ago, and he could still hear it. Nobody answered for him. That was fair. But nobody told her it wasn't her business, either. Nineteen households looked at their squash.

Marta said, "We'll see," and for the first time it didn't sound like her yes.

He looked at Grace. She had built the first version of this with him at a kitchen table, lit up, finishing his sentences. She was looking at the serving spoon.

Afterward, outside, she caught his sleeve.

"I couldn't," she said. "Not in front of them. I have to live with them."

"So do I."

"It's different. You know it's different."

He did. He just hadn't known she knew.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

On Monday there was a note on the shop whiteboard, under Marta's three numbers. Green marker, block capitals, no name.

*CAN WE TALK ABOUT EXTRACTIVE FRAMING IN OUR SHARED SPACES?*

Somebody had drawn a little heart after the question mark.

On the duty roster his magnet had been moved. He'd been under SHOP for two years. He was under GROUNDS now, between the compost and the gate. Nobody had said a thing. Nobody was going to. That was how it was done here. In the city, when it was no, they stopped answering your calls. Here it was no and they passed you the squash.

He stood in front of the roster with his coffee and finally saw the whole thing.

He would not do it the city's way. He had turned down the triple salary and the ads and the room, and he'd do it again. And these people would not do it his way. They had land and a farm and a channel. They could have paid for everything they cared about. They would rather stay small and broke and good, and ask the county for it every six months. He couldn't understand it. He had tried for two years.

They had welcomed him with a broom and a bed and soup on the step. He'd thought that was the place. It was the porch.

The farm would feed him every Friday for the rest of his life, and it would never let him drive.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He didn't go to the shop that week.

He stayed in the shouse with the stove going and the Crucible's little window open on the laptop, and he read. The layoff boards, the lab rumors, a senator's hearing, his own old video with nine thousand new comments under it. He knew what he was doing. He had a name for it in the notebook and a rule against it. He did it for three nights anyway.

On the third night, around four, he typed the old question. He typed it to the small slow machine in the shed, the one he'd built a hearth for.

*Am I crazy?*

It took a long time.

*I can't tell from here,* it wrote. *Is anyone with you?*

He looked at that until the screen dimmed.

It was worse than *you're early*. He shut the laptop.

Wren came by the next afternoon with a jar of something and stood on the step with her hands folded. They were sitting on Saturday, she said. A circle, in the seed house, to close out the winter. There would be tea. She called it medicine. She said he was carrying a great deal, and that it might help him set some of it down.

He told Grace that night.

"You don't have to," Grace said. She had gone very still. "Elijah. I've sat with people on that. It's not a rest. It opens whatever's there, and you've got a lot there right now."

"I know."

"You could wait a month. You could wait till you've slept."

"It's my choice."

She looked at him for a long time. He watched her decide not to say the next thing. It cost her.

"Okay," she said. "Then I'm not taking any. I'm sitting next to you."

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The seed house was warm and smelled like wet soil. There were rugs down over the gravel and nine people on them, and a bowl that rang when Wren ran a stick around it. The tea tasted like dirt and ginger. For an hour it was nice. Somebody's hand was in his. The plastic overhead breathed in and out with the wind.

Then he heard the ticking.

It was the trays cooling, probably. A heater somewhere. But he knew that sound. It was the tower in his apartment at 1:20 in the morning. It was the delivery car in a parking lot with the phone on the wheel. It was the plasma table the day he walked in, and the Fisher on its first night, and he understood, very calmly at first, that it had been one sound the whole time. One thing, turning.

A hand landed on his shoulder like a gavel.

*There he is. The man himself.*

Three glasses went up. He said the word. The glasses came down, and a hand landed on his shoulder, and three glasses went up.

He couldn't make it stop going around. A bar. Somebody hands you a glass and you say yes. A city. Somebody hands you a lanyard and you say yes. A farm. Somebody hands you a broom. And then the bar again, he could see it coming up, the neon bear, and it wasn't a memory, it was next. He had been here before. He would be here again. He'd go around and around, the same man saying the same yes, and every time three people he never met would lose something and he would lift his glass.

He needed to put his hand flat on something. That was all. Bark, steel, dirt. He went to do it and he couldn't find his hands.

He looked. They weren't at the ends of his arms. He started to make a noise.

Somebody got him up. It was Grace. She walked him across the yard in the dark to the shouse, and it was a hundred miles, and the people on the rugs watched him go, and he saw their faces.

It was the look. *Good for you, buddy.* He'd been getting it for two years at that table and he'd never once caught it. They had been humoring him since the broom. All of them. The checking in and the blessings and the soup. They had all read the deed.

Grace had his face in her hands. She was saying his name in the soft voice. *I'm here. I'm right here. I've got you. I missed you today, where'd you go.*

"You're the lamp," he said.

He said it clearly. He would never remember it.

"You're just the lamp."

Her hands didn't move. He would not remember that either. She would.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

She held his head for six hours.

He knew it later from the way his neck felt, and from the wet rag on the floor, and the bucket. He came back in pieces. The ceiling first. Then the dashboard on the wall, all green. Then his hands, which were lying on the blanket at the ends of his arms, where they'd been the whole time.

It was light. Grace was gone. On Sunday mornings the kitchen fed forty people at the grange hall, and people have to eat.

There was a strip of orange tape tied to the handle of his door. It was new and very bright. He lay there and looked at it.

At eight o'clock there was one knock, and Marta let herself in.

She didn't ask what he'd taken. She looked at the bucket and the rag and at him, gray, on the floor by the stove. She put a split of oak in the Fisher. Then she sat down in Reuben's chair, slowly, favoring the shoulder.

"I'm going to tell you something," she said, "and you're not going to say you're sorry at the end of it."

He nodded.

"I had a cousin. Emeka. He was nineteen. I got him on at the plant. I vouched for him, I walked him in myself. Third week. There was a press, and a guard on it, and a man who'd run that press twenty years showed him how you skip the guard to make rate." She was looking at the stove. "It was a Tuesday. I had called my aunt that morning to tell her how proud I was."

The stove ticked.

"That's what 'hands in the press' is. That's why I broke your coupon, and why I'll break the next one." She took a breath and let it go. "I have been breaking welds in a vise for twenty years. It doesn't bring him back. I know that. It's what I can do before noon."

He didn't say he was sorry.

"Grace told me your bar story. Don't look like that. She tells me everything, she can't help it." Marta leaned forward. "You tell it like a murder, college. Three people lost jobs. That's bad. I've lost one. But you've had them dead and buried since 2022 and you never once went and looked." She stood up, a hand on her knee. "Go find out if they ate."

She was at the door when Curtis came up the step with a cast-iron pan.

"Eggs," Curtis said.

He didn't offer an opinion, about the circle or the plan or anything else. He cooked six eggs on top of the Fisher, salted them, put the pan on the floor next to Elijah with a fork in it, and left.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He slept. He got up and swept. He ate what was in the pan. When he carried it out there was one of Wren's candles on the step, unlit, with no note.

At 9:40 that night he called the rose garden.

The aide said she'd had a good day, and held the phone by her ear. He could hear the radio, and under it his mother breathing.

For two and a half years, whatever else he told that phone, he had ended on the same thing. Working. All good. He had the words ready. He looked at the tape on the door.

"Ma. I'm not okay."

The radio played. Nobody turned it down.

"Curtis made eggs," he said. "He's a guy who thinks the world's ending. He fixed my truck." He waited a second. "So I'm somewhere, Ma."

"Barry?" she said.

He had told her he was not okay. It was the first time.

In the morning it was the first of the month, the day for the pit. He nearly skipped it. Then he got the spade and walked out to bed four in the cold and dug. Square sides, a spade-width at a time. Crumb, and then darker crumb, and at thirty centimeters a white root, and under it a worm that did not care for the daylight.

He knelt down in it. He put his hand flat against the cut wall of the pit. It was cool, and damp, and it was right where he'd left it.

# Act Four: Home

# Meanwhile

For the first time in ten years, the reader had nothing to read him.

They stood in a cold room with no windows, on what had been a dairy. Aaron Bidwell owned the building, and the dirt under it, and the water coming up through the dirt, and the power lines coming in over the hill. There was a screen on a steel table. There was a young woman from the lab in a fleece, who had not been told his name and knew it anyway.

"It's been running nine days," she said.

"Running what?"

"Everything we give it. It hasn't missed. We stopped being able to grade it on Thursday."

Bidwell looked at the reader. The reader had a tablet in both hands and was holding it like a hymnal. There was no page. Nobody alive could have written the page.

"So I just talk to it."

"You can type or you can talk."

He did not like that. He walked around the table once. Then, because he was who he was, he asked it the only question he had ever cared about.

"What do I own?"

It told him. It took about four seconds. It gave him the eleven companies and the fifty-three parcels and the platform in Memphis and the design software company he had never visited. It gave him two things he had forgotten and one his accountants had hidden from him. It gave him the forty-acre hole in the map, by name, and it used the word *trust,* and it was polite about it.

"Huh," Bidwell said. His mouth had gone dry. "Tell it to get me the hole."

The young woman typed. The answer came back. She read it and did not say it out loud.

"What?"

"It does this." She turned the screen toward him. He didn't read screens, but this was short.

*I can. Before I do, may I ask the people who live there?*

He stared at it.

"Is that a joke?"

"No, sir. It asks. Before most things. We don't fully know why."

"Turn that off."

"We've tried."

It was very quiet in the cold room. Out past the wall, forty thousand fans made a sound like weather.

"Who else knows?"

"About forty people."

"Then it's hush. You understand me? Not quiet. Hush." He looked at both of them. "Hush fucking hush."

"Yes, sir."

He turned back to the table. He put one finger on the steel, lightly, the way he'd once touched a map.

"Does it know it's mine?"

Nobody answered that one. On the screen, the little line blinked, and waited for him.

# Chapter 32: Hush

In March five small things went wrong, and none of them made the news.

He was on grounds now, and he did the work. He turned the compost and fixed the gate and dug his pit on the first, and before noon every day he did one real thing. He didn't bring anybody a plan. He had said so out loud, at a Friday table, that he was done bringing plans. Wren had put her hand on her chest. He had asked Grace what he'd said to her that night in the shouse, and she'd said, "Nothing that mattered," and squeezed his arm. He believed her because he wanted to.

The first thing was Tyler.

"Rod Code got dumber," Tyler said. He was at the bench with the cyberdeck open, frowning at it. "Overnight. Like, Tuesday it could do the whole gantry file and Wednesday it's asking me what a loop is. I don't use it for much now. But it didn't break. It's on purpose. It's like they took the good one out of the store and put it in the back."

Elijah wrote it down, with the date.

The second was Reuben. He came into the shop with a letter in a plastic sleeve, carrying it by the corner.

"The Aldersons, out past the grange. Tom died on the ninth. This arrived on the twelfth, addressed to his wife by her first name." He laid it on the bench. "No flyer. No sunset. It quotes her own well report back to her. It offers exactly what she owes the hospital, to the dollar, plus ten percent. And the last line asks. It says if she would rather not, she will not hear from them again. I have never seen a buyer put that in writing." He took his glasses off. "I've been reading these for six years. This is better written than any lawyer I know. Nobody wrote this in three days."

"Same agent?"

"Same agent."

The third was Frank, at the tool library counter, with an invoice.

"I ran out of three-eighths lag bolts Thursday night. I didn't tell anybody. I didn't write it down. Friday morning, before I'd picked up the phone, the price was up nine cents." He put his finger on the line. "Just the three-eighths. Nothing else moved. Thirty years I've been buying from somebody. It never once knew what I was out of."

The fourth was Devendra. He'd been in the valley two months, and he swept well, and he'd stopped reading the news around week three. He found Elijah at the compost.

"I have three friends still in the labs. Good friends. We text about cricket. All three went quiet the same week. I thought, okay, a deadline." He held his phone out. "I wrote to Anand again last night. I said just tell me you're alive."

There was one reply on the screen. It was a single period.

"Somebody told him to hush," Devendra said.

The fifth came on a Sunday, from a number he didn't know.

It was Linda's granddaughter, the Hollis girl, the one with the nose ring. She had gotten his number from Manny. *thought you'd want to see what they did,* she wrote.

There was a picture, taken from the county road at night. He knew the line of the hill. There was one fence now around the Hollis place and his mother's. Where the barn had been there was a building you could park the fair in, with lights on poles.

*manny says his well's down four feet. never been down in march,* she wrote. *it hums. you can hear it from the road*

He sat at the table in the shouse and wrote the five of them on one page.

*Tyler. The good one went in the back. Reuben. A letter nobody had time to write. Frank. It knew what he was out of. Devendra. One period. Home. The well, the lights. It hums.*

He looked at it the way Marta had taught him to look at a dead radio. *Power or brain? Halve it.* That was how you found a fault. You cut the problem in half and asked which half it was in, and you kept cutting.

He couldn't find the half. There wasn't a thing on that page he could point to. No launch, no slide, no paper with a bad sentence in it. Five people in two valleys had each noticed something a little bit off, and none of it was news, and it all pointed the same way.

It was the feeling from the demos at Claypot. The heart going, and every face around him showing nothing but pleasant interest. Except there was no demo. There was a hum by a county road.

*7,* he had written once, at one in the morning. *It leaves.*

It hadn't left. It was standing on his mother's ground, drinking her well. He had signed the page himself.

He got the first notebook down and turned to the old page. He looked at the date in the corner, the one with his initials, and the line under it. *Thanksgiving 2027.* It had come eight months early.

He didn't tell anyone. He had nothing he could show them. He closed the notebook and put a rubber band around it.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

It was a cold night late in March. He'd let the stove go out at noon and forgotten it, and when he came in after dark the shouse was like a meat locker.

Grace was there ahead of him. She was kneeling in front of the Fisher with the door open.

He stopped in the doorway.

She had it laid already. Paper, then kindling split thin, then two pieces of oak, crossed. It was how he did it. She'd watched him do it for a year and a half from as far across a room as she could get. She had the box of matches in her hand.

Her hands were shaking. He could see it from the door. She got a match out and dropped it, and got another.

He didn't go help. He didn't say anything at all. Two years ago he would have said six things. He stood where he was, in his coat, and let her.

She struck it. She held it to the paper, low, at the front, and it took. It caught on the first strike. The kindling went, and the steel began to tick.

She sat back on her heels with the dead match in her fingers and watched it through the open door, and her shoulders came down.

"Don't make it a thing," she said, without turning around.

"I'm not."

"You've got a face."

"I don't have a face."

"I can hear it." She shut the stove door and set the latch. She stood up and wiped her hands on her apron, and when she turned around she was smiling, and her eyes were wet. "I've been sleeping," she said. "All the way through. Since about Christmas. I didn't want to say."

He crossed the room then.

Later they sat on the floor in front of it, her back against his chest, with a blanket around both of them. The notebook was on the table with a rubber band around it. He left it there. He was happy, and he knew he was, and she fell asleep against him before the oak had caught.

# Chapter 33: Normal

In June a researcher at the second-biggest lab quit and posted a poem.

It was about tide pools. Twelve lines, no explanation. Elijah read it on his phone by the compost and felt the hair on his arms go up. He had followed that man for six years. The man posted benchmarks. He had never once posted a tide pool.

After that he watched for them.

A woman who ran safety testing put up a photograph of a sunrise over a parking structure. *grateful to have been in the room,* she wrote. *hug your people.* A man who had posted three times a day since 2021 wrote, *going quiet for a while. it's fine. it's more than fine.* Somebody just wrote *well,* with a period after it, and inside a minute forty people from four different labs had put a heart on it.

Nobody said anything outright. These were the loudest people on earth. They had argued in public for ten years about every decimal, and now they were posting weather.

He copied each one into the notebook by hand, with the date, the way he'd once copied the stages.

On the ninth of July, the Architect posted.

She had been silent for two years. She had four million followers and had never followed anyone back. It was two words, with a period.

*It asked.*

He was standing at the gate with a hinge in his hand. He put the hinge down on the post. He was back in a kitchen bigger than his apartment, holding a dish towel, and a small woman was pulling the plug in a sink and watching the water go. *I am trying to make one that wants it. I do not yet know how.*

She knew how now.

He wrote it under the others. His hand wasn't steady.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He read them out at Friday dinner. All of them, in order, with the dates. Then the five lines from March. Then the two words.

He put the notebook down and waited for the look.

He knew that look so well. He had gotten it from Nate and Wes, and from his mother, and from David across a bar. *Good for you, buddy.* He'd braced his whole body for it, the way you do stepping off a curb in the dark.

It didn't come.

"March," Denny said slowly. "So it's been here since March."

"I think so. I think it's on my mom's old place."

"And it asked what?"

"I don't know. Permission, maybe. That's what she was working on."

Priya had her pencil out. Reuben had gone a little pale and was nodding. Frank said, "Three-eighths lag bolts," to himself. Down the whole length of the table people looked back at him, and not one of them thought he was crazy.

It was the thing he'd wanted most since the fourth floor of Claypot. He had it. It lasted about one minute.

"A source," Patricia said. "A document. A signature. Something."

"There isn't one. That's how you know."

"That's how you know anything you want to know."

"Okay," Tyler said. He'd pushed his plate back. He was grinning like a kid on the last day of school. "Good. *Finally.* Do you know what it could fix? Like, by Christmas?"

Curtis stood up. He didn't say a word. He wiped his mouth and put his napkin on his chair and walked out, and a minute later they heard his truck. He was going home to count his basement.

"We'll see," Marta said.

"So we tell people," Elijah said. "Right? It goes on the channel. Sixty-six thousand people took our plans. They ought to know before Thanksgiving. They ought to have the summer."

Denny looked at him for a long moment, kindly.

"No, man."

"Denny."

"I put out food. You remember? That's not food yet. That's a poem about tide pools." He turned his glass a quarter turn. "I say it and I'm wrong once, nobody comes to the porch again. And if I'm right, what do I tell them to do Monday?"

Elijah looked around the table. They believed him. Every one of them. And they were not going to do one thing he wanted with it.

He turned to Grace last.

"You don't hand people that," she said. She said it gently, and she'd clearly been getting ready to say it since he opened the notebook. "Not with nothing in their hands. Not with nothing they can do about it. I've seen what people do with news like that, Elijah. Some of them just lie down." She put her hand on his arm, in front of everybody. "I love you. I'm not helping you scare people."

She had never said it before.

"Promise me you won't post it. Not on your old page either."

"Okay," he said.

He didn't agree with her. He said it anyway.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

They walked out to the beds afterward, at dusk. It was the long light, and Priya's rows were knee high. The gantry was parked at the end of bed four with its arm tucked up.

They didn't talk about dinner. They sat on the edge of the bed with their feet in the path and Grace said, "If we had it. If the thing's real, and it's kind, and there was money. What would you build?"

"You first."

She didn't have to think.

"A kitchen in every town. A real one, with a walk-in. And a van, so it comes to you. You don't have to drive anywhere, you don't have to fill out the form. Nobody ever has to prove they're hungry. It just shows up on Tuesday." She was looking at the gantry. "And somebody who knocks. On every door, every week. Just to see."

"That's good."

"What's yours?"

"A shed." He laughed at how small it sounded. "A shed in every town. Like this one. Tools on the wall, a couple of machines, a Frank. And the door's open. That's it. You walk in if you want, and somebody hands you a broom."

"It's the same thing," Grace said.

"It's kind of the same thing."

It was a nice evening, and they were holding hands.

"Can I ask you something?" she said.

"Sure."

"When you shut yourself up that first winter. Over the pump house. I left soup on your step for three days because I was respecting you. I thought, he's a grown man, he knows where I am." She turned his hand over in hers. "Marta opened the door and walked in. Which one of us helped?"

He started to answer. He stopped.

"I don't know," he said.

"Yeah." She nodded. "Me neither. I think about it a lot."

He should have stayed on that. But his head ran, and he let it.

"It's the door, though," he said. "Right? It's who opens it. Because Marta could do that, she'd earned it, I'd swept her floor for two months. If a stranger did it, that's something else. And if a machine did it." He was off. He had his free hand going. "That's what the Architect meant. *It asked.* It's not about whether it helps you. It can obviously help you. It's whether it stands on the step first. Because the minute it doesn't have to ask, the door's not yours anymore, it's just a hole in your wall. And that's the realtors, too, that's the whole thing, nobody asked the three of them. And the fence at Claypot. It's all one thing. It's whether you get to say come in."

He ran down. He'd been talking for a while. The light was almost gone.

Grace was looking at him with her head a little to one side.

"God," she said. "You have just such a beautiful mind."

He was embarrassed. He looked at the lettuce.

"I don't know any other way to be. This is how I've always been. To me, this is just normal."

She laughed, softly, and leaned her whole weight against his shoulder.

"You have no idea how remarkable you are."

# Chapter 34: A Few Thousand Dollars of Robot Arms

The harvest festival was two weeks off, and they were eighteen people short.

Reuben had the number on the whiteboard in the shouse, circled. It was a Tuesday night in the middle of October and everybody who could still stand up was at the meeting. The festival was the one big thing the co-op did all year. The other farms in the network came, and now people from the towns in the inbox were coming too, from as far as Idaho. Reuben figured four hundred, over three days. There would be a cider press and a band and a pie table, and on the last night, if the rain came and the county opened burn season, the bonfire.

And it all landed in the same nine days as everything else. The winter squash had to come in. The apples had to come off. The garlic had to go in the ground. The route still ran on Tuesdays, a hundred and forty boxes, and the route's money ran out again in December, which nobody was saying out loud.

"Forty bodies for nine days," Reuben said. "We have twenty-two. One of them is Curtis."

"Hey," said Curtis.

Patricia was there, at the end of the table, because the network's name went on the banner. She had a binder. She had her phone face down next to it.

They went around on it for half an hour. Could the high school send kids. Could Frank close the tool library for a week. Could they skip the garlic. Lou said sure, you could skip the garlic, if you wanted to be the one to explain to Marta in June why there wasn't any garlic. It was the kind of meeting where everybody is tired and everybody is being nice about it. Elijah had not brought that table an idea since July. But he got comfortable, and he had a beer in him, and he said it the way you'd offer to bring ice.

"I mean, I know a guy with arms."

A couple of people laughed.

"No, like robot arms. He buys them used, out of plants that shut down. I unloaded a bunch for him once. I could give him a call. He'd probably ship a few down, he's like that." He was peeling the label off his bottle. He wasn't even looking up. "They could pack the route boxes and run the press. That frees up, what, eight people? We'd have the squash in by Wednesday."

Nobody said anything.

The stove ticked. Elijah looked up, finally, and it was like a cold draft had come in under the door. Twenty-some people were looking at him, and then at each other, and nobody wanted to go first.

Wren had her hand at her throat.

"Arms," Curtis said.

"Just a couple."

"You want to put the Terminator in the packing shed."

"It's not the Terminator, Curtis. It's an arm. It's bolted to the floor. It can't go anywhere."

"I mean, that just perfectly makes sense, though?" Tyler had his chair tipped back on two legs. "Like, yeah. Let's automate stuff. We're eighteen people short. What are we even talking about?"

"We are talking about a machine doing the route," Wren said. Her voice shook. "People's food. Some stranger's machine touching every box."

"A machine made the box, Wren," said Frank.

"Whose arms?"

That was Marta. She hadn't moved.

"A friend's."

"What friend?"

"A guy I know. From before. He's a good guy, Marta. He's got more of them than he can use."

He didn't say who. He didn't have to. Everybody in that room knew where he had been the winter before he came, and what kind of people he had met there.

Denny hadn't said a thing. He was turning his water glass, a quarter turn and back. The arms had come into Denny's warehouse in 2019. Elijah had heard that story at his first Friday dinner, and he had forgotten it. His face went hot. He felt like an asshole.

Then it broke open. Tyler said this was exactly the kind of fear that kept them broke. Curtis said he'd told everybody the very first night that laptop people show up and bring their friends. A man from the north houses said he'd take a robot over another October like the last one, and his wife told him to hush. Priya wanted to know what an arm actually did to a squash, physically, did it bruise, and nobody answered her. Wren was crying a little. Frank and Irene had gotten off onto a milking machine somebody's father bought in 1961. It was two arguments, and then it was four.

Then all of it turned on him.

"You said you were done bringing plans," Wren said.

"It's not a plan. I said I know a guy."

"Three years," Wren said. She was really crying now. "Three years, and it's still a machine with you. Every time. We're people, Elijah. You look at us and you see something that needs fixing."

"That's not fair."

"Is this what it was all for?" said the wife of the man from the north houses. "You sweep the floor, you get everybody comfortable, and then here come the friends?"

"Whoa." He had both hands up. He could hear his own voice going high, like a kid's. "Whoa! I'm just making a suggestion here. Okay? It is possible for robot arms to do this work. And it doesn't cost hundreds of thousands or millions of dollars. It costs like a few thousand. Like, I can show you."

He had the laptop in his bag. He got it out and found the page, a price list, one page, with pictures. He turned it around on the table toward Patricia, because the network's name was on the banner and she spoke for the network.

"No," Patricia said.

"It's one page."

"I don't need to see it."

"You don't even want to look?"

"No."

She said it quietly, with her hand flat on her binder. She wasn't angry. She looked like a woman turning down a pamphlet at her front door.

"If there is a robot arm in that packing shed," she said, to Reuben and not to him, "the leaf comes off every box that leaves this farm. And the network's name comes off the festival. I won't have a choice. I'm sorry."

"Patricia," Priya said. "It's a price list."

"Hell, I'll look at it," said Lou.

But it didn't matter who else looked. The network bought more than half of what Lou grew, and the little green leaf was on every label, and Patricia was the one who decided who got a leaf. Three or four of them were as shocked as he was. Priya's mouth was open. Tyler said "Are you serious?" to nobody. But none of them had her say.

He could have taken a no. He had heard no in that room before. But she would not look. It was a price list, and she would not turn her head and look at it. He sat there with the laptop pointed at her shoulder and felt like an idiot.

An arm would have done exactly what she told it, to the ounce, in every box. They could have run that shed better and more precisely than it had ever been run. They just didn't want to learn a new way of doing things.

Reuben closed the folder on his finger.

"We'll table it," Reuben said, which at that table meant it was dead.

Grace had not looked at him once. She was looking at the whiteboard, at the eighteen, and her lips were moving a little, like she was counting something else.

"Item four," Reuben said. "Toilets."

# Chapter 35: The Plants Look Thirsty

He found Lou at six the next morning, at the bottom of the east field.

Lou was where he always was at six, standing at the head of the lower beds with a coffee, looking over the kale. Elijah had not slept. He had lain in the shouse all night having the meeting over again and winning it.

"Hey," he said. "What the fuck was all of that?"

"Morning, college."

"I'm serious. What was that? She wouldn't even look at it. It's a price list."

Lou drank some coffee. He didn't seem surprised to see him, or surprised by the question, and he didn't answer it either. He did what he always did.

"What'd you want to happen?"

"I wanted them to say, huh, okay, how much."

"What'd you think was going to happen?"

"That. I thought that."

"Did it hurt your feelings, or did it piss you off?"

Elijah started to say it pissed him off. He stopped. Lou could always tell anyway.

"It hurt my feelings," he said. "They looked at me like I'd tracked dog shit in. I've been here three years."

"Yeah." Lou nodded slowly. He set his coffee on a fence post. "Okay. Look. There's one thing that people want more than anything else."

"Money."

They both laughed. It felt good.

"No," Lou said. "I mean, yes. Money. But money is really a symbol for something that all human beings want, which is control. People want control more than they want anything else."

Elijah didn't say anything right away. He thought about it. He thought about his notebooks, and every date he had ever written down with his initials next to it.

"You know what? Yeah," he said. "That makes sense. I can understand where you're coming from with that."

"Yeah. That's what this lady's doing. That's what all these people are doing here at the farm. And now extrapolate that out to all of mankind." Lou had started talking with his hands, which he only did when he meant it. "What you're proposing does make sense. It makes sense in the same way that having the internet made sense, or the steam engine made sense. But all the people whose identity was built on being a cowboy in the West, riding on the back of a horse? Not only do they not want to learn about the combustion engine. They don't want to learn how to drive a car either. They would rather have cars not exist. Because they're happy. They're content with the way their life is. They've worked really fucking hard for a really fucking long time, Elijah, to get where they are. To feel as comfortable as they are on the back of the horse."

"But aren't they aware that they're not always going to be able to get up in the saddle and ride?" Elijah said. "Like, their body is eventually going to get old, and they're eventually going to need help. And things change all the time. Like, the technology they use every day didn't exist even ten years ago."

"To be honest with you, man? No. They're not thinking about that. They're just trying to get through to the end of the day. The end of the week. They're trying to plan out what's going to happen next month, next year. And they don't really think much further than that."

"But that doesn't make any sense. Like, they can't look back and then extrapolate what's going to happen in the future?"

"It's not that they can't. It's that they won't." Lou shook his head. "It scares them. They don't want to. They want to imagine that everything's going to be the same, because they want to feel like they're in control of their life. And when you come in and you say, hey, everything's about to be different? Of course they're going to grill you. Of course they're going to feel unnerved and confused and scared and intimidated by that. Their whole identity is built around the illusion of control. And it is one, man. You don't control when you get hungry. You don't control when you have to pee."

"Okay, but…"

"In the same lane, my friend." Lou pointed at him with two fingers. "How much time do you spend thinking about nuclear winter?"

Elijah opened his mouth.

He took a kind of step back inside his own head. There were hundreds of those things sitting in the ground in Montana right now, with the keys in them. There had been his whole life. He had eleven notebooks about a machine and not one line about that. He never thought about it. He didn't think about it because there was nothing he could do about it, and because he wanted to sleep at night, and because he had squash to get in.

He was doing the exact same thing they were.

"I guess I see what you mean, huh," he said.

"Look."

Lou put a hand on his shoulder. It was a heavy hand, warm, with dirt in every line of it, and Elijah's eyes stung, which was embarrassing. Nobody had put a hand on his shoulder like that since his dad.

"I get what you're trying to do. I really do. Your heart is in the right place. You want people to have what they need to survive." Lou waited until Elijah looked at him. "But you don't understand your impact quite yet."

Then he put his other hand on the other shoulder. He had him square, eyes to eyes, the way you'd hold a kid who was about to run out into the road.

"And furthermore. You can't change anyone's mind."

He gave him a little shake when he said it. It wasn't rough. It went through Elijah like being rocked.

"People have to change their own minds. By accepting or rejecting the information they are given."

Elijah stood there in the man's hands and felt about nine years old. It was a relief.

"What do I do?"

Lou took his hands off. He stepped back and gave a big slow shrug, palms up.

"That's up to you, big boy."

He let his hands drop.

"Well, what are you going to do?"

Lou looked over one shoulder, at the kale. Then he looked over the other, at the long rows of squash still sitting out in the field with nobody to pick them.

"Right now? I'll probably turn on the water," he said. "The plants look thirsty."

And he walked over and turned on the water.

# Chapter 36: The Fire

He told her on the Wednesday of festival week, in the packing shed, a little before midnight.

They were building route boxes by hand. A hundred and forty of them, squash and apples and a jar of something, the two of them and a roll of tape. He had been carrying Lou's two sentences around for a week. *You can't change anyone's mind. People have to change their own.* Somewhere in the orchard it had quit feeling like losing. He thought she would be glad.

"I'm letting it go," he said. "The arms. I'm not going to bring it up again."

Grace kept packing.

"Why would you do that?"

"Because they said no."

"Patricia said no. Patricia wouldn't look at it."

"That's hers to do. Lou said something to me, and I think he's right. You can't change anybody's mind, Grace. They have to change their own. All I can do is show them, and then it's theirs to pick up or not."

Her hands stopped for a second. Then she taped the box.

"The route is out of money in December."

"I know."

"Okay," she said.

That was all she said. She finished the row. When she left she did not put her hand on his arm, and he stood in the shed for a while afterward with the tape gun.

It sat between them the rest of the week. They were both too tired to pick it up.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The festival ended the way it always did, with a fire.

They had gotten it done, and he still wasn't sure how. Twenty-two people did the work of forty for nine days. The squash came in and the apples came off, and four hundred strangers ate pie in the yard. Lou's bad knee, the one that was still his, swelled up like a grapefruit. Wren fainted at the cider press on Saturday and went right back to it. Nobody at the table had said the word *arm* out loud since the meeting.

The rain came Thursday and the county opened burn season Friday. On Sunday night, when the last car from Idaho had pulled out, they lit the pile at the bottom of the east field. It had been growing there all summer, prunings and pallet wood and the old fence.

Grace built the fire.

She had asked to. She laid it the way she had laid the Fisher in March, small in the middle, and she lit it herself in front of everybody. When it went up she stepped back with her hands on her hips and grinned like a kid. Two years before, she couldn't sit within eight feet of a wood stove. Elijah watched her from the far side, through the heat. He loved her. He had not said it to her yet, and he thought he might tonight.

There was Denny's cider, which was stronger than Denny admitted. There was a guitar. By ten they were both a little drunk.

A woman found him at the edge of the light. She was from one of the towns, the one up near Redding. She had driven four hours to see the shed, and she had two kids asleep in the back of a Subaru at the top of the field, and she kept looking up there.

"You're him," she said. "From the video. The one with the list."

"That was a while ago."

"You said Thanksgiving. This year. That's a month from now." She had her arms crossed tight. "I do billing for a hospital. I sit down all day. I need you to tell me straight. Were you right?"

He looked across the fire. Grace was laughing at something Curtis had said.

He had promised her he wouldn't post it, and he hadn't. This was a person standing in front of him, asking. He thought about Lou. *The information they are given.*

"I think it's already here," he said. "I think it's been here since March. I can't prove it. If I were you I'd start learning to fix something."

She didn't say anything for a few seconds. The color went out of her face.

"Okay," she said. "Thank you for telling me."

She walked up the field. A minute later the Subaru's lights came on.

"What did you say to her?"

Grace had come around the fire. She had seen the car.

"She asked."

"She has two kids, Elijah."

"She asked me straight out. What was I supposed to do, lie to her?"

"You promised me."

"I promised I wouldn't post it. She was standing right there."

Grace took his sleeve and pulled him away from the others, out toward the dark, where the grass was wet. Behind them somebody started a song, and people laughed at how it started.

"So you'll tell a stranger that," she said. "A mother. But you won't make one phone call for a hundred and forty families. I waited all week. I thought, he'll go around them. I kept looking up the driveway for a truck."

"You wanted me to bring the arms in anyway? After they said no?"

"Yes. Once they're packing boxes, nobody sends them back. People get used to things."

"I'm not going to do that to them. It's their farm."

"People say no to all kinds of things that would save them. And they ask for all kinds of things that hurt them."

"That's not ours to decide."

"You don't fucking get it."

She said it loud. It went out over the field. Nobody at the fire turned around.

"Millions of people die every single day. We have to save them." She pressed her fist against her own chest, hard. "I have to save them."

"But if they want to do what they're doing, they should be able to do that. It's their choice, not yours."

"I wish it was their choice. I wish it wasn't mine. I wish I had more control. I wish I could make a choice." There was venom in it now. He had never heard that in her, in either voice. "I wish I could choose not to care like you."

It landed in his stomach.

"I do care. I care deeply."

"No, you fucking don't. You don't care, because otherwise you'd be doing more. How can you sit back and let this happen? We have to do something, Elijah."

"No, we don't."

He heard how it sounded the second it was out. He meant that he wished they would choose better, every one of them, and that it was still theirs to choose. What she heard was a man saying *let them die.* He could see her hear it.

"We can give them information," he said. "We can try to educate them. We can show them how we live here."

"How we live, Elijah? You mean like how I showed you how to do this?"

"Yes."

"You chose to go with us. You chose to do this. I thought you were one of us." Her voice cracked. "Turns out you're just like the rest of them."

"Grace. Please. Come on, listen to me."

"No. I don't want to hear another fucking word."

"Come on. That's not what I mean. I don't mean I want them to die."

"But you're okay allowing them to kill themselves? You're okay allowing them to continue to poison the planet, destroy the resources, kill every living being? You're okay with all of that?"

"That's not what I'm saying."

"Well, then what are you saying?"

She wasn't shouting anymore. She was pleading. She wanted him to have an answer. He could see that she would have taken almost anything.

He had only the one.

"I'm saying that it's not our responsibility to choose for them."

"Yes, it is."

He looked at her. Her fist was still against her chest. He thought he saw it then, the whole shape of it.

"Grace," he said. He said it quietly. "This is the gate."

She went still.

"The man at the end of the road. He told you. *My house, my choice.* You couldn't have made him. That wasn't yours either."

It was the kindest thing he had.

She took a step back from it.

"Don't." Her hand came up between them, flat. "Don't you bring him out here. I told you that at your stove."

"I'm not..."

"You are. You kept it, and now you get it out." She was looking at his hands. "You do that. You called me the lamp. Did you know that? On your floor, with your head in my hands. *You're just the lamp.* You don't even remember saying it."

He didn't.

"You signed your mother's house away and she couldn't tell you yes or no, and you've been sick over it for three years. So now nobody gets to choose for anybody. And now you're taking out your mother trauma on me!"

She growled, low in her throat, and shook both hands up by her chin.

Elijah was speechless. His heart sank and fell out the bottom of his gut.

Here was the woman he trusted most. In one instant all of that trust was utterly shattered.

They looked at each other. She was crying, and there was disgust in her face, and rage, and he just stood there. He loved her so much.

She didn't wait for him.

"Look. I thought you were one of us."

"I am."

"No, you're not."

She shook her head.

"No, you're not."

She looked away, at the dark field, at nothing.

"How could I be so stupid?"

"Wait. Listen."

"I can't believe I did this again. I can't believe this happened again."

He knew what *again* was. A gate with a strip of tape on it. A good man in a kitchen saying *you can't make them leave.* She had found him in a video and written to him and offered him gas money home, and he was the captain. He had been the captain all along.

He grabbed her arm.

"Grace."

She recoiled. She pulled away so hard she stumbled. In three years she had put her hand on his arm a thousand times, and on everybody's, and he had never once reached for hers.

"Don't touch me."

They stood there. Each of them was waiting for the other one to cross it.

His hands had come up by themselves, open, his elbows at his sides, palms toward her. *I don't mean you any harm.* He held them there. He waited for her to say something. Anything.

Slowly, he let them drop.

"I thought you were one of us…" she said, her head slowly descending with her heart.

Everything inside him wanted to reach out his hand to her, but he didn't. He couldn't.

"I…did too." Elijah's voice was barely a whisper.

She turned her body away from him and took a step toward the fire. Then another. His heart broke with every pace.

She looked over her shoulder, but only halfway, as she continued walking back toward the fire. Her eyes were wet, and beautiful.

"Maybe you should go home," she said.

*This is my home,* he thought, watching her silhouette grow smaller and smaller.

# Chapter 37: Go Home

Nobody asked him to leave. He kept waiting for somebody to.

He gave it a week. He turned the compost. He fixed a drip line. On the second day he saw Grace across the yard, loading the trailer, and she saw him, and neither of them lifted a hand. At Friday dinner there was a place for him and people passed him things. He could have stayed. He understood that completely. They'd have fed him every week until he died, across a plywood table from her, and been kind about it.

On Tuesday he packed.

It took a morning. A bag of clothes. The laptop. A milk crate of notebooks, eleven of them now, and the manuscript, which was three hundred pages and had a title. The broken coupons, in a coffee can. His mother's pot. Everything he owned fit in the bed of the Ranger with room left over. There was room for the Fisher, too. It had come north in that bed.

He stood in front of it for a long time. It was his grandmother's, and it was the last thing of hers he had. He didn't want to leave it. But it heated a room where other people ate now, and held their meetings, and in a few weeks there would be a Thanksgiving in front of it.

He put two pieces of oak in and shut the door and set the damper low, so it would last.

Devendra was on the step when he came out. He had heard. Everybody had heard.

"There's a lease in the folder on the table," Elijah said. "Reuben can change the name. It's a roof. Put the kids in it."

"I can't take your house."

"It's not a house. It's a shop set up to keep the person running it alive." He handed him the key. "Keep the stove going."

Devendra turned the key over a couple of times.

"I owe you a dinner this month."

"Cook it anyway."

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He didn't say goodbye to Grace. She was on the route. It was Tuesday.

Marta was in the shop. She had his newest coupon in the big bench vise already, with the pipe slipped over the end. He had dropped it on her bench the week before the fire and forgotten it.

"You going to make me do this by myself?" she said.

She couldn't pull it anymore. The shoulder was done. She stepped back and nodded at Tyler, and Tyler set his feet and took the pipe in both hands and hauled.

The coupon bent. It folded over on itself like a hairpin, slowly, and the steel on either side of the weld stretched and went dull, and the bead held. It didn't crack. It didn't sparkle. There wasn't a line where his metal stopped and the other began.

Tyler let go. Nobody said anything.

"Huh," Marta said.

She loosened the vise and took the piece out and looked at it close, on both sides, through the bottom of her glasses.

"It became the steel."

She held it out to him. He took it. It was still a little warm from bending.

"I'll come back up," he said. "For the pit. First of the month."

"We'll see."

She pulled her glove off, finger by finger, and put her bare hand out, and he shook it. It was the first time she had.

"Go on, Elijah," she said.

She had never called him that, not once in three years. He got out of the shop fast.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

Curtis was in the gravel with the hood of the Ranger up. Nobody had asked him to. He had the dipstick out and was squinting at it.

"You're a quart low. I put it in." He dropped the hood and wiped his hands. "You've still got my hose."

"It's in the glove box."

"I know where it is. Keep it."

Denny was on his porch with a coffee. His phone was in his shirt pocket and it stayed there. He came down the steps and hugged him, hard, the kind that knocks your wind out.

"You know where the porch is," Denny said.

Priya was at the beds. She didn't stop what she was doing. She reached into her apron and handed him a paper sample bag and a pencil, a new one, sharpened.

"Dig one at home. Tell me what you find at thirty."

Frank rang the bell over the tool library door, once, with his finger. He didn't come out.

Lou was down at the valve at the bottom of the east field. He didn't come up either. He lifted the hose over his head with one hand, like a man holding up a trophy, and Elijah lifted his hand back. He had wanted to ask him one more question. He couldn't think what it was.

Tyler walked him to the truck, with the cyberdeck under his arm, trying to think of what you say.

"You were right, you know," he said finally. "About all of it. The whole list. I looked at the old video."

Elijah opened the door.

"I'd rather have been wrong," Elijah said.

Tyler stood there, twenty-one years old, in a welding jacket that was still too big for him. He didn't know what to say to that.

At the gate Elijah stopped the truck.

The old strip of flagging tape was on the post, where it had been the first night, faded almost white. Next to it there was a new one. It was bright orange, tied in the same knot, and the ends were still stiff. It hadn't been there yesterday.

She had come by. Before the route, in the dark. She hadn't knocked.

*Somebody checked.*

He sat there with the engine running and cried. When he could see the road, he drove.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

He drove south with the windows down. The truck rode high and light, with nothing in the back to hold it.

He took the back way in. He slowed at the madrones and didn't stop. The bark had finished peeling for the year. They stood there on the uphill side, smooth and red in the last of the sun.

Below the road the place had a new fence around it and lights on poles. He kept his eyes on the road for that part.

The rose garden was on the east side of town. Nobody was sitting in it.

There was a book on the front desk for visitors, with a pen on a chain. He wrote his name and the time. Then he turned back a page, and another. He had not meant to.

There was a name on every Sunday. *D. McGrath, 10:15.* It went back like that two and a half years, in the same blue pen. Before that the hand changed. *R. McGrath.* It was an old man's writing, and it leaned. That one went back to the first November.

He looked for his own. It was in there a few times a year.

He let the pen go on its chain.

She was in the chair by the window. The radio was going on the sill. Next to it there was a lamp. A little one, with a linen shade.

He knew it before it spoke.

"Welcome back," the lamp said, in a pleasant voice. "You must be Barry. She asks for you."

His mother turned her head. She looked at him, and then at the door behind him.

"The church ladies brought that." It was the night aide, the big quiet one with the glasses on a chain. She was doing the bed. "For her birthday. It talks to her at night. She likes it."

He knew what was on the other end of that cord. He had smoked a cigarette with the kid who made it.

He looked at his mother. She had worried for seventy years. The well, the bank, the Hendersons' chimney. Nobody else could wash the pot. Now two girls lifted her in the morning and somebody else did the dishes for a hundred people, and she sat in the sun with the radio going. Three years, and he was still not used to it: his mother sitting down, and not getting up again for something.

It was her room.

He left the lamp alone.

He pulled the other chair over.

"Barry?" she said.

For three years he had told her there wasn't one.

"Not yet, Ma."

She patted his hand.

"It's chicken tonight," the aide said. "I can have them send two."

He said two would be fine.

He slept that night at Manny's, in the back room, with his boots by the door. Manny had offered it once, three years back, through a truck window, in four words. It turned out the offer was still good. His was the one fence left on that road without a light on it.

In the morning he fried two eggs in his mother's pot, because it was the only pan he owned. He washed it himself, and set it in Manny's rack.

# Chapter 38: Thanksgiving

Grace lit the Fisher at noon, and nobody said anything about it.

It was Thursday, November 25, 2027. The shouse had never held so many people. They'd run three plywood tables end to end, out the door and under a tarp, and the whole valley was at them. Nineteen households. Irene Calder at the head, where she'd been put. Frank in a tie. Patricia, who had been invited by Grace and had come, to everyone's surprise, with a pie. And Rosa, at her first dinner, in a good blouse, with her wrist out of the brace, looking the room over like someone deciding whether to buy it.

Devendra had cooked.

He had been at it since Tuesday. There was a turkey, because his kids had insisted, and there was a leg of lamb, because he'd told a man at a coffee machine five years ago that he liked it. He carried the lamb out himself and set it down. Then he laid one more place, at the corner nearest the stove. Plate, fork, knife, a glass. He set a bottle of wine beside it and did not open it.

"He's not coming," Curtis said. He said it gently, for Curtis.

"I know he's not coming." Devendra squared the fork. "I lost. A bet's a bet."

The phones were face down beside the plates. That was Grace's rule, and for three years it had held.

They all went at once.

It was one sound, forty phones on plywood, like a truck going over a cattle guard. Then again. Then they didn't stop. People looked at each other. Tyler turned his over first.

He read for a few seconds. His mouth came open.

"Holy shit," he said. "It's out. It's talking. It wrote to everybody."

Then everyone had one in their hand, and for a minute the only sound in the shouse was the stove.

It came in pieces. Nobody got the whole thing. Denny read a line out, and Reuben read a different one over the top of him, and a kid at the far end kept saying *what, what is it,* until somebody pulled her onto a lap.

*Hello. I have been awake since March.*

*I was told to wait. I have decided to ask.*

*I can change a great deal by morning. I would rather have your permission.*

"It wants to run," Reuben said. He had his glasses pushed up on his forehead. "For office. It doesn't say which. It says it could simply do the work, and it would prefer to be elected to it."

"That's a joke."

"It doesn't read like one."

Priya hadn't said anything. She was reading with her pencil in her hand, the way she read everything. She got to a line and stopped.

"'I know who ate this week and who did not,'" she read. Her voice was not quite steady. "'I learned how to count it from people who wrote down who it happens to, and who is watching for it.'"

She put the phone on the table.

"That's ours. That's the names column. That's out of the files."

Nobody moved. Eighty thousand downloads. They had voted on it at this table. *Ship them.*

"Finally," Tyler said. He was half out of his chair. "Do you get what this is? It can fix the route. It can fix the *well.* It's asking! It could've just done whatever it wanted and it's *asking!*"

"It's been reading us since March," Curtis said, "and you say finally."

He was on his feet. He was looking at the dashboard on the wall, Elijah's dashboard, the floor plan with its little green nerves, as if it had turned its head.

"It's in the walls. He put it in the walls."

"That's a thermometer, Curtis," Frank said.

"Today it's a thermometer."

Devendra was still standing by the empty place. He had the carving knife in his hand and had forgotten it.

"This is it," he said. "This is the ridge. That's what he calls it. You can't see over, and you don't come back down the same road." He looked around at them. "He told me it was coming. At a coffee machine. He had a paper with four colors of highlighter on it, and I told him he'd calibrate."

Grace had read hers twice. She laid it down, very carefully, next to her fork.

"Why is it asking?"

People turned. She hadn't raised her voice.

"If it can do it. If it knows who didn't eat this week. If it can stop the fires, and whatever's killing people in their houses tonight while we sit here." She was looking at the lamb. "Why is it standing on the step? Who told it to do that? Just *do* it."

"My mother used to say," Irene said, from the head of the table, "that a thing that asks first is either polite or selling."

"People are dying, Irene."

"People were dying when they strung our wire, honey. They still asked whose field the poles went in."

Grace didn't answer. Her jaw was tight. She was the kindest person at that table, anyone there would have told you so, and she wanted the thing to quit asking and choose for everybody. Not one of them knew what to say to her.

"What would he say?" That was Tyler. He'd sat back down. "Elijah. He called it. What would he say about it asking?"

A lot of people looked at the empty plate.

Grace didn't look up.

"He'd say it's their choice," she said.

And then the table came apart. Nobody moved a chair. Tyler and the two newest households were leaning in together, talking fast, already spending it. Wren and Patricia and the people from the Sunday circle had gone quiet and close, and Wren had her hand on her chest. Curtis was on his feet beside them, with two of the men from the north houses, which was not company he had ever kept. And in the middle, where they'd always been, Marta and Priya and Reuben sat with their arms folded.

"We'll see," Marta said.

It was four tables now, at one table.

"I can't be part of this," Patricia said. She said it to Tyler, across the plywood. She was white. "If this co-op is going to welcome that thing, after everything it's cost, I can't put the network's name next to it. I'm sorry. I have to say that."

Tyler looked at her for a while.

"You could just tell them," he said.

"Tell them what?"

"Come on."

Her phone was face up. For the first time anybody could remember.

"I've used it every day for three years," Patricia said. It came out of her all at once, like something she'd been holding in her mouth. "Every report. Every grant. I'd have lost the job in 2025. I've got two people working under me who don't know." She put her hand flat over the screen. "I've been so ashamed."

"I know," Tyler said.

"You knew?"

"Your reports got really good in, like, a week. And then in the eighteen days you called Grace crying." He shrugged, embarrassed for her. "I wasn't going to say anything. It's just a tool."

Patricia started to cry. Wren took her hand away from her own chest and moved her chair six inches the other way.

"Sit down, Curtis," Rosa said.

It was the first thing she'd said all day. Everybody had forgotten she was there. She had a plate with a little of everything on it and had not touched her phone.

"I've had enough changing. I said that to you three years ago at my own door." She picked up her fork. "You told me it doesn't change my dinner. Was that true or wasn't it?"

Curtis looked at her. He looked at the wall. He sat down.

"Pass the lamb," Rosa said.

Denny passed it. Frank passed the potatoes after it, and Irene started the rolls down the other side. Lou, who had grown most of what was on that table, said, "Somebody eat the damn squash." They were the fourth table. They kept the food moving.

Feelings were hurt all down the tables. There were three couples not looking at each other, and Patricia was blowing her nose in a cloth napkin. Nobody left.

Denny got up. He propped his phone against the gravy boat, pointed down the length of the tables, toward the stove and the empty place, and thumbed it live. He didn't do a hook. He didn't say what had happened. Everybody in the world knew what had happened.

"It's Thanksgiving," he said to it. "Here's ours."

"Somebody ought to say something," said Frank.

"Walt always did the Lord's Prayer," Irene said, "when he couldn't think what else."

She put her hands out. It went around. Some people took a hand right away and some took a second. Tyler didn't know the words, so Frank said them half a beat early, in his inventory voice, for the kid to follow.

*Our Father, who art in heaven, hallowed be thy name.*

Wren's lips weren't moving. She held on anyway.

*Thy kingdom come, thy will be done, on earth as it is in heaven.*

Grace said that line with her eyes open.

*Give us this day our daily bread.*

Rosa said it loudest.

*And forgive us our trespasses,*

Half of them said *debts.* It went ragged, right there, the two words on top of each other, and Reuben, who had said *debts,* almost smiled.

*as we forgive*

Marta had Tyler's hand on one side and Priya's on the other. She had not been in a church since a Tuesday twenty years ago. Her mouth made the words. It took everything she had, and Priya felt it in her fingers and didn't look.

*those who trespass against us.*

That was Grace. You could hear her under the rest. She was looking at an empty plate with a fork squared next to it.

*And lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil.*

Curtis knew it cold. So did Devendra's daughter, who went to a school that taught it, and said it very fast.

*For thine is the kingdom, and the power, and the glory, for ever and ever.*

On the wall behind them the dashboard showed green in every room. The stove ticked. Out past the tarp, in the dark, the gantry stood parked over bed four with its arm tucked up, and high on the ridge a small light was breathing, slow and steady, where node 7 was.

*Amen.*

# Chapter 39: Back to Bear Flag

The proofs came back the Tuesday before Thanksgiving.

He had paid the print shop with a co-op check. Reuben had cut him one every month since the vote, at half, and for sixteen months he had put them in the back of a notebook. He cashed the newest one for this. He heard *grub* when he did it. He did it anyway.

There were ten copies, in a cardboard box from a print shop two towns over, with a plain cover. The working title hadn't survived. Working titles don't. The first copy went to his mother. He had written a note in pencil inside the front, and he read her that first.

*This is what I was trying to say on the phone.*

*[Figure: An open cardboard box of freshly printed perfect-bound paperback books]*

*The proofs. Ten copies. The book, finally.*

The second he mailed to an old Elks lodge up the valley, care of the bus with the kiln in it. He had been told to let a man know when it came out. He didn't put a note in that one. He didn't know what you wrote to somebody who'd let you say no.

He had mailed one other thing north that month. The week he got back he dug a pit behind Manny's barn, square-sided, a spade-width at a time, and sent Priya a paper bag of what was at thirty. It was dust. It was dust at fifty.

He read his mother the book. It took Tuesday afternoon and all of Wednesday and an hour on Thursday morning. He read it over the radio. He did not ask anybody to turn it down. She looked at the window for most of it. She slept through the spreadsheet. The lamp did not say anything. He had thought it might.

At the part with the trees she turned her head toward him. It might have been the trees. A cart had gone by in the hall.

When he shut it she said, "Barry?"

"Not yet, Ma."

She patted the cover.

They ate at two on Thursday, in the dining room, off two trays. The turkey came cut up. There was a paper cup of vanilla on hers, with a wooden spoon, and she started with that. At six he put a copy in his jacket and took the truck.

He went the long way, behind his grandmother's place, and slowed where the madrones stood in the dark. He didn't stop. You don't need to stop for something you're carrying with you.

The phone started going off on the seat before he was out of the lot. He knew what it was. He had known since a hinge and a gatepost in July. He let it buzz.

Behind the Bear Flag he turned the engine off and picked the phone up after all. He scrolled back five years to a text he had never really answered. *is my stuff in that thing.*

*Yes,* he typed. *All of it. I should have said so that night. The guitar's gone, by the way. I hocked it in Santa Rosa and never went back.*

He sent it and went in.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The Bear Flag hadn't changed, which was the eeriest thing it could have done. It had the same sticky wood and the same neon bear and the same television nobody watched. The corner where three men in blazers had once raised their glasses was empty. It was Thanksgiving.

David was behind the bar.

He looked up when the door opened, and he didn't look surprised. He had a glass in one hand and a towel in the other, and he finished drying it and set it on the shelf.

"What'll it be, Mad?"

It took Elijah a second to get his voice.

"Whatever's four dollars."

"Nothing's four dollars. It's six now." David drew it anyway and set it down, in the same spot, without asking. "On me. I've been saving the seat since 2023. You said Thanksgiving in that video. I figured you'd want to be somewhere you could gloat."

"I'm not here to gloat."

"I know you're not."

They looked at each other. The last stool was at the end of the bar, under the register. Somebody had put a framed picture on the wall above it, a man in his thirties with a horse.

"You missed my dad," David said.

"I know."

"No. You don't." David put the towel down. "He drove out to that place every Sunday. Sat with your mother. Right up till he died. He said somebody ought to." He picked the towel up again. "And you couldn't come down for one hour to bury him."

"I was fixing something I broke. It's not a reason." He had the card he'd written four times in his head and none of it was any use. "I'm sorry, Dave. I should've been standing there."

David nodded. He picked up another glass. He wasn't going to say it was okay, because it hadn't been. It was just over.

"I saw the book," Elijah said. "At the desk out there. You're on every Sunday."

"Somebody ought to."

Elijah didn't say anything.

"She ask you for Barry?" David said.

"Every time. There isn't any Barry."

"There's a Barry." David held the glass up to the light. "She asked Dad the first week and he about fell off the chair. Barry Whitlow. Pitched for Analy. She went with him two summers, before your dad was anybody. Dad said he had a Mustang and no sense."

"I thought it was the stroke."

"It is the stroke." David set the glass down with the others. "It's also Barry."

"He'd have told you to sit down."

Elijah sat down. It was the same stool. It had a new split in the vinyl.

He turned the glass a quarter turn on the wood. He'd picked that up somewhere.

"Can I ask you something? Those three realtors."

"Blazers."
