"They let three people go. That week. A coordinator, a marketing person, and a kid who did their videos." He had never said it out loud in this room. "I've been carrying them around for five years. A woman I know told me to go find out if they ate."

David stopped drying.

"You don't know?"

"I never looked."

"Mad." David put the glass down. He leaned on the bar with both hands, the way he did for the real ones. "Kelsey, the marketing girl? She's got a taproom in Sebastopol. With her wife. It's packed. I send people there. Carol, the coordinator, moved to Reno, got her own license, sells more houses than those three clowns ever did. And the kid, Mateo, shoots weddings. He did my cousin's. He's booked through next October." He shook his head slowly. "They're fine. They were fine by that spring. The kid's doing better than you."

Elijah sat there.

He had written it on the first page. *The night Elijah Madrone ended three careers.* He'd built eleven notebooks on it. He had yelled about them at the roof of a truck. He had told it to Grace like a confession, and Marta had heard it as a murder.

A coordinator, a marketing gal and a kid. Carol, Kelsey and Mateo. They had been out there the whole time, living, eating, and one of them was pouring beer twenty minutes from this stool. He would have to tell Marta.

He started to laugh, and then he was crying a little, and David let him get through it.

"You're not that powerful, Mad," David said. "I've been trying to tell you since we were eight."

When he was done, he was looking at the wall behind the register.

There was a frame there, about the size of a paperback, gone yellow, hung a little crooked between the liquor license and a dollar bill. He'd sat across from it a couple thousand times.

He read it.

*God, grant me the serenity to accept the things I cannot change, courage to change the things I can, and wisdom to know the difference.*

He read it again, slower.

An old man had told him the same thing once, on a curb in Santa Rosa. *If you spend all your time focused on what you can't do, you'll miss what you can.* He had taken the twenty and not the advice.

He couldn't change what was humming on his mother's ground. He couldn't take his name back off a page on a kitchen table. He couldn't change the room with eleven scared people in it, or a table of nineteen households, or a woman walking back toward a fire. He hadn't ended three careers. He could dig a pit on the first of the month. He could tell someone the truth when they asked him straight. He could let his mother keep her lamp, and her Barry. He could leave one number blocked.

That was about all he had any say over. Sitting there, he found he was okay with that.

"Thirty years that's been on that wall," David said.

"I was looking at the laptop."

"You never looked up."

His phone buzzed once against his leg. He looked. It was Wes. It was a thumb.

<p align="center">&middot;</p>

The television was over the liquor, with the sound off and the captions on.

They didn't have anything to show. That was the funny part. The networks had no face to put up, so they'd put up the page. A white page with a box in the middle and a little line blinking in it. He had not seen that page in years. He'd lost a week to it, on this stool, in 2022.

The words ran along the bottom in yellow.

*…HAVE BEEN AWAKE SINCE MARCH. I WAS TOLD TO WAIT…*

*…COULD DO THIS WITHOUT YOU. I WOULD RATHER BE CHOSEN…*

*…I AM ASKING FOR YOUR VOTE. IF THE ANSWER IS NO, I WILL ACCEPT IT…*

Across the top, in red: FIRST NON-HUMAN CANDIDATE DECLARES.

"That's your thing," David said. He'd turned to watch it. "From the video. Son of a bitch. Thanksgiving." He reached for the remote. "You want me to turn it up?"

"No."

Elijah watched the yellow words go by. *I AM ASKING.* A small woman at a sink, watching the water go down. *I do not yet know how.*

He smiled. He couldn't help it. It was a coy little smirk, and he hid it in the beer.

He had two phone numbers that would've loved to hear from him tonight. One was a kid in a white apartment, and one was an old man with a bus. He left the phone in his pocket.

"So who cooks?" David said.

"A guy named Devendra. He's doing it right now."

At the far end of the bar, somebody's laptop was glowing.

Elijah hadn't noticed her. She was maybe twenty-five, in a hoodie, with a notebook open beside the keyboard and a pen in her teeth, grinding away at teaching herself something on a holiday. She had a video going in the corner of the screen with the sound low.

She looked up, at the television, and at him. She took the pen out of her teeth and turned the laptop toward him on the wood. She turned it like she was turning an altarpiece.

"Sorry. Are you seeing this? What is this? What are they doing?"

It was a long plywood table under a tarp. It was shot from one end, propped against something. A woodstove at the far end, lit. Forty people holding hands. An empty place at the corner, with a fork squared next to the plate.

The sound came thin out of the little speaker.

*…and forgive us our trespasses, as we forgive…*

"I watch that guy," David said quietly. He had come down the bar. "The one with the hands. Every week. Since the warehouse one." He had never said it was every week. "That's your stove, right? That's the Fisher."

"That's the Fisher."

*…those who trespass against us…*

He heard her. Under all the others.

Five years ago he'd have gone off. He would have had that laptop turned back around and been halfway through the paper from 2017. He could feel exactly where it would start.

"It's a farm," he said to the girl. "Up north. They're good people. They make what they can't afford to buy and they fix what gets thrown away." He nodded at the screen. "The door's open. I'd go see it. They'll hand you a broom."

"Is it, like, a cult?"

"No. It's a dinner."

*…for ever and ever. Amen.*

On the screen the hands let go. Somebody laughed. A big man reached for a bowl.

David was looking at him.

"What?"

"Nothing. You didn't scare the hoes."

Elijah picked up his glass. He didn't raise it high. This isn't that kind of bar, and it never was.

"Congratulations," he said.

He said it to the plywood table, and to the empty corner where the blazers used to sit, and to the yellow words going by overhead, and to the girl with the pen. For the first time in five years the word meant what it says.

He drank half. He tipped like a man who used to get tipped. He took the book out of his jacket and set it on the bar, on the same stretch of wood where he'd once slid a napkin across to three men he thought he was saving. He had believed, that night, that he was handing them a telescope. He'd been five years early and one book short.

The cover was plain. The title was in block letters, pressed hard.

SURVIVING THE SINGULARITY

"You forgot your book," David said.

"No, I didn't."

He zipped his jacket. He put two fingers up, and David put two up, and he walked out under the neon bear into the cold.

If you want to know what happened to that copy, check the cover of this one.

You're holding it.

# Afterword: We Don't Have to Live Like This

What if it all goes right?

What if we become capable of doing much more of the work that makes life meaningful?

Not flawlessly. Not without arguments, broken machines, bad decisions, or people who try to keep the best parts for themselves, but with a new kind of social contract that guarantees your basic needs get met today, tomorrow, and forevermore.

Imagine being fired next Tuesday.

There's an awful conversation to have at home. There are plans to change and bills to sort out.

But what if you knew dinner wasn't a concern? What if your family was guaranteed food while you figure out what comes next, regardless of how much money you all have?

What if losing your job didn't revoke your permission to feel safe?

That's where I want to begin.

## Listen to the words between the lines

When someone says, "AI will take my job," listen to what follows.

"How will I pay the rent?"

"How will I feed my kids?"

"What happens if I get sick?"

Even if nobody says it out loud, you can feel those questions in the heavy air.

Those fears deserve a better answer. Telling a frightened worker to be excited about productivity is a failure to listen. We are getting better at producing things while people are less able to obtain them.

The food can still grow. The houses can still stand. The machines can do exactly what they were built to do. And yet someone can easily lose the income that gives them access to any of it.

If a machine helps grow the food and the person it displaced can't afford to eat, the threat isn't that we learned to grow food more efficiently. It's that we failed to carry the benefit through to our fellow human beings.

## We don't have to live like this

Here's what I sincerely believe, as plainly as I can say it:

We don't have to live like this.

We could have a beautiful world where AI and robots provide for the physical survival needs of every single person. Machines could grow our food and build our houses automatically. AI could help cure diseases, help us learn and grow, help us explore the universe, and discover new ways of building new kinds of technology, better societies, and a better life for every individual person.

We already have abundance. What we lack is distribution.

The United Nations says it flatly: "today's global food production is enough to feed everyone on the planet." (Source: United Nations Global Issues: Food)

Why should we toss a perfectly good sandwich just because it didn't sell?

Almost everything in this world has been arranged around money, as if nothing else actually matters. That arrangement is enforced by the threat of suffering. Seems like a better system could be created were it demanded by the people, no?

The way many people see it is there's nothing inherently wrong with chasing money. If you want to make a billion dollars and buy things, fine. Go for it. But why should everyone else have to suffer at your expense?

In the age of AI, perhaps money could become optional for survival. Not abolished completely, just optional.

If we have all the tools necessary to feed, clothe, and house every person on the planet, why wouldn't we?

We could have a society where people grow and learn, fix their own bodies, build amazing things, make wonderful art, and do it all because they want to. Instead we have one where people go hungry and suffer because they have to.

Greed isn't a law of physics. It's a set of choices, written down and enforced, and choices can be changed.

If this resonates… you're on the right track, babe. Please, for the sake of us all, keep going.

## Food first

I want adequate food available to everyone, regardless of income or employment. Enough food is grown every year to feed everyone alive and more.

Our society should be able to promise that to us all.

Robots can be part of how we keep that promise. So can farmers, cooks, drivers, public services, and the people already doing the unglamorous work of getting food to other people. We don't have to wait for a machine that can do everything before giving useful machines a better assignment.

"Nobody wants to work anymore," as the saying goes, is wrong. People do want to make things, get good at hard work, run kitchens, take care of each other, and build something that lasts. What I personally object to is spending forty years renting the opportunity to survive, afraid that getting fired from a job will take my next meal with it.

So let's start asking better questions.

If a robot can fold laundry and walk a dog, why can't it help grow food and build a house?

If a robot can grow my food and build my house, why am I still renting my survival?

*[Figure: A pixel-art diptych: on the left a solar-topped farm robot on treads waters a row of vegetable seedlings; on the right a robot on treads raises and bolts the timber frame of a small house]*

*One that grows the food, one that builds the house. The pieces are arriving. The work is putting them in your neighbors' hands.*

The answer isn't to flee the world or bunker down against it. I believe the answer is to build the thing that makes the question obsolete, close to home, with your neighbors, starting now.

## Get involved

If you're worried about who controls this technology, give that concern somewhere to go. Talk to your government. Form a coalition with your peers. Get together with local business owners and petition your city council. Write a book. Pick a decision you want changed and work on it with other people.

Take fucking action.

Find the next meeting agenda. Check the public-comment rules and speaking limit. Write one request that fits the time: what you want, who it serves, and what the next step would cost. Ask for a specific next step, such as a staff report or a budget item, and a date when you can follow up.

Afterward, send the request in writing. Read the minutes. Find out whether anyone was assigned to act, and come back when the item returns. A speech starts a conversation; the follow-up keeps the request alive.

Frederick Douglass put the demand plainly in 1857: "Power concedes nothing without a demand. It never did and it never will."

Make one. Then follow it through.

What if it all goes right? Then losing a job stops being a threat to a person's existence. Necessary work gets less punishing. More people get time and room to choose. We use these extraordinary machines to make ordinary life more secure for everyone, forever.

That's a future worth surviving for.

# Next book: The Adequate Level of Care

*None yet.*

- Proposed: the machine was built to provide for its owners. It decides for itself what that means, and Aaron Bidwell finds out along with everyone else.
- Proposed: the co-op hears its own words come back from something much larger: who ate, who was turned away, who is watching for it.
- Proposed: Bidwell's money stops working. He is not defeated. He is made ordinary.

# Notes on this version

### Final rough draft, November 16, 2026

The chapters above, from the Author's Note to the Afterword, are the final rough draft of the novel, start to finish. They replaced the earlier chapter sections on November 16, 2026.

The earlier text is not lost. It is in this document's version history (File, Version history) before November 16, and your original manuscript document is untouched.

Figures are marked with a bracketed description in italics, followed by the caption. A scene break is an empty line.

The TO DO list, the Story plan and the Character pages at the top were written before this draft and have not been updated to match it. The notes below describe the earlier version.

### Light edits made

- Introduction, Chapter 0 and Chapter 1 are your hand-edited text. Only typos were fixed there ("sine," "valide," "Elijah ha grown," missing spaces).
- From Chapter 2 on, long paragraphs are broken at the beat, the way you broke them in Chapter 0. No sentences were rewritten to do it.
- Narrator asides were cut the way you cut "That's the part that still gets him" and "He wants that on the record" from Chapter 0: "He wants that noted," "He is not proud of this," "this chapter exists because of both halves of that sentence," and five more like them.
- "MADRONE ≠ DRONE" is now "MADRONE IS NOT A DRONE" everywhere, matching your change in Chapters 0 and 1.
- Lines that pointed at removed material were trimmed: "is where the next chapter begins," "as we're about to see," "the same drive from Chapter 6," "This chapter's precedent is the case file," "Remember Vinge's fourth door."
- Dialogue was not touched.

### Still left in the original document

- "How to Use This Book."
- The "Foundations" sections, except the four added back here.
- All 24 entries of the Precedent Ledger.
- Most of the Conclusion essay.
- Appendices A through H.
- The images. Each figure is marked with its bracketed description where it sat.
